Equivariant Linearization and Rotation Domains on K3 Surfaces MSC(2020): 14J28, 14J50, 11K16. Keywords: K3 surface; rotation domain; equivariant linearization; Kleinian singularity: Salem number; multiplier; hypergeometric group; fixed point formula; periodic cycle. 15 tables; 3 figures.
Katsunori Iwasaki

Abstract
We construct a lot of K3 surface automorphisms of positive entropy having rotation domains of ranks 1 1 and 2 2 . To carry out this construction, we first lay theoretical foundations concerning equivariant linearization of nonlinear maps under resolutions of quotient singularities, linear models near exceptional components, Salem numbers and multipliers at periodic points, two kinds of fixed point formulas and related indices at exceptional components. Then these basic tools are combined with the method of hypergeometric groups to enable us to detect various types of rotation domains on K3 surfaces.



1
Introduction

Given an automorphism
f:X\to X
of infinite order on a compact complex surface
X
,
its
Fatou set

\mathcal{F}(f)
is the set of all points
p\in X
such that
the family
\{f^{n}\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
is equicontinuous in a neighborhood of
p
,
where
f^{n}=f\circ\cdots\circ f
(
n
times) stands for the
n
-th iterate of
f
.
It is the largest open subset of
X
on which the dynamics of
f
exhibits regular behaviors.
Each connected component of
\mathcal{F}(f)
is referred to as a
Fatou component
.

Following Bedford and Kim
[
3
, §1]
, we rewiew the concept of a rotation domain;
see also Fornæss and Sibony
[
4
]
and Ueda
[
18
]
.
An
f
-invariant Fatou component
U\subset\mathcal{F}(f)
is called a
rotation domain of rank

r
,
if iterations of
f
induce an
r
-dimensional real torus action on
U
.
To be more precise, let
\mathcal{G}(U)
be the set of all normal limits

g=\displaystyle\lim_{n_{j}\to\infty}(f|_{U})^{n_{j}}:U\to\overline{U}

of subsequences of
\{(f|_{U})^{n}\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
.
By
[
3
, Theorem 1.2]
, if the condition

\mbox{for any $g\in\mathcal{G}(U)$ its image $g(U)$ contans an open set in $\overline{U}$},

(1)

is satisfied, then
\mathcal{G}(U)
is a compact, infinite abelian, Lie group of automorphisms
on
U
in the compact-open topology, so the connected component
\mathcal{G}_{0}(U)
of
the identity in
\mathcal{G}(U)
is a real torus of positive dimension.
We say that
U
is a
rotation domain of rank

r
, if
\mathcal{G}_{0}(U)
is a real torus of dimension
r
.
According to
[
3
, Theorems 1.3 and 1.5]
we have
1\leq r\leq 4
and if
X
is
Kähler and
f
has positive topological entropy, then
r
is either
1
or
2
.
Replacing
f
by some iterate
f^{m}
with
m\geq 2
,
we can also speak of a
periodic
rotation domain for the map
f
.
A fixed (or periodic) point lying in a (periodic) rotation domain is called
a
center
of that rotation domain.

Bedford and Kim
[
2
,
3
]
constructed rational surface automorphisms of
positive entropy having rotation domains of ranks
1
and
2
, and made a detailed
study of the constructed mappings.
In their construction they started with explicit birational self-maps of the
projective plane
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and performed iterated blowups to get desired automorphisms.
We also refer to McMullen
[
10
]
, Bedford and Kim
[
2
]
for rotation domains
on rational surfaces.
Now let us turn our attention to rotation domains on K3 surfaces.
McMullen
[
9
]
constructed K3 surface automorphisms of positive entropy with
a rotation domain of rank
2
, which he called a
Siegel disk
.
His examples had Picard number
0
.
Oguiso
[
11
]
found a similar example of Picard number
8
.
Iwasaki and Takada
[
6
]
obtained more automorphisms with Siegel disks that
covered all possible Picard numbers, that is, even integers between
0
and
18
.
Their construction was based on
the method of hypergeometric groups
introduced
in
[
5
]
.

This article develops a detailed study of rotation domains on K3 surfaces, with emphasis
on rotation domains of rank
1
or on the coexistence of rank
1
domains with rank
2
ones.
A K3 surface is a simply connected, compact complex surface
X
admitting a nowhere vanishing
holomorphic
2
-form
\eta_{X}
.
It is Kähler by Siu
[
13
]
.
Let
f:X\to X
be an automorphism of
X
.
There exists a complex number
\delta(f)
of modulus
1
such that

f^{*}\eta_{X}=\delta(f)\,\eta_{X}.

(2)

In this article
\delta(f)
is referred to as the
twist number
of
f
.
It was called the
determinant
of
f
by McMullen
[
9
]
and
the
special eigenvalue
of
f
by Iwasaki and Takada
[
5
,
6
]
respectively.
Equation (
2
) implies that
f
preserves the smooth volume form

\eta_{X}\wedge\overline{\eta}_{X}
, so that condition (
1
) is satisfied
by
[
3
, Proposition 1.1]
.

A
Salem number
is a real algebraic integer
\lambda>1
which is conjugate to

\lambda^{-1}
and whose remaining conjugates have modulus
1
.
Its degree is an even integer
d\geq 4
.
We refer to Smyth
[
16
]
for a good survey on Salem numbers.
The twist number
\delta=\delta(f)
in (
2
) is either a root of unity or a
Galois conjugate to a Salem number
\lambda
of degree
d\leq 22
.
We always assume that the automorphism
f:X\to X
satisfies the following two conditions:

(A1)

the twist number
\delta=\delta(f)
is conjugate to a Salem number
\lambda=\lambda(f)
,

(A2)

the Picard group
\operatorname{Pic}(X)
is non-degenerate with respect to the intersection form on
H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
.

From (A1) the K3 surface
X
is non-projective and the automorphism
f
has topological
entropy

h(f)=\log\lambda(f)>0,

where
\lambda(f)
is referred to as the
dynamical degree
of
f
.
When
X
is non-projective,
\operatorname{Pic}(X)
may degenerate, so condition (A2) is
imposed to rule out this possibility so that
\operatorname{Pic}(X)
is negative definite.
Under (A1) and (A2), all irreducible curves in
X
are
(-2)
-curves
and the number of them is at most the Picard number
\rho(X)
, the rank of
\operatorname{Pic}(X)
.
The union
\mathcal{E}(X)
of all
(-2)
-curves in
X
is called the
exceptional set
of
X
.
Each connected component
E\subset\mathcal{E}(X)
is called an
exceptional component
.
The dual graph of
E
is a Dynkin diagram
D
of type
\mathrm{A}
,
\mathrm{D}
or
\mathrm{E}
.
The map
f
permutes the exceptional components and may leave some of them invariant.
If
E
is
f
-invariant, then
f
permutes the
(-2)
-curves in
E
and induces an automorphism
of the diagram
D
,

\sigma(f,E)\in\operatorname{Aut}(D).

(3)

The order of
\sigma(f,E)
is
1
,
2
, or
3
.
In the respective cases we say that the pair
(f,E)
, or rather the set
E
when
the map
f
is understood, has
trivial
,
reflectional
, or
tricyclic symmetry
,
where the tricyclic case occurs only when
D
is of type
\mathrm{D}_{4}
.
Since the number of exceptional components is finite, all exceptional components
are left invariant by some iterate
f^{m}
.
Now it is natural to pose the following problem.

Problem 1.1

Given an
f
-invariant exceptional component
E\subset\mathcal{E}(X)
, we ask whether

(R1)

E
is completely contained in a rotation domain of rank
1
,

(R2)

E
is completely contained in a rotation domain of rank
2
,

(Hy)

E
is hyperbolic to the effect that a stable curve flows into
E
and an unstable curve emanates from
E
.

This question can be asked for
f^{m}
in place of
f
and hence makes sense
for all exceptional components in
X
.

One of the main results in this article is the following solution to this problem.

Theorem 1.2

Let
E
be an
f
-invariant exceptional component.
If
E
is of Dynkin type
\mathrm{D}
or
\mathrm{E}
, then
(\mathrm{R}1)
is always the case for
(f,E)
.
The same is true when
E
is of Dynkin type
\mathrm{A}
with reflectional symmetry.
On the other hand, when
E
is of Dynkin type
\mathrm{A}
with trivial symmetry,
all of the three cases
(\mathrm{R}1),(\mathrm{R}2),(\mathrm{Hy})
can occur.

Formally, after a precise formulation, Theorem
1.2
is established as Theorems
9.3
and
9.7
.

Remark 1.3

Let
(f,E)
be a pair of type
\mathrm{A}
with trivial symmetry.
If
f
is the square of another automorphism
g
such that
(g,E)
is of type
\mathrm{A}
with reflectional symmetry,
then Theorem
1.2
implies that
(f,E)
falls in case
(\mathrm{R}1)
.
If
(f,E)
is not of this form but still lies in case
(\mathrm{R}1)
,
then we say that it is
genuinely
of case
(\mathrm{R}1)
.

Finding a lot of K3 surface automorphisms illustrating Theorem
1.2

is also a main goal of this article.

Result 1.4

We give examples of
K3
surface automorphisms in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec:mhgg}
and
detect rotation domains on them in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec:rd}
.

(\mathrm{i})

Table
8.1
gives examples having an
exceptional component of type
\mathrm{A}
with trivial symmetry.
Proposition
8.3
establishes basic properties of these examples,
and Theorem
9.7
shows that they cover all of the three cases

(\mathrm{R}2),(\mathrm{R}1),(\mathrm{Hy})
.
We emphasize that Table
8.1
contains an example which is genuinely of case
(\mathrm{R}1)
.

(\mathrm{ii})

Tables
8.2
and
8.3
give examples having
exceptional components of type
\mathrm{A}
with reflectional symmetry.
Proposition
8.4
establishes basic properties of these examples.
They fall in case
(\mathrm{R}1)
by Theorem
1.2
.

(\mathrm{iii})

Tables
8.4
and
8.5
give examples having
exceptional components of type
\mathrm{D}
or
\mathrm{E}
.
Propositions
8.5
and
8.6
establish basic properties of these examples.
They fall in case
(\mathrm{R}1)
by Theorem
1.2
.

Table
8.1
contains examples having a rotation domain of rank
1
and one of
rank
2
at the same time, the former of which contains an exceptional component of
type
\mathrm{A}
and the latter contains one of type
\mathrm{E};
see Theorem
9.7
.

While Theorem
1.2
and Result
1.4
are concerned with
phenomena around an exceptional component, it is more classical to
find a fixed point
p

off
the exceptional set
\mathcal{E}(X)
and ask whether
p
is a
center of some rotation domain or a hyperboluc fixed point.
This problem is more interesting when we focus not only on fixed points
but also on cycles of periodic points, i.e.
periodic cycles
, with various primitive periods.
We remark that all periodic points off
\mathcal{E}(X)
are isolated.
We now give only a rough and partial sketch of the results in this direction.

Result 1.5

Among other things we obtain the following kinds of results in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec:pc}
.

(\mathrm{i})

An automorphism
f
in Theorem
10.6
has periodic cycles
of primitive periods
1,7,12,14
, which are centers of rotation domains of rank
2
;
a periodic cycle of primitive period
3
, each point of which is a center of a rotation domains of rank
1
;
and hyperbolic periodic cycles of primitive periods
2,5,9,10,11
.
While those cycles are off
\mathcal{E}(X)
, the exceptional set
\mathcal{E}(X)
itself is an
f
-invariant
exceptional component of type
\mathrm{A}_{2}
with reflectional symmetry, which therefore is
contained in a rotation domain of rank
1
by Theorem
1.2
.

(\mathrm{ii})

Example
10.7
presents two automorphisms which
have no exceptional set, but still admit a rotation domain of rank
1
.
These maps are found by Y. Takada in a conversation with the author.

All examples in Results
1.4
and
1.5
arise from the
method of
hypergeometric groups
developed in
[
5
,
6
]
.
A survey of this method is given in §
8
.
We remark that any K3 surface automorphism created by this method
satisfies conditions
(\mathrm{A}1)
and
(\mathrm{A}2)
.
This method is suitable for discussing rotation domains on K3 surfaces.

In order to detect a rotation domain for a given map
f:X\to X
, it is
standard to linearize
f
around a fixed point
p\in X
into its tangent map

(df)_{p}:T_{p}X\to T_{p}X
and examine if
p
is a center of some rotation
domain by looking at the linear map
(df)_{p}
.
This is typically the Siegel-type linearization of a local biholomorphism

F:(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)\to(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)

(4)

into its Jacobian matrix
J:=F^{\prime}(0)\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, where
(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)
stands
for an open neighborhood of the origin
0
in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, not the whole space
\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
In (
4
) we usually assume that the multipliers of
F
at the origin, i.e. the
eigenvalues of
J
, have modulus
1
and satisfy a certain Diophantine
condition; see §
2
for a brief review.

In §
2
we generalize this idea to that of
equivariant linearization
,
which enables us to deal with a K3 surface automorphism
f:X\to X
in
a neighborhood of an
f
-invariant exceptional component
E
.
A small neighborhood
V
of
E
is a minimal resolution of a complex surface

S
with a Kleinian singularity
p\in S
, and
(S,p)
is isomorphic to
the quotient space of
(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)
by a finite subgroup
G
of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
.
Thus the automorphism
f
restricted to
V
lifts to a local biholomorphism
F

as in (
4
), which is
G
-
equivariant
.
In this situation we have the following result.

Theorem 1.6

Let
G
be a nontrivial finite subgroup of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
and suppose that
the map
F
in (
4
) is
G
-equivariant.
Then the Jacobian matrix
J:=F^{\prime}(0)\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
normalizes
G
,
that is,
JgJ^{-1}\in G
for every
g\in G
, and
J
is diagonalizable
unless
G
is a group of order
2
.
Suppose that
J
is diagonalizable in this exceptional case and hence in any case.
Under a Diophantine condition on its multipliers,
F
is
G
-equivariantly
linearizable to
J
.

The terminology used here is explained in §
2
and
Theorem
1.6
is established formally as Theorem
2.4
.
Any normalizor
J\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
of
G
acting linearly on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
leads back to
an automorphism

f_{J}:(X,E)\to(X,E)

(5)

in a vicinity of
E
, which we call
the
linear model
associated with
J
.
In §
4
we give a detailed description of how
f_{J}
acts on the exceptional component
E
.
To this end, in §
3
, we need to recall explicitly how a minimal resolution of a Kleinian
singularity takes place, although it is rather technical.
Now we start with an automorphism
f:(X,E)\to(X,E)
, proceed to a
G
-equivariant map
F
as
in (
4
), apply Theorem
1.6
to
G
-equivariantly linearize
F
into its Jacobian matrix
J
,
and come back to
(X,E)
with a linear model
f_{J}
as in (
5
).
As Proposition
5.1
shows, there is a subtle but not negligible difference
between
f|_{E}
and
f_{J}|_{E}
, although they are almost the same.
In any case, the linear model
f_{J}
can be used to detect rotation domains for
the original map
f
; see Lemma
9.2
.

For the linearization of the map
F
in (
4
), in both classical and
G
-equivariant versions,
it is necessary to judge whether the multiplier pair
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
of
F
at
0
is multiplicatively
independent (MI), or multiplicatively dependent (MD) but non-resonant (NR), or resonant; see §
6

for definitions.
Writing
\delta:=\det J=\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}
, we express the multipliers
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}

as
\delta^{1/2}\alpha^{\pm 1}
with
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.
At this stage there is a sharp contrast between

(a)

the equivariant linearization around an exceptional component of type
\mathrm{D}
or
\mathrm{E}
,
as well as around an exceptional component of type
\mathrm{A}
with reflectional symmetry,

(b)

the equivariant linearization around an exceptional component of type
\mathrm{A}
with trivial symmetry,
as well as the ordinary linearization around an isolated fixed or periodic point off the exceptional set.

In case (a) the number
\alpha
is confined into roots of unity which can be determined explicitly,
while in case (b) the number
\alpha
remains unknown and yet to be determined from a
global geometry

of the automorphism
f:X\to X
.
To cope with case (b), we impose an algebraic constraint, or ansatz, on the number
\alpha
; see Hypothesis
6.1
.
The ansatz there is to assume that there exists
a rational function
\hat{P}(w)\in\mathbb{Q}(w)
such that

\alpha+\alpha^{-1}=\hat{P}(\hat{\tau}),\qquad\hat{\tau}:=\delta^{1/2}+\delta^{-1/2},

where
\delta
is supposed to be a non-real conjugate to a Salem number,
in anticipation of applications to K3 surface automorphisms.
In this setting, using some properties of Salem numbers, we establish in Theorem
6.3

an exact criterion for the judgement of the trichotomy mentiond at the beginning of this paragraph.

Now the problem is to determine the function
\hat{P}(w)
over an
exceptional component
E
of type
\mathrm{A}
with trivial symmetry, or at a fixed or periodic
point
p
off
\mathcal{E}(X)
.
In the latter situation, it is also necessary to find such a point
p
beforehand.
To this end we use two kinds of Lefschetz-type fixed point formulas (FPFs); one is
a
topological
FPF (
64
), which comes from S. Saito’s FPF
[
15
]
,
and the other is a
holomorphic
FPF (
67
), which comes from Toledo and Tong’s FPF
[
17
]
.
While these formulas already appear in our papers
[
5
,
6
]
, this article develops an
extensive study of the related indices around exceptional components in §
7
.
As Theorem
7.3
and its use in §
9
suggest, the treatment of exceptional components of
type
\mathrm{A}
with trivial symmetry is quite subtle.
A rich variety of functions
\hat{P}(w)
make the phenomena in case (b) more colorful
than those in case (a).

All the theoretical foundations in §§
2
–
7
are
combined with the method of hypergeomteric groups (as reviewed in §
8
) to
enable us to detect rotation domains and their periodic versions
in §§
9
–
10
.
We remark that, logically, the contents of §§
2
–
7

should not be limitted to K3 surface automorphisms—although applications to them
always stay in our mind—and the results in those sections should be applicable in wider situations.
On the other hand, the contents proper to K3 surface automorphisms are presented
in §§
8
–
10
.
Appendix
A
gives a detailed study of particular examples,
which is interesting in itself, but digresses somewhat from the main stream of the article.
Appendix
B
provides some data used in §
10
, which is too messy
to be included in the main text.
This rather long sketch of the article also speaks about its organization,
so there is no need to repeat it.
Throughout the paper
\mathbb{N}:=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
stands for the set of all
positive
integers;

\mathrm{i}:=\sqrt{-1}
is the imaginary unit.

2
Equivariant Linearization

Let
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
be nonzero complex numbers, which are referred
to as
multipliers
.
The multiplier pair
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
is said to be
Diophantine

if there exist positive constants
K
and
c
such that

|\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{1}^{n_{1}}\alpha_{2}^{n_{2}}|\geq K(n_{1}+n_{2})^{-c}\qquad\mbox{for any}\quad(n_{1},n_{2})\in N,\quad j=1,2,

where
N:=\{(n_{1},n_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\mid n_{1}\geq 0,\,n_{2}\geq 0,\,n_{1}+n_{2}\geq 2\}
.
A Siegel-type linearization theorem for nonlinear mappings, e.g. a theorem of
Pöschel
[
12
]
restricted to
2
-dimensions, states the following result.

Theorem 2.1

Let
F:(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)\to(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)
be a local biholomorphism with Jacobian matrix

J=F^{\prime}(0)
at the origin, and let
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
be the eigenvalues of
J
.
If
J
is diagonalizable and the pair
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
is
Diophantine, then there exists a unique local biholomorphism

\Phi:(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)\to(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)
such that

\Phi\circ F\circ\Phi^{-1}=J,\qquad\Phi^{\prime}(0)=I.

(6)

If
\alpha
is Diophantine, then it is obviously
non-resonant
(NR for short)
to the effect that

\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{1}^{n_{1}}\alpha_{2}^{n_{2}}\neq 0\qquad\mbox{for any}\quad(n_{1},n_{1})\in N,\quad j=1,2.

Transcendence theory
[
1
]
tells that there is an important
situation where the converse implication is also true.

Theorem 2.2

Let
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
be algebraic numbers of modulus
1
.
If
\alpha
is NR then it is Diophantine.

We shall establish an equivariant version of Theorem
2.1
for
finite subgroups of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
.
To this end, let us recall the classification of nontrivial finite subgroups of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
;
see e.g. Lamotke
[
8
]
for details.

Theorem 2.3

Any nontrivial finite subgroup
G
of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
is one of the groups
listed in Table
2.1
, where the first column exhibits the
Dynkin diagram
D
of the exceptional set for the minimal resolution of the
quotient singularity
\mathbb{C}^{2}/G
, and binary dihedral groups of order
8
are distinguished
from other binary dihedral groups as quaternion groups.
Any two groups of the same Dynkin type are conjugate to each other in
\operatorname{SU}(2)
.

D_{\phantom{n}}

groups
G

order

n

\mathrm{A}_{n}

cyclic groups

n+1

\geq 1

\mathrm{D}_{4}

quaternion groups

8

4

\mathrm{D}_{n}

binary dihedral groups

4(n-2)

\geq 5

\mathrm{E}_{6}

binary tetrahedral groups

24

6

\mathrm{E}_{7}

binary octahedral groups

48

7

\mathrm{E}_{8}

binary icosahedral groups

120

8

Table 2.1:
Classification of nontrivial finite subgroups of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
.

Let
F:(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)\to(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)
be a local biholomorphism at the origin and
let
G
be a nontrivial finite subgroup of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
.
We say that
F
is
G
-
equivariant

if for any
g\in G
and any
z\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
sufficiently close to the origin

(F\circ g\circ F^{-1})(z)
stays in the
G
-orbit through
z
.
The aim of this section is to establish the following result.

Theorem 2.4

Let
G
be a nontrivial finite subgroup of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
and

F:(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)\to(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)
be a
G
-equivariant local biholomorphism with
Jacobian matrix
J:=F^{\prime}(0)\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
at the origin.
Then
J
normalizes the group
G
, that is,
JgJ^{-1}\in G
for every
g\in G
,
and
J
is diagonalizable unless
G
is a group of order
2
.
Suppose that

(\mathrm{i})

J
is diagonalizable when
G
is a group of order
2
, so it is diagonalizable in any case,

(\mathrm{ii})

the pair
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
is Diophantine, where
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}

are the eigenvalues of
J
.

Then there exists a unique local biholomorphism

\Phi:(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)\to(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)
that linearizes
F
into
J
as in equation (
6
).
The map
\Phi
commutes with every element of
G
.

The proof of this theorem is completed at the end of this section.
We begin with the following lemma, which is helpful in investigating the concept of
G
-equivariance.

Lemma 2.5

For
g\in\operatorname{SU}(2)
we put
K(g):=|\lambda-1|\geq 0
for an eigenvalue
\lambda
of
g
.
The number
K(g)
is well defined, i.e. independent of the choice of
\lambda
.
We have
K(g)=0
if and only if
g=1
, the unit element.
Moreover,

|gz-z|=K(g)\,|z|\qquad\mbox{for any}\quad z\in\mathbb{C}^{2},

(7)

where
|z|
is the standard Euclidean norm of
z
.
In particular the group
\operatorname{SU}(2)
acts on
\mathbb{C}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
freely.

Proof
.
The eigenvalues of
g\in\operatorname{SU}(2)
are a complex number
\lambda
of modulus
1
and
its reciprocal
\lambda^{-1}
, so we have
|\lambda^{-1}-1|=|\lambda^{-1}(1-\lambda)|=|\lambda-1|
.
Thus the value of
K(g)
is independent of the eigenvalue
\lambda
chosen.
There exists an orthonormal basis
u
,
v
of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
such that

gu=\lambda u
and
gv=\lambda^{-1}v
.
Notice that
g=1
if and only if
\lambda=1
, that is,
K(g)=0
.
Writing
z\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
as a linear combination
z=au+bv
with coefficients
a
,
b\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

gz-z=(\lambda-1)au+(\lambda^{-1}-1)bv
and hence
|gz-z|^{2}=|(\lambda-1)a|^{2}+|(\lambda^{-1}-1)b|^{2}=|\lambda-1|^{2}(|a|^{2}+|b|^{2})=K(g)^{2}|z|^{2}
, which gives formula (
7
).
If
g\in\operatorname{SU}(2)
fixes a nonzero vector
z\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
, then formula (
7
)
implies
K(g)=0
, which in turn forces
g=1
.
Thus the group
\operatorname{SU}(2)
acts on
\mathbb{C}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
freely.

\Box

Lemma 2.6

If
F
is
G
-equivariant, then its Jacobian matrix
J:=F^{\prime}(0)\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

at the origin normalizes the group
G
, that is,
JgJ^{-1}\in G
for every
g\in G
,
and we have

F\circ g\circ F^{-1}=JgJ^{-1}\quad\mbox{for every}\quad g\in G.

(8)

Proof
.
Since
F
is
G
-equivariant, for any
g\in G
and any
z\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\setminus\{0\}

near the origin there exists an element
\chi(g;z)\in G
such that

(F\circ g\circ F^{-1})(z)=\chi(g;z)z
.
Note that
\chi(g;z)
is unique since
G
acts on
\mathbb{C}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
freely.

Claim
. The element
\chi(g;z)\in G
is independent of
z
in a connected
punctured neighborhood of the origin.

For any
z
,
w\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
near the origin it follows from
formula (
7
) and
\chi(g;w)\in\operatorname{SU}(2)
that

\displaystyle K(\chi(g;w)^{-1}\chi(g;z))\,|z|

\displaystyle=|\chi(g;w)^{-1}\chi(g;z)z-z|=|\chi(g;z)z-\chi(g;w)z|

\displaystyle=|\chi(g;z)z-\chi(g;w)w+\chi(g;w)(w-z)|

\displaystyle\leq|\chi(g;z)z-\chi(g;w)w|+|\chi(g;w)(w-z)|

\displaystyle=|(F\circ g\circ F^{-1})(z)-(F\circ g\circ F^{-1})(w)|+|w-z|.

Fix
z\neq 0
and let
w
tend to
z
.
Since
(F\circ g\circ F^{-1})(w)
is continuous in
w
, there exists a positive
constant
\varepsilon
such that

|(F\circ g\circ F^{-1})(z)-(F\circ g\circ F^{-1})(w)|+|w-z|<K_{0}|z|

whenever
|w-z|<\varepsilon
, where
K_{0}
is defined by

K_{0}:=\min\{K(h)\mid h\in G\setminus\{1\}\}>0.

For some
w
with
|w-z|<\varepsilon
, if
\chi(g;w)\neq\chi(g;z)
, then

\chi(g;w)^{-1}\chi(g;z)\in G\setminus\{1\}
and the estimate above yields

K_{0}|z|\leq K(\chi(g;w)^{-1}\chi(g;z))\,|z|\leq|(F\circ g\circ F^{-1})(z)-(F\circ g\circ F^{-1})(w)|+|w-z|<K_{0}|z|,

which is a contradiction.
Thus
\chi(g;w)=\chi(g;z)
whenever
|w-z|<\varepsilon
.
Namely
\chi(g;z)
is a locally constant function of
z
in a punctured neighborhood of the origin.
This establishes the Claim.

By the Claim there exists a unique map
\chi:G\to G
,
g\mapsto\chi(g)
such that

(F\circ g\circ F^{-1})(z)=\chi(g)z
for every
z\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
near the origin (including
z=0
).
Differentiating this equation with respect to
z
at the origin, we have

JgJ^{-1}=\chi(g)\in G
for every
g\in G
.
This shows that
J\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
normalizes
G
and formula (
8
) holds true.

\Box

Lemma
2.6
tempts us to determine the normalizer
N_{\operatorname{GL}}
of
G
in
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
It suffices to know the normalizer
N_{\operatorname{SL}}
of
G
in
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, as

N_{\operatorname{GL}}=\mathbb{C}^{\times}\cdot N_{\operatorname{SL}}
.
When
G
is non-abelian, the following lemma is useful.

Lemma 2.7

If
G
is a non-abelian finite subgroup of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
then
N_{\operatorname{SL}}
is a finite subgroup of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
.

Proof
.
We begin by showing the following fact:
Let
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
u_{j}\in\operatorname{SU}(2)
for
j=1,2
.

Claim 1
. Suppose that
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
do not commute.
If
gu_{j}g^{-1}\in\operatorname{SU}(2)
for
j=1,2
, then
g\in\operatorname{SU}(2)
.

Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization allows us to write
g=th
for an

h\in\operatorname{SU}(2)
and an upper triangular matrix

t=\begin{pmatrix}r&s\\[2.84526pt]
0&r^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\qquad\mbox{with}\quad r>0,\quad s\in\mathbb{C}.

Put
v_{j}:=hu_{j}h^{-1}\in\operatorname{SU}(2)
for
j=1,2
.
Then
tv_{j}t^{-1}=gu_{j}g^{-1}\in\operatorname{SU}(2)
for
j=1,2
.
Since
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are non-commutative, so are
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
,
hence at least one of them, say
v_{j}
, is not diagonal.
Here we have

tv_{j}t^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha+r^{-1}s\beta&-s\{r(\alpha-\bar{\alpha})+s\beta\}-r^{2}\bar{\beta}\\[2.84526pt]
r^{-2}\beta&\bar{\alpha}\end{pmatrix}\quad\mbox{for}\quad v_{j}=\begin{pmatrix}[r]\alpha&-\bar{\beta}\\[2.84526pt]
\beta&\bar{\alpha}\end{pmatrix},

where
|\alpha|^{2}+|\beta|^{2}=1
and
\beta\neq 0
.
In view of
tv_{j}t^{-1}\in\operatorname{SU}(2)
, the
(1,1)
-entry of this
matrix must be the complex conjugate to the
(2,2)
-entry.
This forces
\alpha+r^{-1}s\beta=\alpha
, hence
s=0
and

tv_{j}t^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&-r^{2}\bar{\beta}\\[2.84526pt]
r^{-2}\beta&\bar{\alpha}\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SU}(2).

So the
(1,2)
-entry of this matrix must be the negative of the complex
conjugate to the
(2,1)
-entry.
This forces
r^{2}=r^{-2}
and so
r=1
, as
r
is real and positive.
Thus
t=I
and
g=h\in\operatorname{SU}(2)
, which proves the claim.

Claim
1
implies that
N:=N_{\operatorname{SL}}
is a subgroup of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
, since
G
is non-abelian.
Consider the homomorphism

\operatorname{Ad}_{G}:N\to\operatorname{Aut}(G),\,\,n\mapsto\operatorname{Ad}_{G}(n),\quad\mbox{defined by}\quad\operatorname{Ad}_{G}(n)g:=ngn^{-1}\quad\mbox{for}\quad g\in G.

Claim 2
. We have
\operatorname{Ker}(\operatorname{Ad}_{G})=\{\pm I\}
.

Indeed, the inclusion
\{\pm I\}\subset\operatorname{Ker}(\operatorname{Ad}_{G})
is obvious.
Conversely, let
h
be any element of
\operatorname{Ker}(\operatorname{Ad}_{G})
.
Then
h
commutes with every element in
G
.
However, any non-abelian finite subgroup of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
, in particular
the group
G
under consideration, acts on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
irreducibly.
Thus Schur’s lemma implies that
h
must be a scaler matrix.
Since
\det(h)=1
, we have
h=\pm I
and hence

\operatorname{Ker}(\operatorname{Ad}_{G})\subset\{\pm I\}
.
This proves Claim
2
.

Let
\operatorname{Ad}_{G}(N)\subset\operatorname{Aut}(G)
be the image of

\operatorname{Ad}_{G}:N\to\operatorname{Aut}(G)
.
Claim
2
yields the short exact sequence

\begin{CD}1@>{}>{}>\{\pm I\}@>{\mathrm{inclusion}}>{}>N@>{\operatorname{Ad}_{G}}>{}>\operatorname{Ad}_{G}(N)@>{}>{}>1.\end{CD}

Since
G
is a finite group, its automorphism group
\operatorname{Aut}(G)
is also finite,
and so is its subgroup
\operatorname{Ad}_{G}(N)
.
Now the exact sequence shows that
N
is also a finite group
with order
|N|=2|\operatorname{Ad}_{G}(N)|\leq 2|\operatorname{Aut}(G)|<\infty
.
\Box

Remark 2.8

By the
trivial subgroups
of a binary group we mean
\{I\}
,
\{\pm I\}

and the group itself.

(\mathrm{1})

Any binary octahedral group contains exactly two nontrivial normal
subgroups, which are a quaternion group and a binary tetrahedral group.

(\mathrm{2})

No binary octahedral group is a proper subgroup of a finite subgroup of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
.
Similarly, no binary icosahedral group is a proper subgroup of a finite subgroup of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
.

Theorem 2.9

If
G
be a nontrivial finite subgroup of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
, then its normalizer
N_{\operatorname{GL}}
in
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

is the nonzero scalar multiples of its normalizer
N_{\operatorname{SL}}
in
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, that is,

N_{\operatorname{GL}}=\mathbb{C}^{\times}\cdot N_{\operatorname{SL}}
, where
N_{\operatorname{SL}}
is given as follows.

(\mathrm{1})

Suppose that
G
is a cyclic group of order
n+1
.
If
n=1
then
N_{\operatorname{SL}}=\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
If
n\geq 2
and
G
is generated by
A(\varepsilon)
with
\varepsilon=\exp(2\pi\mathrm{i}/(n+1))
, then

N_{\operatorname{SL}}=\{A(a)\mid a\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}\}\cup\{B(b)\mid b\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}\}
,
where

A(a):=\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\[2.84526pt]
0&a^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\qquad B(b):=\begin{pmatrix}0&b\\[2.84526pt]
-b^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix}.

(\mathrm{2})

If
G
is a binary dihedral group of order
4(n-2)
with
n\geq 5
,
then
N_{\operatorname{SL}}
is the unique binary dihedral group of order
8(n-2)
that contains

G
as a subgroup of index
2
.

(\mathrm{3})

If
G
is a quaternion group or a binary tetrahedral group, then

N_{\operatorname{SL}}
is the unique binary octahedral group that contains
G
as a normal
subgroup.

(\mathrm{4})

If
G
is a binary octahedral group or a binary icosahedral group,
then
N_{\operatorname{SL}}
is the group
G
itself.

Every matrix in
N_{\operatorname{GL}}
is diagonalizable, unless
G
is a group of order
2
.

Proof
.
Assertion (1).
The case
n=1
is obvious.
Suppose that
n\geq 2
.
A direct calculation shows that

g\,A(\varepsilon)\,g^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}ad\varepsilon-bc\varepsilon^{-1}&-ab(\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{-1})\\[2.84526pt]
cd(\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{-1})&ad\varepsilon^{-1}-bc\varepsilon\end{pmatrix}\quad\mbox{for}\quad g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\[2.84526pt]
c&d\end{pmatrix},\quad ad-bc=1.

Notice that
\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{-1}
is nonzero, since
\varepsilon=\exp(2\pi\mathrm{i}/(n+1))

with
n\geq 2
is different from
\pm 1
.
If
g\in N_{\operatorname{SL}}
then
g\,A(\varepsilon)\,g^{-1}
must be diagonal, so we have

ab=0=cd
.
When
b=0
, we have
ad=1
and
c=0
, hence

a\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
,
g=A(a)
and
g\,A(\varepsilon)\,g^{-1}=A(\varepsilon)\in G
.
When
a=0
, we have
bc=-1
and
d=0
, hence

b\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
,
g=B(b)
and
g\,A(\varepsilon)\,g^{-1}=A(\varepsilon)^{-1}\in G
.
Thus
N_{\operatorname{SL}}=\{A(a)\mid a\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}\}\cup\{B(b)\mid b\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}\}
.

Assertion (2).
We may assume that
G
is generated by
A(\varepsilon)
and
B(1)
, where

\varepsilon=\exp(\pi\mathrm{i}/(n-2))
.
In this case
G
has a total of
4(n-2)
elements

A(\varepsilon^{j})
and
B(\varepsilon^{j})
,
j=1,\dots,2(n-2)
, with all

B(\varepsilon^{j})
being of order
2
.
If
g\in N_{\operatorname{SL}}
then
g\,A(\varepsilon)\,g^{-1}\in G
must be diagonal,
because it is an element of order
2(n-2)\geq 6>2
.
Then the same reasoning as in the proof of assertion (1) shows that
g

must be either
A(a)
or
B(a)
for some
a\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.
When
g=A(a)
, we have
g\,A(\varepsilon^{j})\,g^{-1}=A(\varepsilon^{j})
and

g\,B(\varepsilon^{j})\,g^{-1}=B(a^{2}\varepsilon^{j})
.
When
g=B(a)
, we have
g\,A(\varepsilon^{j})\,g^{-1}=A(\varepsilon^{-j})
and

g\,B(\varepsilon^{j})\,g^{-1}=B(a^{2}\varepsilon^{-j})
.
In either case we have
g\,B(\varepsilon^{j})\,g^{-1}\in G
for
j=1,\dots,2(n-2)

if and only if
a^{2}
is a
2(n-1)
-th root of unity, that is,
a
is a
4(n-2)
-th root of unity.
Thus we have

N_{\operatorname{SL}}=\{A(a)\mid a^{4(n-2)}=1\}\cup\{B(a)\mid a^{4(n-2)}=1\}.

This means that
N_{\operatorname{SL}}
is a binary dihedral group of order
8(n-2)
that contains
G
.
Let
G^{\prime}
be any binary dihedral group of order
8(n-2)
that contains
G
.
Then
G
is of index
2
in
G^{\prime}
and hence a normal subgroup of
G^{\prime}\subset\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
This fact forces
G^{\prime}\subset N_{\operatorname{SL}}
and so
G^{\prime}=N_{\operatorname{SL}}
, because
G^{\prime}
and
N_{\operatorname{SL}}

are of the same order.
Therefore
N_{\operatorname{SL}}
is the unique binary dihedral group of order
8(n-2)
that contains
G
.

Assertion (3).
We begin by the case where
G
is a quaternion group.
Take a binary octahedral group
O_{0}
.
It contains a quaternion group
G_{0}
as a normal subgroup by
Remark
2.8
.(1).
Since any two quaternion groups are conjugate to each other in
\operatorname{SU}(2)
,
there exists a conjugation
\varphi:\operatorname{SU}(2)\to\operatorname{SU}(2)
that converts
G_{0}
to
G
.
Let
O\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
be the image of
O_{0}
under the map
\varphi
.
Then
O
is a binary octahedral group that contains
G
as a normal subgroup.
Hence
O
is a subgroup of the normalizer
N_{\operatorname{SL}}
of
G
in
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
Lemma
2.7
implies
O\subset N_{\operatorname{SL}}\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
,
since
G
is non-abelian.
This inclusion forces
O=N_{\operatorname{SL}}
by Remark
2.8
.(2).
We also have the uniqueness of the binary octahedral group that contains

G
as a normal subgroup, because any such group coincides with the
normalizer
N_{\operatorname{SL}}
.
This proves assertion (3) for quaternion groups.
Assertion (3) for binary tetrahedral groups can be established just in the same manner.

Assertion (4).
The proof of this assertion is analogous to that of assertion (3) or even simpler than that.
Indeed, if
G
is a binary octahedral group or a binary icosahedral group,
then Lemma
2.7
implies
G\subset N_{\operatorname{SL}}\subset\operatorname{SU}(2)
.
This inclusion forces
G=N_{\operatorname{SL}}
by Remark
2.8
.(2).

The final statement in the theorem follows from assertion (1) when
G
is a cyclic group
of order
\geq 3
, since
A(a)
is diagonal and
B(b)
is diagonalizable.
It follows from Lemma
2.7
when
G
is a non-cyclic group and
hence a non-abelian group by Theorem
2.3
,
since every matrix in
\operatorname{SU}(2)
is diagonalizable.
\Box

Lemma 2.10

Suppose that the
G
-equivariant map
F
is linearized into its Jacobian matrix

J
by a local biholomorphism
\Phi
as in formula (
6
).
Let
\Psi:(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)\to(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)
be the local holomorphism
defined by

\Psi:=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}\Phi^{g},

(9)

where
\Phi^{g}:=g^{-1}\circ\Phi\circ g
for
g\in G
.
If
\Psi^{g}
is defined in a similar manner, then
\Psi
satisfies the equations

\Psi\circ F\circ\Psi^{-1}=J,\qquad\Psi^{\prime}(0)=I,\qquad\Psi^{g}=\Psi\quad\mbox{for every}\quad g\in G.

(10)

Proof
.
Putting
\chi(g):=JgJ^{-1}
for
g\in G
, we begin by showing that

\Psi=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}\Phi^{\chi(g)},\qquad\Phi^{\chi(g)}\circ F=J\circ\Phi^{g}\quad\mbox{for}\quad g\in G.

(11)

By Lemma
2.6
we have
\chi\in\operatorname{Aut}(G)
and hence the first equality in (
11
).
The second equality follows from

\displaystyle\Phi^{\chi(g)}\circ F

\displaystyle=\chi(g)^{-1}\circ\Phi\circ\chi(g)\circ F=\chi(g^{-1})\circ\Phi\circ(F\circ g\circ F^{-1})\circ F=\chi(g^{-1})\circ(\Phi\circ F)\circ g

\displaystyle=(Jg^{-1}J^{-1})\circ(J\circ\Phi)\circ g=J\circ(g^{-1}\circ\Phi\circ g)=J\circ\Phi^{g},

where formulas (
8
) and (
6
) are used in the second and
fourth equalities.
Formulas (
11
) then lead to

\displaystyle\Psi\circ F=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}\Phi^{\chi(g)}\circ F=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}J\circ\Phi^{g}=J\circ\left(\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}\Phi^{g}\right)=J\circ\Psi.

It is evident from
\Phi(0)=I
in (
6
) and definition (
9
) that
\Psi(0)=I
.
This in particular implies that
\Psi
is a local biholomorphism around the origin, so the
equation
\Psi\circ F=J\circ\Psi
becomes
\Psi\circ F\circ\Psi^{-1}=J
.
Finally we have

\displaystyle\Psi^{g}

\displaystyle=g^{-1}\circ\left(\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{h\in G}\Phi^{h}\right)\circ g=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{h\in G}g^{-1}\circ\Phi^{h}\circ g=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{h\in G}\Phi^{hg}=\Psi

for every
g\in G
.
Therefore all equations in (
10
) are verified.

\Box

Proof of Theorem
2.4
.
The first half of Theorem
2.4
follows from Lemma
2.6

and the last part of Theorem
2.9
.
Now
J
is always diagonalizable by assumption (i) in Theorem
2.4
.
By Theorem
2.1
together with assumption (ii), there exists a unique
local biholomorphism
\Phi:(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)\to(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)
satisfying condition (
6
).
The first two equations in (
10
) show that the map
\Psi
in definition (
9
)
is also such a local biholomorphism.
So the uniqueness assertion in Theorem
2.1
forces
\Phi=\Psi
.
This together with the third equation in (
10
) implies that
\Phi^{g}=\Phi
,
that is,
\Phi
commutes with every element
g\in G
.
\Box

3
Resolutions of Singularities

For a nontrivial finite subgroup
G
of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
the orbit space
S:=\mathbb{C}^{2}/G

has a Kleinian singularity at the origin.
Constructing a minimal resolution
X
of
S
is a classical topic in singularity theory and
a detailed explanation of it can be found e.g. in Lamotke
[
8
]
.
We review it in a manner suitable for our purpose.
Explicit description of an atlas on
X
, although somewhat technical, is necessary
for our calculations in later sections.

3.1
Cyclic Cases

Let us consider the case where
G
is a cyclic group of order
n+1
with
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
We may assume that
G
is generated by the diagonal matrix

g=\operatorname{diag}(\zeta^{-1},\,\zeta)
, where
\zeta=\exp(2\pi\mathrm{i}/(n+1))
.
The group
G
acts on the polynomial ring
\mathbb{C}[w_{1},w_{2}]
in a natural manner
and the ring
\mathbb{C}[w_{1},w_{2}]^{G}
of
G
-invariant polynomials is generated by

x:=w_{1}w_{2},\qquad y:=w_{1}^{n+1},\qquad z:=w_{2}^{n+1},

(12)

which satisfy the relation
x^{n+1}=yz
.
So the orbit space
S:=\mathbb{C}^{2}/G
is realized as an affine algebraic surface

S=\{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\mid x^{n+1}-yz=0\},

which has a Kleinian singularity of Dynkin type
\mathrm{A}_{n}
at the origin
0=(0,0,0)\in S
.

Let
X
be the set of all points
p=((x,y,z),[a_{1}:b_{1}],\dots,[a_{n}:b_{n}])\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\times(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{n}

which satisfy the equations

a_{i}b_{i+1}x=a_{i+1}b_{i},\qquad 0\leq i\leq n,

with
a_{0}:=1
,
a_{n+1}:=y
,
b_{0}:=z
,
b_{n+1}:=1
by convention.
Then a minimal resolution of
S
is given by

X\to S,\quad p=((x,y,z),[a_{1}:b_{1}],\dots,[a_{n}:b_{n}])\mapsto(x,y,z).

Its exceptional set
E
, that is, the inverse image of the origin under this map,
is the union of
(-2)
-curves

E_{i}:=\{((0,0,0),\overbrace{[1:0],\dots,[1:0]}^{i-1},[a_{i}:b_{i}],\overbrace{[0:1],\dots,[0:1]}^{n-i})\mid[a_{i}:b_{i}]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}\},\qquad 1\leq i\leq n.

The dual graph of
E
is a Dynkin diagram of type
\mathrm{A}_{n}
, that is,

\textstyle{E_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{n}}

(13)

where the consecutive
(-2)
-curves
E_{i}
and
E_{i+1}
meet transversally in a single point

p_{i}:=((0,0,0),\overbrace{[1:0],\dots,[1:0]}^{i},\overbrace{[0:1],\dots,[0:1]}^{n-i}),\qquad 1\leq i\leq n-1.

(14)

The smooth surface
X
is covered by the complex coordinate system
\{(W_{i},\varphi_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n+1}

consisting of

\displaystyle W_{i}:=\{p=((x,y,z),[a_{1}:b_{1}],\dots,[a_{n}:b_{n}])\in X\mid a_{1}\cdots a_{i-1}b_{i}\cdots b_{n}\neq 0\},

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}:W_{i}\to\mathbb{C}^{2},\quad p\mapsto(u_{i},v_{i-1}):=(a_{i}/b_{i},b_{i-1}/a_{i-1}),\qquad 1\leq i\leq n+1.

A little calculation shows that the inverse mapping of
\varphi_{i}
is given by

\varphi_{i}^{-1}:\mathbb{C}^{2}\to W_{i},\quad(u_{i},v_{i-1})\mapsto((x,y,z),[1:v_{1}],\dots,[1:v_{i-1}],[u_{i}:1],\dots,[u_{n}:1]),

where
v_{j}:=u_{i}^{i-j-1}v_{i-1}^{i-j}
for
1\leq j\leq i-1
,

u_{j}:=u_{i}^{j-i+1}v_{i-1}^{j-i}
for
i\leq j\leq n
, and

x:=u_{i}v_{i-1},\qquad y:=u_{i}^{n-i+2}v_{i-1}^{n-i+1},\qquad z:=u_{i}^{i-1}v_{i-1}^{i},\qquad 1\leq i\leq n+1.

(15)

Notice also that the inversion of formula (
15
) is given by

u_{i}=x^{i}z^{-1}=x^{i-n-1}y,\qquad v_{i-1}=x^{1-i}z=x^{n+2-i}y^{-1},\qquad 1\leq i\leq n+1.

(16)

For
1\leq i<j\leq n+1
the intersection of
W_{i}
and
W_{j}
is described as follows:

•

If
j=i+1
then
\varphi_{i}(W_{i}\cap W_{i+1})=\{u_{i}\neq 0\}
and
\varphi_{i+1}(W_{i}\cap W_{i+1})=\{v_{i}\neq 0\}
.

•

If
j\geq i+2
then
\varphi_{i}(W_{i}\cap W_{j})=\{u_{i}v_{i-1}\neq 0\}
and
\varphi_{j}(W_{i}\cap W_{j})=\{u_{j}v_{j-1}\neq 0\}
.

For
1\leq i\neq j\leq n+1
the coordinate transformation from
(W_{i},\varphi_{i})
to
(W_{j},\varphi_{j})
is given by

\varphi_{j}\circ\varphi_{i}^{-1}:\varphi_{i}(W_{i}\cap W_{j})\to\varphi_{j}(W_{i}\cap W_{j}),\qquad u_{j}=u_{i}^{j-i+1}v_{i-1}^{j-i},\quad v_{j-1}=u_{i}^{i-j}v_{i-1}^{i-j+1}.

We remark that definition (
14
) makes sense for
i=0
and for
i=n
, allowing to define
two additional points
p_{0}
and
p_{n}
.
If we consider the affine curves
E_{0}:=\{u_{1}=0\}
in
W_{1}
and
E_{n+1}:=\{v_{n}=0\}
in
W_{n+1}
,
then

E_{0}\cap E_{1}=\{p_{0}\}\leftrightarrow(u_{1},v_{0})=(0,0),\qquad E_{n}\cap E_{n+1}=\{p_{n}\}\leftrightarrow(u_{n+1},v_{n})=(0,0).

(17)

The points
p_{0}
and
p_{n}
as well as the affine curves
E_{0}
and
E_{n+1}
also play important
roles in §
3.2
and §
4
.

Another remark is that the construction so far carries over, when we restrict the entire
(w_{1},w_{2})
-space

\mathbb{C}^{2}
to a subspace
\mathbb{C}\times U
, where
U:=\{w_{2}\in\mathbb{C}\mid|w_{2}|<r\}
with
r>0
, redefine
the cyclic quotient sigularity as
S:=(\mathbb{C}\times U)/G
and modify the minimal resolution
X\to S
accordingly.
In §
3.2
we need a description of this construction in terms of a pushforward lift on a cotangent bundle.
For a local biholomorphism of complex manifolds
f:M\to N
, its
pushforward lift

f_{*}:T^{*}M\to T^{*}N

is defined by
(f_{*})_{p}:=(g^{*})_{f(p)}:T_{p}^{*}M\to T_{f(p)}^{*}N
at
p\in M
, where
g:(N,f(p))\to(M,p)
is
the local inverse to the local biholomorphism
f:(M,p)\to(N,f(p))
around
p\in M
.
The pushforward lift
f_{*}
is again a local biholomorphism; if
f
is a global biholomorphism then so is
f_{*}
.
We will always think of a space as a subspace of its cotangent bundle, upon identifying it with
the zero section of the bundle.
In particular a point on the space is a point in its cotangent bundle.

Back in the current situation, under the identification
\mathbb{C}\times U=T^{*}U
,
(w_{1},w_{2})\leftrightarrow w_{1}\,dw_{2}
,
the action of
g=\operatorname{diag}(\zeta^{-1},\zeta)\in G
on
\mathbb{C}\times U
is just the pushforward lift
\gamma_{*}:T^{*}U\to T^{*}U

of the rotation
\gamma:U\to U
,
w_{2}\mapsto\zeta w_{2}
.
The quotient map
U\to V:=U/\langle\gamma\rangle
is realized by

V:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid|z|<r^{n+1}\}
and
w_{2}\mapsto z:=w_{2}^{n+1}
.
It restricts to an unramiled
\langle\gamma\rangle
-covering
U\setminus\{0\}\to V\setminus\{0\}
,
so the pushforward lift
T^{*}(U\setminus\{0\})\to T^{*}(V\setminus\{0\})
,

w_{1}\,dw_{2}\mapsto\xi\,dz
, induces an isomorphism

(T^{*}(U\setminus\{0\}))/G\to T^{*}(V\setminus\{0\})
.
This gives an identification

T^{*}(V\setminus\{0\})=(T^{*}(U\setminus\{0\}))/G=S\setminus\{z=0\},

(18)

which avoids the singularity
0\in S
.
Thus
T^{*}(V\setminus\{0\})
can be thought of as an open subset of the space
X
.

Lemma 3.1

The zero section of
T^{*}(V\setminus\{0\})
coincides with the punctured curve
E_{0}\setminus\{p_{0}\}
in
X
.

Proof
.
Formula (
15
) with
i=1
reads
x=u_{1}v_{0}
and
z=v_{0}
,
so the definition of pushforward lift together with formula (
12
) tells us that
the fiber coordinate
\xi
of the cotangent bundle
T^{*}(V\setminus\{0\})
is calculated as

\xi=\dfrac{w_{1}}{(dz/dw_{2})}=\frac{w_{1}}{(n+1)w_{2}^{n}}=\frac{w_{1}w_{2}}{(n+1)w_{2}^{n+1}}=\frac{x}{(n+1)z}=\frac{u_{1}v_{0}}{(n+1)v_{0}}=\frac{u_{1}}{n+1}.

Note also that
z=v_{0}\neq 0
on
T^{*}(V\setminus\{0\})=S\setminus\{z=0\}
.
Thus the zero section
\{\xi=0\}
of
T^{*}(V\setminus\{0\})
coincides with
the curve
E_{0}=\{u_{1}=0\}
with the point
p_{0}\leftrightarrow(u_{1},v_{0})=(0,0)
removed.

\Box

3.2
Non-Cyclic Cases

Any finite non-cyclic subgroup of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
is a binary polyhedral group by Theorem
2.3
.
To deal with this case, we begin with a general construction for the standard
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-action on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
First we consider the action of the subgroup
\{\pm 1\}\subset\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
The quotient map
\pi:\mathbb{C}^{2}\to Y:=\mathbb{C}^{2}/\{\pm 1\}
is realized by

\pi:\mathbb{C}^{2}\to Y:=\{(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\mid y_{1}y_{2}=y_{3}^{2}\},\quad(w_{1},w_{2})\mapsto(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}):=(w_{1}^{2},w_{2}^{2},w_{1}w_{2}).

The group
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
acts on
Y\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}
through the group homomorphism

\rho:\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\to\operatorname{GL}_{3}(\mathbb{C}),\quad\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}a^{2}&b^{2}&2ab\\
c^{2}&d^{2}&2cd\\
ac&bd&ad+bc\end{pmatrix},

where
\rho(g)
preserves
Y
for every
g\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
This action descends to an action of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})/\{\pm 1\}
, since the kernel of
\rho
is
\{\pm 1\}
.
Now the map
\pi:\mathbb{C}^{2}\to Y
is
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-equivariant.

Consider the blowup
\varSigma_{1}\to\mathbb{C}^{2}
at the origin in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
Explicitly,
\varSigma_{1}
is realized by

\varSigma_{1}:=\{(w,[z])=((w_{1},w_{2}),[z_{1}:z_{2}])\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\mid z_{1}w_{2}=z_{2}w_{1}\},

with the blowup morphism being the first projection
(w,[z])\mapsto w
.
The group
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
acts on
\varSigma_{1}
diagonally by

g(w,[z]):=(gw,[gz])\qquad\mbox{for}\quad g\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}),\,\,(w,[z])\in\varSigma_{1}.

Moreover the quotient space
\varSigma_{2}:=\varSigma_{1}/\{\pm 1\}
and the quotient map

\phi:\varSigma_{1}\to\varSigma_{2}
are realized by

\displaystyle\varSigma_{2}

\displaystyle:=\{((y_{1},y_{2}),[z_{1}:z_{2}])\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\mid z_{1}^{2}y_{2}=z_{2}^{2}y_{1}\},

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle:\varSigma_{1}\to\varSigma_{2},\quad((w_{1},w_{2}),[z_{1}:z_{2}])\mapsto((y_{1},y_{2}),[z_{1}:z_{2}])\qquad\mbox{with}\quad(y_{1},y_{2}):=(w_{1}^{2},w_{2}^{2}).

Then the map
\phi:\varSigma_{1}\to\varSigma_{2}
is
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-equivariant,
where
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
acts on
\varSigma_{2}
by

g\left(\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{pmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}z_{1}\\
z_{2}\end{bmatrix}\right):=\left(\begin{pmatrix}a^{2}y_{1}+b^{2}y_{2}+2ab\,y_{3}\\
c^{2}y_{1}+d^{2}y_{2}+2cd\,y_{3}\end{pmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}az_{1}+bz_{2}\\
cz_{1}+dz_{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)\quad\mbox{for}\quad g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}).

(19)

This action naturally descends to an action of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})/\{\pm 1\}
.
Thanks to the relation
z_{1}^{2}y_{2}=z_{2}^{2}y_{1}
, a well-defined map

\psi:\varSigma_{2}\to Y
,
((y_{1},y_{2}),[z_{1}:z_{2}])\mapsto(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})

is defined by
y_{3}:=(z_{2}/z_{1})\,y_{1}
if
z_{1}\neq 0
and
y_{3}:=(z_{1}/z_{2})\,y_{2}
if

z_{2}\neq 0
, so that there exists a
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-equivariant commutative diagram

\begin{CD}\varSigma_{1}@>{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{blowup}}}>{}>\mathbb{C}^{2}\\
@V{{\phi}}V{}V@V{}V{{\pi}}V\\
\varSigma_{2}@>{{\psi}}>{}>Y.\end{CD}

(20)

The surface
\varSigma_{2}
together with the second projection
\varSigma_{2}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
(y,[z])\mapsto[z]
is a line bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
isomorphic to the
holomorphic cotangent bundle
T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
Let
g\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
act on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
as a projective linear transformation
in the usual manner.
Then under the identification
T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1}=\varSigma_{2}
the pushforward
lift
g_{*}:T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1}\to T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1}
is given by

g_{*}\left(\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{pmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}z_{1}\\
z_{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)=\left(\frac{1}{\det g}\begin{pmatrix}a^{2}y_{1}+b^{2}y_{2}+2ab\,y_{3}\\
c^{2}y_{1}+d^{2}y_{2}+2cd\,y_{3}\end{pmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}az_{1}+bz_{2}\\
cz_{1}+dz_{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)\quad\mbox{for}\quad g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}).

(21)

In particular the actions (
19
) and (
21
) coincide
when they are restricted to the subgroup
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Consider a diagonalizable matrix
g\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
with eigenvalues
\beta\alpha^{\pm 1}
.
Let
l\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}
be an eigenline of
g
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\beta\alpha
.
If
\alpha\neq\pm 1
then
l
is unique; otherwise,
g
is a scalar matrix
and
l
is an arbitrary line through the origin.
The line
l
designates a unique point
q\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
, which is fixed by
[g]\in\operatorname{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Lemma 3.2

In the setting mentioned above the matrix
g\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
acts on
\varSigma_{2}=T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1}
by

g:(w_{1},\,w_{2})\mapsto(\beta(\beta\alpha^{2})w_{1},\,\beta(\beta\alpha^{2})^{-1}w_{2})\quad\mbox{around the fixed point $q\in\mathbb{P}^{1}\subset T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1}$},

(22)

in terms of an inhomogeneous coordinate
w_{2}
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
around
q
and the associated
fiber coordinate
w_{1}
on
T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Proof
.
Taking a conjugate to
g
in
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
if necessary, we may assume from the beginning that

g
is a diagonal matrix
\operatorname{diag}(\beta\alpha,\,\beta\alpha^{-1})
and
q
is the point
[1:0]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
Then formula (
19
) reads

g((y_{1},y_{2}),\,[z_{1}:z_{2}])=(((\beta\alpha)^{2}y_{1},\,(\beta\alpha^{-1})^{2}y_{2}),\,[\beta\alpha z_{1}:\beta\alpha^{-1}z_{2}]).

We can now take
w_{2}:=z_{2}/z_{1}
as an inhomogeneous coordinate of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
around
q=[1:0]

and
w_{1}:=y_{1}
as the associated fiber coordinate on the cotangent bundle
T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1}
near the point
q
.
In terms of the local chart
(w_{1},w_{2})
we have

g(w_{1},\,w_{2})=(\beta^{2}\alpha^{2}w_{1},\,\alpha^{-2}w_{2})
and hence formula (
22
).

\Box

Table 3.1:
Numerical data concerning exceptional orbits.

Let
G
be a binary polyhedral group in
\operatorname{SU}(2)
.
Then
G
has center
\{\pm 1\}
and the quotient group
\varGamma:=G/\{\pm 1\}

is a regular polyhedral group in
\operatorname{PSU}(2)\cong\operatorname{SO}(3)
.
We have an identification
S:=\mathbb{C}^{2}/G=Y/\varGamma
via the map
\pi
in diagram (
20
).
Together with the identification
\varSigma_{2}/\varGamma=(T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1})/\varGamma
the map

\psi
in (
20
) induces a morphism

\psi:(T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1})/\varGamma=\varSigma_{2}/\varGamma\to Y/\varGamma=S.

This map is composed with a minimal resolution of
(T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1})/\varGamma
to get a
minimal resolution of
S
, where
\varGamma
acts on
T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1}
as the pushforward
lift of the usual action of
\varGamma
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
as projective linear transformations.
A point
q\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
is said to be
exceptional
if the isotropy subgroup

\varGamma_{q}:=\{\gamma\in\varGamma\mid\gamma(q)=q\}
is nontrivial.
All exceptional points in
\mathbb{P}^{1}
are partitioned into three
\varGamma
-orbits
P^{\mathrm{e}}
,
P^{\mathrm{f}}
,
P^{\mathrm{v}}
, which
arise respectively from the mid-edge points, face-centers, vertices of a regular polyhedron having
\varGamma
as its rotational symmetry group.
For
\nu=\mathrm{e},\mathrm{f},\mathrm{v}
, if
q\in P^{\nu}
then
\varGamma_{q}
is a cyclic group of order
n^{\nu}+1
and
P^{\nu}

is a set of cardinality
|\varGamma|/(n^{\nu}+1)
, where the number
n^{\nu}
and related quantities are given
in Table
3.1
.
The quotient space
\mathbf{P}^{1}:=\mathbb{P}^{1}/\varGamma
is again a projective line and the quotient map

\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbf{P}^{1}
restricts to an unramified
\varGamma
-covering

\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus(P^{\mathrm{e}}\cup P^{\mathrm{f}}\cup P^{\mathrm{v}})\to\mathbf{P}^{1}\setminus\{p^{\mathrm{e}},p^{\mathrm{f}},p^{\mathrm{v}}\}
,
where the point
p^{\nu}\in\mathbf{P}^{1}
is the image of
P^{\nu}
.
So its pushforward lift is again an unramified
\varGamma
-covering,

T^{*}(\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus(P^{\mathrm{e}}\cup P^{\mathrm{f}}\cup P^{\mathrm{v}}))\to T^{*}(\mathbf{P}^{1}\setminus\{p^{\mathrm{e}},p^{\mathrm{f}},p^{\mathrm{v}}\}),

(23)

which induces an isomorphism
T^{*}(\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus(P^{\mathrm{e}}\cup P^{\mathrm{f}}\cup P^{\mathrm{v}}))/\varGamma\to T^{*}(\mathbf{P}^{1}\setminus\{p^{\mathrm{e}},p^{\mathrm{f}},p^{\mathrm{v}}\})
.
For each
\nu=\mathrm{e},\mathrm{f},\mathrm{v}
, take a representative point
q^{\nu}\in P^{\nu}
and write
\varGamma^{\nu}:=\varGamma_{q^{\nu}}
.
Let
U^{\nu}
be a sufficiently small
\varGamma^{\nu}
-invariant disk in
\mathbb{P}^{1}
centered at
q^{\nu}
.
The pushforward lift
T^{*}U^{\nu}\to T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1}
of the inclusion
U^{\nu}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
induces
an isomorphism of
(T^{*}U^{\nu})/\varGamma^{\nu}
onto an open subset of
(T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1})/\varGamma
.
From these observations we have open embeddings

T^{*}(\mathbf{P}^{1}\setminus\{p^{\mathrm{e}},p^{\mathrm{f}},p^{\mathrm{v}}\})\hookrightarrow(T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1})/\varGamma\hookleftarrow(T^{*}U^{\nu})/\varGamma^{\nu},\qquad\nu=\mathrm{e},\mathrm{f},\mathrm{v}.

(24)

Since
S^{\nu}:=(T^{*}U^{\nu})/\varGamma^{\nu}
is a cyclic quotient singularity of order

n^{\nu}+1
, the recipe in §
3.1
gives a minimal resolution
X^{\nu}\to S^{\nu}
.
Various objects associated with this resolution are denoted by the same symbols as those
in §
3.1
followed by a superscript
\nu=\mathrm{e},\mathrm{f},\mathrm{v}
.
For example the exceptional set
E^{\nu}\subset X^{\nu}
is the union of
(-2)
-curves

E^{\nu}_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n^{\nu}
.
A minimal resolution
X
of
(T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1})/\varGamma
and hence of
S:=\mathbb{C}^{2}/G
is obtained by gluing three pieces

X^{\nu}
,
\nu=\mathrm{e},\mathrm{f},\mathrm{v}
, to
T^{*}(\mathbf{P}^{1}\setminus\{p^{\mathrm{e}},p^{\mathrm{f}},p^{\mathrm{v}}\})

via the mappings (
24
) along the open sets

S^{\nu}\setminus\{z^{\nu}=0\}=(T^{*}(U^{\nu}\setminus\{q^{\nu}\}))/\varGamma^{\nu}=T^{*}(V^{\nu}\setminus\{p^{\nu}\}),

where
V^{\nu}
is the image of
U^{\nu}
under the projection
\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbf{P}^{1}

and the identification (
18
) is employed.
Let
E_{0}
be the closure in
X
of the zero section of
T^{*}(\mathbf{P}^{1}\setminus\{p^{\mathrm{e}},p^{\mathrm{f}},p^{\mathrm{v}}\})
.
Here the present
E_{0}
is different from
E_{0}
in §
3.1
, the latter being
E_{0}^{\nu}

in the current situation.
Then
E_{0}
is also a
(-2)
-curve in
X
and the union
E:=E_{0}\cup E^{\mathrm{e}}\cup E^{\mathrm{f}}\cup E^{\mathrm{v}}

constitutes the exceptional set of the minimal resolution
X\to S
.
Lemma
3.1
implies

E_{0}^{\nu}\subset E_{0},\qquad E^{\nu}_{1}\cap E_{0}=\{p^{\nu}_{0}\},\qquad\nu=\mathrm{e},\mathrm{f},\mathrm{v},

(25)

and the dual graph of
E
is given by the left diagram in Figure
3.1
,
while the right diagram shows its arm
E^{\nu}
.

\textstyle{E^{\mathrm{e}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{\mathrm{f}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{\mathrm{v}}}

\textstyle{E_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{1}^{\nu}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{n^{\nu}}^{\nu}}

Figure 3.1:
Dynkin diagram of type
\mathrm{D}
or
\mathrm{E}
and its arms.

4
Normalizers on Minimal Resolutions

Let
G
be a nontrivial finite subgroup of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
and
X\to S
be a minimal
resolution of the quotient singularity
S:=\mathbb{C}^{2}/G
as constructed in §
3
.
The exceptional set
E\subset X
is a union of
(-2)
-curves, the dual graph of which
is a Dynkin diagram
D
of type
\mathrm{A}
,
\mathrm{D}
or
\mathrm{E}
, where the correspondence
G\mapsto D
is
given in Table
2.1
.
Let
N_{\operatorname{GL}}
be the normalizer of
G
in
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
For each
J\in N_{\operatorname{GL}}
the linear automorphism
J:\mathbb{C}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}^{2}
descends to a map
S\to S
,
which in turn lifts to a holomorphic automorphism
f_{J}:X\to X
.
It permutes the
(-2)
-curves in
E
and induces a diagram automorphism
\sigma_{J}:D\to D
.
According to their Dynkin types, the automorphism groups
\operatorname{Aut}(D)
are classified in Table
4.1
.
The aim of this section is to describe how the map
f_{J}\in\operatorname{Aut}(X)
acts on the exceptional set
E

in connection with the action of
\sigma_{J}\in\operatorname{Aut}(D)
on the diagram
D
.

D

\mathrm{A}_{n}

\mathrm{D}_{4}

\mathrm{D}_{n}

\mathrm{E}_{6}

\mathrm{E}_{7}

\operatorname{Aut}(D)
