Table 4.1:
Dynkin diagrams
D
and their automorphism groups
\operatorname{Aut}(D)
.

Remark 4.1

For
K\in N_{\operatorname{SL}}
let
\langle K\rangle
denote the class of
K
in the quotient group
N_{\operatorname{SL}}/G
.
If
J\in N_{\operatorname{GL}}
is expressed as
J=\beta K
with
\beta\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and

K\in N_{\operatorname{SL}}
, then
f_{J}
depends only on
\beta
and
\langle K\rangle
, since
so does the map
S\to S
.
Theorem
2.9
and Table
4.1
show that when
G
is of type
\mathrm{D}
or
E

we have an isomorphism
N_{\operatorname{SL}}/G\cong\operatorname{Aut}(D)
.

4.1
Cyclic Cases

Let
G
be the cyclic group of order
n+1
as in §
3.1
, where
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
The notation there is retained in this subsection.

Lemma 4.2

If
J\in N_{\operatorname{GL}}
is a diagonal matrix of the form

J=\beta K\qquad\mbox{with}\quad K=\operatorname{diag}(\alpha,\,\alpha^{-1})\in N_{\operatorname{SL}},

(26)

where
\alpha
,
\beta\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, then the induced automorphism

f_{J}:X\to X
maps each
W_{i}
onto itself, sending

(u_{i},v_{i-1})\mapsto(u_{i}^{\prime},v_{i-1}^{\prime}):=(\beta^{2i-n-1}\alpha^{n+1}u_{i},\,\beta^{n+3-2i}\alpha^{-n-1}v_{i-1}),\qquad 1\leq i\leq n+1.

(27)

It preserves each
E_{i}
,
0\leq i\leq n+1
, and fixes each
p_{i}
,
0\leq i\leq n
.
In particular
\sigma_{J}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathrm{A}_{n})
is trivial.
The multipliers of
f_{J}
at
p_{i}
are
\beta^{n+1-2i}\alpha^{-n-1}
along
E_{i}
and

\beta^{2i-n+1}\alpha^{n+1}
along
E_{i+1}
, so
\det(df_{J})_{p_{i}}=\beta^{2}=\det J
.

Proof
.
In view of formula (
12
) the matrix
J
induces an automorphism

S\to S,\quad(x,y,z)\mapsto(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime}):=(\beta^{2}x,\,(\beta\alpha)^{n+1}y,\,(\beta\alpha^{-1})^{n+1}z).

(28)

The map
f_{J}
is then calculated as the composite of the following three maps

\begin{CD}(u_{i},v_{i-1})@>{\eqref{eqn:resol2-1a}}>{}>(x,z)@>{\eqref{eqn:f2a}}>{}>(x^{\prime},z^{\prime})@>{(\ref{eqn:resol2-3}^{\prime})}>{}>(u_{i}^{\prime},v_{i-1}^{\prime}),\end{CD}

where
(\ref{eqn:resol2-3}^{\prime})
is the map (
16
) with
(u_{i},v_{i-1})
and
(x,y)

replaced by
(u_{i}^{\prime},v_{i-1}^{\prime})
and
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
.
Explicitly we have

\displaystyle u_{i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(x^{\prime})^{i}(z^{\prime})^{-1}=(\beta^{2}x)^{i}\{(\beta\alpha^{-1})^{n+1}z\}^{-1}=\beta^{2i-n-1}\alpha^{n+1}x^{i}z^{-1}=\beta^{2i-n-1}\alpha^{n+1}u_{i},

\displaystyle v_{i-1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(x^{\prime})^{1-i}z^{\prime}=(\beta^{2}x)^{1-i}(\beta\alpha^{-1})^{n+1}z=\beta^{n+3-2i}\alpha^{-n-1}x^{1-i}z=\beta^{n+3-2i}\alpha^{-n-1}v_{i-1},

which proves formula (
27
).
Since
E_{i}\cap W_{i+1}=\{u_{i+1}=0\}
,
E_{i+1}\cap W_{i+1}=\{v_{i}=0\}
and

p_{i}
has coordinates
(u_{i+1},v_{i})=(0,0)
for
0\leq i\leq n
, the remaining assertions
follow from formula (
27
).
\Box

Lemma 4.3

Let
n\geq 2
.
If
J\in N_{\operatorname{GL}}
is an anti-diagonal matrix of the form

J=\beta K\qquad\mbox{with}\quad K=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathrm{i}\gamma\\
\mathrm{i}\gamma^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix}\in N_{\operatorname{SL}},

(29)

where
\beta
,
\gamma\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, then the induced automorphism

f_{J}:X\to X
maps each
W_{i}
onto
W_{n+2-i}
, sending

(u_{i},v_{i-1})\mapsto(u_{n+2-i}^{\prime},v_{n+1-i}^{\prime}):=((\mathrm{i}\beta)^{n+3-2i}\gamma^{n+1}v_{i-1},\,(\mathrm{i}\beta)^{2i-n-1}\gamma^{-n-1}u_{i}),\quad 1\leq i\leq n+1.

(30)

It swaps
E_{i}\leftrightarrow E_{n+1-i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, and

p_{i}\leftrightarrow p_{n-i}
,
1\leq i\leq n-1
, so
\sigma_{J}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathrm{A}_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is an involution.

Proof
.
In view of formula (
12
) the matrix
J
induces an automorphism

S\to S,\quad(x,y,z)\mapsto(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime}):=((\mathrm{i}\beta)^{2}x,\,(\mathrm{i}\beta\gamma)^{n+1}z,\,(\mathrm{i}\beta\gamma^{-1})^{n+1}y).

(31)

The map
f_{J}
is then calculated as the composite of the following three maps

\begin{CD}(u_{i},v_{i-1})@>{\eqref{eqn:resol2-1a}}>{}>(x,z)@>{\eqref{eqn:f2b}}>{}>(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})@>{(\ref{eqn:resol2-3}^{\prime\prime})}>{}>(u_{n+2-i}^{\prime},v_{n+1-i}^{\prime}),\end{CD}

where
(\ref{eqn:resol2-3}^{\prime\prime})
is the map (
16
) with
(u_{i},v_{i-1})
and
(x,y)

replaced by
(u_{i}^{\prime},v_{i-1}^{\prime})
and
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
and then followed by the change of index
i\mapsto n+2-i
.
Explicitly we have

\displaystyle u_{n+2-i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(x^{\prime})^{(n+2-i)-n-1}y^{\prime}=\{(\mathrm{i}\beta)^{2}x\}^{1-i}(\mathrm{i}\beta\gamma)^{n+1}z=(\mathrm{i}\beta)^{n+3-2i}\gamma^{n+1}x^{1-i}z=(\mathrm{i}\beta)^{n+3-2i}\gamma^{n+1}v_{i-1},

\displaystyle v_{n+1-i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(x^{\prime})^{n+2-(n+2-i)}(y^{\prime})^{-1}=\{(\mathrm{i}\beta)^{2}x\}^{i}\{(\mathrm{i}\beta\gamma)^{n+1}z\}^{-1}=(\mathrm{i}\beta)^{2i-n-1}\gamma^{-n-1}x^{i}z^{-1}=(\mathrm{i}\beta)^{2i-n-1}\gamma^{-n-1}u_{i},

which proves formulas (
30
).
The remaining assertions follow from formula (
30
).
\Box

Figure 4.1:
The case of
n=2k
with
k\geq 1
.

Figure 4.2:
The case of
n=2k-1
with
k\geq 2
.

Corollary 4.4

In the situation of Lemma
4.3
the following assertions hold.

(\mathrm{1})

If
n=2k
with
k\geq 1
then
f_{J}
swaps
E_{k}\leftrightarrow E_{k+1}

while fixing the point
p_{k}
.
This is the unique fixed point of
f_{J}
on
E
.
The tangent map
(df_{J})_{p_{k}}:T_{p_{k}}X\to T_{p_{k}}X
has eigenvalues
\pm\mathrm{i}\beta
,
whose eigen-directions are shown by the dotted arrows in Figure
4.2
.
In particular we have
\det(df_{J})_{p_{k}}=\beta^{2}=\det J
.

(\mathrm{2})

If
n=2k-1
with
k\geq 2
then
f_{J}
swaps
(E_{k-1},p_{k-1})\leftrightarrow(E_{k+1},p_{k})
, preserves
E_{k}
, and has two fixed points
q_{\pm}
on
E_{k}
.
They are the only fiexd points of
f_{J}
on
E
.
At each of them the tangent map
(df)_{q_{\pm}}:T_{q_{\pm}}X\to T_{q_{\pm}}X
has
eigenvalue
-1
along
E_{k}
and eigenvalue
-\beta^{2}
in a normal direction to
E_{k}

as shown by the dotted arrows in Figure
4.2
.
In particular we have
\det(df_{J})_{q_{\pm}}=\beta^{2}=\det J
.

Proof
.
Assertion (1).
Formula (
30
) with
i=k+1
shows that
f_{J}
maps
W_{k+1}
onto itself,
sending
(u_{k+1},v_{k})\mapsto(u_{k+1}^{\prime},v_{k}^{\prime})
via

u_{k+1}^{\prime}=(\mathrm{i}\beta)\gamma^{2k+1}v_{k}
and
v_{k}^{\prime}=(\mathrm{i}\beta)\gamma^{-2k-1}u_{k+1}
.
This expression readily leads to the assertion, since

E_{k}=\{u_{k+1}=0\}
,
E_{k+1}=\{v_{k}=0\}
and
p_{k}
corresponds to
(u_{k+1},v_{k})=(0,0)
.

Assertion (2).
Formula (
30
) with
i=k
shows that
f_{J}
maps
W_{k}
onto
W_{k+1}
, sending

(u_{k},v_{k-1})\mapsto(u_{k+1}^{\prime},v_{k}^{\prime})
via
u_{k+1}^{\prime}=-\beta^{2}\gamma^{2k}v_{k-1}
and

v_{k}^{\prime}=\gamma^{-2k}u_{k}
.
Thus
f_{J}
swaps
(E_{k-1},p_{k-1})\leftrightarrow(E_{k+1},p_{k})
,
since
E_{k-1}=\{u_{k}=0\}
and
E_{k+1}=\{v_{k}=0\}
while
p_{k-1}
and
p_{k}

correspond to
(u_{k},v_{k-1})=(0,0)
and

(u_{k+1},v_{k})=(0,0)
, respectively.
In terms of the local coordinates
(u_{k},v_{k-1})
the map
f_{J}
is represented as

u_{k}^{\prime}=\gamma^{2k}u_{k}^{-1},\qquad v_{k-1}^{\prime}=-\beta^{2}\gamma^{-2k}\cdot u_{k}^{2}v_{k-1}.

Hence
f_{J}
preserves
E_{k}=\{v_{k-1}=0\}
and has two fixed points
q_{\pm}\in E_{k}

corresponding to
(u_{k},v_{k-1})=(\pm\gamma^{k},0)
.
In the current coordinates the tangent map
(df_{J})_{q_{\pm}}:T_{q_{\pm}}X\to T_{q_{\pm}}X
is
represented by the Jacobian matrix
\operatorname{diag}(-1,\,-\beta^{2})
.
From this observation the assertion about its eigenvalues follows immediately.

\Box

When
n=1
, we have
N_{\operatorname{GL}}=\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
by Theorem
2.9
.(1),
so
J\in N_{\operatorname{GL}}
is not always diagonalizable.
Whether
J
is diagonalizable or not can be judged in terms of a property of the
Möbius transformation
f_{J}|_{E}
on
E=E_{1}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
A Möbius transformation is said to be
parabolic
if it has only one fixed point.

Lemma 4.5

When
n=1
, a matrix
J\in N_{\operatorname{GL}}=\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
is not diagonalizable if and only if
f_{J}|_{E}
is parabolic.

Proof
.
Let
\beta\alpha^{\pm 1}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be the eigenvalues of
J
.
Up to conjugacy in
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
we may assume

J=\beta\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\alpha b_{1}\\[2.84526pt]
0&\alpha^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\mbox{so that}\qquad\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}^{\prime}\\[2.84526pt]
w_{2}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=J\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}\\[2.84526pt]
w_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\beta\begin{pmatrix}\alpha(w_{1}+b_{1}w_{2})\\[2.84526pt]
\alpha^{-1}w_{2}\end{pmatrix},

where
b_{1}\in\mathbb{C}
.
Equations (
12
) with
n=1
become
x=w_{1}w_{2}
,
y=w_{1}^{2}
,
z=w_{2}^{2}
.
Thus we have

x^{\prime}=w_{1}^{\prime}w_{2}^{\prime}=\beta^{2}(w_{1}w_{2}+b_{1}w_{2}^{2})=\beta^{2}(x+b_{1}z),\qquad z^{\prime}=(w_{2}^{\prime})^{2}=\beta^{2}\alpha^{-2}\,w_{2}^{2}=\beta^{2}\alpha^{-2}z.

Formula (
16
) with
i=1
reads
u_{1}=xz^{-1}
and
v_{0}=z
,
so the map
f_{J}
is calculated as

u_{1}^{\prime}=x^{\prime}(z^{\prime})^{-1}=\alpha^{2}(xz^{-1}+b_{1})=\alpha^{2}(u_{1}+b_{1}),\qquad v_{0}^{\prime}=z^{\prime}=\beta^{2}\alpha^{-2}\,z=\beta^{2}\alpha^{-2}\,v_{0},

(32)

in terms of the local coordinates
(u_{1},v_{0})
, where
E
is the closure of
\{v_{0}=0\}
.
Hence
J
is not diagonalizable, if and only if
\alpha^{2}=1
and
b_{1}\neq 0
, if and only if

f_{J}|_{E}
is a parabolic transformation
u_{1}\mapsto u_{1}+b_{1}
.
\Box

4.2
Non-Cyclic Cases

We proceed to the case where
G
is a binary polyhedral group as in §
3.2
.
The notation there is retained in this subsection.
Let
J\in N_{\operatorname{GL}}
and write
J=\beta K
with
\beta\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and
K\in N_{\operatorname{SL}}
as in Remark
4.1
.
The projective linear transformation
[J]=[K]
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
induced by
J
or
K
permutes the exceptional orbits

P^{\mathrm{e}}
,
P^{\mathrm{f}}
,
P^{\mathrm{v}}
, while every element of
\varGamma=G/\{\pm 1\}
leaves them invariant.
Thus the permutation of them depends only on the class
\langle K\rangle\in N_{\operatorname{SL}}/G
.
Upon passing to the resolution, the map
f_{J}:X\to X
restricts to a projective linear transformation on

E_{0}
permuting the three points
p_{0}^{\mathrm{e}}
,
p_{0}^{\mathrm{f}}
,
p_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\in E_{0}
; see also (
25
).
The class
\langle K\rangle
, the permutation of
P^{\mathrm{e}}
,
P^{\mathrm{f}}
,
P^{\mathrm{v}}
and that of

p_{0}^{\mathrm{e}}
,
p_{0}^{\mathrm{f}}
,
p_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}
are all identified with the Dynkin automorphism
\sigma_{J}\in\operatorname{Aut}(D)
.
The map
f_{J}
depends only on
\beta\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and
\sigma_{J}=\langle K\rangle\in\operatorname{Aut}(D)=N_{\operatorname{SL}}/G
,
so we can take a various representative
K\in N_{\operatorname{SL}}
of
\sigma_{J}
without changing the map
f_{J}
.
The order of
\sigma_{J}
is
1
,
2
, or
3
, and each case is discussed separately.

Lemma 4.6

Suppose that
\sigma_{J}
is the unit element in
\operatorname{Aut}(D)
.
Then the map
f_{J}
preserves each of
E^{\mathrm{e}}
,
E^{\mathrm{f}}
,
E^{\mathrm{v}}
and fixes
E_{0}
pointwise.
For each
\nu=\mathrm{e},\mathrm{f},\mathrm{v}
and
0\leq i\leq n^{\nu}
, the point
p_{i}^{\nu}
is a fixed point of
f_{J}

having multipliers
(\det J)^{i+1}
along
E_{i+1}^{\nu}
and
(\det J)^{-i}
along
E_{i}^{\nu}
.

Proof
.
Since
\sigma_{J}=\langle K\rangle
is the unit element in
\operatorname{Aut}(D)=N_{\operatorname{SL}}/G
, we have

J=\beta K
with
K\in G
and hence we can take
K=I
as a representative of the class
\sigma_{J}
, so that
J=\beta I
.
For each
\nu=\mathrm{e},\mathrm{f},\mathrm{v}
, take a point
q^{\nu}\in P^{\nu}
.
Since
q^{\nu}
is fixed by
[\beta I]\in\operatorname{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, Lemma
3.2
is available with

q=q^{\nu}
and
\alpha=1
in formula (
22
).
This enables us to apply Lemma
4.2
with
\alpha=\beta
to the resolution
X^{\nu}\to(T^{*}U^{\nu})/\varGamma^{\nu}

of a cyclic quotient singularity of order
n^{\nu}+1
.
The result is that
f_{J}
preserves
E^{\nu}
and its multipliers at the fixed points
p_{i}^{\nu}\in E^{\nu}
,

0\leq i\leq n^{\nu}
, are
\beta^{2(i+1)}=(\det J)^{i+1}
along
E_{i+1}^{\nu}
and

\beta^{-2i}=(\det J)^{-i}
along
E_{i}^{\nu}
.
The map
f_{J}
fixes
p_{0}^{\mathrm{e}}
,
p_{0}^{\mathrm{f}}
,
p_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}
and hence fixes the entire

E_{0}
pointwise, because a projective linear transformation fixing three points is the identity.

\Box

Lemma 4.7

Suppose that
\sigma_{J}
is of order
2
in
\operatorname{Aut}(D)
.
Then
D
must be of type
\mathrm{D}
or
\mathrm{E}_{6}
and the map
f_{J}
preserves just
one of
E^{\mathrm{e}}
,
E^{\mathrm{f}}
,
E^{\mathrm{v}}
, say
E^{\nu}
, and swaps the remaining two.
For each
0\leq i\leq n^{\nu}
the point
p_{i}^{\nu}
is a fixed pont of
f_{J}
having
multipliers
-(\det J)^{i+1}
along
E_{i+1}^{\nu}
and
-(\det J)^{-i}
along
E_{i}^{\nu}
.
Moreover
f_{J}
fixes a unique point
p_{\infty}
on
E_{0}\setminus\{p_{0}^{\mathrm{e}},p_{0}^{\mathrm{f}},p_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\}
,
at which
f_{J}
has multipliers
-1
along
E_{0}
and
-(\det J)
along
T_{p_{\infty}}^{*}E_{0}
.

Proof
.
(1) Case of type
\mathrm{D}_{n}
with
n\geq 5
.
In this case
G
is a binary dihedral group of order
4(n-2)
and
N_{\operatorname{SL}}
is the unique
binary dihedral group
H
that contains
G
as an index
2
subgroup.
Notice that
P^{\mathrm{f}}
is special to the effect that
n^{\mathrm{f}}\neq n^{\mathrm{e}}=n^{\mathrm{v}}
(see Table
3.1
).
Let
Q^{\mathrm{e}}
,
Q^{\mathrm{f}}
,
Q^{\mathrm{v}}
denote the exceptional orbits for the dihedral group
\varDelta:=H/\{\pm I\}
.
Then
Q^{\mathrm{f}}=P^{\mathrm{f}}
and
Q^{\mathrm{v}}=P^{\mathrm{e}}\sqcup P^{\mathrm{v}}
, while

Q^{\mathrm{e}}
is a non-exceptional orbit of
\varGamma=G/\{\pm I\}
.

(1a) Take a point
q^{\mathrm{f}}\in Q^{\mathrm{f}}=P^{\mathrm{f}}
.
Its isotropy subgroup in
\varDelta
is a cyclic group of order
2(n^{\mathrm{f}}+1)
,
whose binary lift is a cyclic subgroup of order
4(n^{\mathrm{f}}+1)
in
H
.
Let
K^{\mathrm{f}}
be a generator of the latter group.
Then
[K^{\mathrm{f}}]\in\operatorname{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
swaps
P^{\mathrm{e}}
and
P^{\mathrm{v}}
, so
K^{\mathrm{f}}
lies in
H\setminus G

and represents the nontrivial class
\sigma_{J}\in H/G\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
We may take
K^{\mathrm{f}}
as a representative of
\sigma_{J}
, so that
J=\beta K^{\mathrm{f}}
.
The eigenvalues of
K^{\mathrm{f}}
are a primitive
4(n^{\mathrm{f}}+1)
-st root of unity, say
\zeta
, and its
reciprocal
\zeta^{-1}
.
Since
q^{\mathrm{f}}
is fixed by
[\beta K^{\mathrm{f}}]\in\operatorname{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, Lemma
3.2
is available with

q=q^{\mathrm{f}}
and
\alpha=\zeta
in formula (
22
).
This enables us to apply Lemma
4.2
with
\alpha=\beta\zeta^{2}
to the resolution

X^{\mathrm{f}}\to(T^{*}U^{\mathrm{f}})/\varGamma^{\mathrm{f}}
of a cyclic quotient singularity of order
n^{\mathrm{f}}+1
.
The result is that
f_{J}
preserves
E^{\mathrm{f}}
and has multipliers
\beta^{2(i+1)}\zeta^{2(n^{\mathrm{f}}+1)}=-\beta^{2(i+1)}=-(\det J)^{i+1}
along
E_{i+1}^{\mathrm{f}}
and
\beta^{-2i}\zeta^{-n^{\mathrm{f}}-1}=-\beta^{-2i}=(\det J)^{-i}
along
E_{i}^{\mathrm{f}}
at the fixed points
p_{i}^{\mathrm{f}}\in E^{\mathrm{f}}
,
0\leq i\leq n^{\mathrm{f}}
.
Moreover
f_{J}
swaps
E^{\mathrm{e}}\leftrightarrow E^{\mathrm{v}}
as
[\beta K^{\mathrm{f}}]
swaps
P^{\mathrm{e}}\leftrightarrow P^{\mathrm{v}}
.

(1b) Take a point
q^{\mathrm{e}}\in Q^{\mathrm{e}}
.
Its isotropy subgroup in
\varDelta
is a group of order
2
,
whose binary lift is a cyclic subgroup of order
4
in
H
.
Let
K^{\mathrm{e}}
be a generator of the latter group.
Then
[K^{\mathrm{e}}]\in\operatorname{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
swaps
P^{\mathrm{e}}\leftrightarrow P^{\mathrm{v}}
, so
K^{\mathrm{e}}
lies in
H\setminus G

and represents the nontrivial class
\sigma_{J}\in H/G\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
We may take
K^{\mathrm{e}}
as an alternative representative of
\sigma_{J}
, so that
J=\beta K^{\mathrm{e}}
.
The eigenvalues of
K^{\mathrm{e}}
are
\pm\mathrm{i}
.
Since
q^{\mathrm{e}}
is fixed by
[\beta K^{\mathrm{e}}]\in\operatorname{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, Lemma
3.2
is available with

q=q^{\mathrm{e}}
and
\alpha=\mathrm{i}
in formula (
22
).
It implies that the multipliers of
\beta K^{\mathrm{e}}
at
q^{\mathrm{e}}
are
-1
along the base space
\mathbb{P}^{1}
and

-\beta^{2}=-(\det J)
along the fiber
T^{*}_{q^{\mathrm{e}}}\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
Let
p_{\infty}
be the image of
Q^{\mathrm{e}}
under the quotient map
\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus(P^{\mathrm{e}}\cup P^{\mathrm{f}}\cup P^{\mathrm{v}})\to\mathbf{P}^{1}\setminus\{p^{\mathrm{e}},p^{\mathrm{f}},p^{\mathrm{v}}\}
.
Since the map (
23
) is a local biholomorphism, we observe that
p_{\infty}
is a fixed point
of
f_{J}
in
E_{0}\setminus\{p_{0}^{\mathrm{e}},p_{0}^{\mathrm{f}},p_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\}
having multipliers

-1
along
E_{0}
and
-(\det J)
along
T^{*}_{p_{\infty}}E_{0}
.

(2) Case of type
\mathrm{E}_{6}
.
In this case
G
is a binary tetrahedral group and
N_{\operatorname{SL}}
is the unique binary octahedral group
H

that contains
G
as an index
2
subgroup.
Notice that
P^{\mathrm{e}}
is special to the effect that
n^{\mathrm{e}}\neq n^{\mathrm{f}}=n^{\mathrm{v}}
(see Table
3.1
).
Let
Q^{\mathrm{e}}
,
Q^{\mathrm{f}}
,
Q^{\mathrm{v}}
denote the exceptional orbits for the octahedral group

\varDelta:=H/\{\pm I\}
.
Then
Q^{\mathrm{v}}=P^{\mathrm{e}}
and
Q^{\mathrm{f}}=P^{\mathrm{f}}\sqcup P^{\mathrm{v}}
, while

Q^{\mathrm{e}}
is a non-exceptional orbit of
\varGamma=G/\{\pm I\}
.
Take a point
q^{\mathrm{v}}\in Q^{\mathrm{v}}=P^{\mathrm{e}}
.
Its isotropy subgroup in
\varDelta
is a cyclic group of order
4
, whose binary lift is a cyclic subgroup of order
8
in
H
.
Let
K^{\mathrm{v}}
be a generator of the latter group.
We can take
K^{\mathrm{v}}
as a representative of the nontrivial class
\sigma_{J}\in H/G\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, so
J=\beta K^{\mathrm{e}}
.
As in (1a) we can show that
f_{J}
preserves
E^{\mathrm{e}}
and has multipliers
-(\det J)^{i+1}
along

E_{i+1}^{\mathrm{e}}
and
-(\det J)^{-i}
along
E_{i}^{\mathrm{e}}
at the fixed points
p_{i}^{\mathrm{e}}\in E^{\mathrm{e}}
,
0\leq i\leq n^{\mathrm{e}}=1
.
Moreover
f_{J}
swaps
E^{\mathrm{f}}
and
E^{\mathrm{v}}
.
Next, take a point
q^{\mathrm{e}}\in Q^{\mathrm{e}}
.
Its isotropy subgroup in
\varDelta
is a group of order
2
, whose binary lift is a cyclic subgroup of order
4
in
H
.
Let
K^{\mathrm{e}}
be a generator of the latter group.
We can take
K^{\mathrm{e}}
as an alternative representative of
\sigma_{J}
, so
J=\beta K^{\mathrm{e}}
.
Let
p_{\infty}
be the image of
Q^{\mathrm{e}}
under the quotient map
\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus(P^{\mathrm{e}}\cup P^{\mathrm{f}}\cup P^{\mathrm{v}})\to\mathbf{P}^{1}\setminus\{p^{\mathrm{e}},p^{\mathrm{f}},p^{\mathrm{v}}\}
.
As in (1b) we can show that
p_{\infty}
is a fixed point of
f_{J}
in
E_{0}\setminus\{p_{0}^{\mathrm{e}},p_{0}^{\mathrm{f}},p_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\}

having multipliers
-1
along the base
E_{0}
and
-(\det J)
along the fiber
T^{*}_{p_{\infty}}E_{0}
.

(3) Case of type
\mathrm{D}_{4}
.
In this case
G
is a quaternion group and
N_{\operatorname{SL}}
is the unique binary octahedral group
H

that contains
G
as a normal subgroup.
Notice that
P^{\mathrm{e}}
,
P^{\mathrm{f}}
,
P^{\mathrm{v}}
are “even” as
n^{\mathrm{e}}=n^{\mathrm{f}}=n^{\mathrm{v}}
(see Table
3.1
).
Let
Q^{\mathrm{e}}
,
Q^{\mathrm{f}}
,
Q^{\mathrm{v}}
be the exceptional orbits for the octahedral group

\varDelta:=H/\{\pm I\}
.
Then
Q^{\mathrm{v}}=P^{\mathrm{e}}\sqcup P^{\mathrm{f}}\sqcup P^{\mathrm{v}}
, while
Q^{\mathrm{e}}
is the disjoint union of three
non-exceptional orbits
Q^{\mathrm{e}}_{\mathrm{e}}
,
Q^{\mathrm{e}}_{\mathrm{f}}
,
Q^{\mathrm{e}}_{\mathrm{v}}
of the Klein
4
-group

\varGamma=G/\{\pm I\}
.
Corresponding to this decomposition there are three dihedral groups
\varDelta^{\mathrm{e}}
,
\varDelta^{\mathrm{f}}
,
\varDelta^{\mathrm{v}}
in

\varDelta
that contain
\varGamma
as an index
2
subgroup, where
\varDelta^{\mathrm{e}}
preserves
P^{\mathrm{e}}
and swaps

P^{\mathrm{f}}\leftrightarrow P^{\mathrm{v}}
, and so on.
For
\nu=\mathrm{e},\mathrm{f},\mathrm{v}
, the binary lift
H^{\nu}
of
\varDelta^{\nu}
is a binary dihedral group in

H
that contains
G
as an index
2
subgroup.
Given
J=\beta K
with
\sigma_{J}=\langle K\rangle
of order
2
, we have
K\in H^{\nu}\setminus G

for a unique
\nu=\mathrm{e},\mathrm{f},\mathrm{v}
.
Our claim can then be proved by the same argument as that in case (1) with
H^{\nu}
in place of
H
.
Meanwhile,
Q^{\mathrm{f}}
is the disjoint union of two non-exceptional
\varGamma
-orbits
Q^{\mathrm{f}}_{\pm}
, which are
antipodal to each other on the octahedron.
Discussion about them is left to Lemma
4.8
.
\Box

Lemma 4.8

Suppose that
\sigma_{J}
is of order
3
in
\operatorname{Aut}(D)
.
Then
D
must be of type
\mathrm{D}_{4}
and the map
f_{J}
permutes
E^{\mathrm{e}}
,
E^{\mathrm{f}}
,
E^{\mathrm{v}}
cyclically.
Moreover
f_{J}
fixes exactly two points
p_{\pm}
on
E
, which lie in
E_{0}\setminus\{p_{0}^{\mathrm{e}},p_{0}^{\mathrm{f}},p_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\}
and at which
f_{J}
has multipliers

\omega^{\pm 1}
along
E_{0}
and
\omega^{\mp 1}(\det J)
along

T_{p_{\pm}}^{*}E_{0}
, where
\omega
is a primitive cube root of unity.

Proof
.
It is clear from Table
4.1
that
D
must be of type
\mathrm{D}_{4}
and
G
is a quaternion group.
We are in the situation of case (3) in the proof of Lemma
4.7
.
Take a pair of antipodal points
q^{\mathrm{f}}_{\pm}\in Q^{\mathrm{f}}_{\pm}
.
Their (common) isotropy subgroup in
\varDelta
is a cyclic group of order
3
, whose binary lift is a cyclic
group of order
6
.
A generator
K^{\mathrm{f}}
of the latter group is a representative of the given class
\sigma_{J}\in H/G\cong S_{3}

of order
3
, so that
J=\beta K^{\mathrm{f}}
.
Since
K^{\mathrm{f}}
has eigenvalues
\omega^{\mp 1/2}
and
[K^{\mathrm{f}}]\in\operatorname{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
fixes
q^{\mathrm{f}}_{\pm}
,
Lemma
3.2
is available with
q=q^{\mathrm{f}}_{\pm}
and
\alpha=\omega^{\mp 1/2}
in formula (
22
).
It implies that the multipliers of
\beta K^{\mathrm{e}}
at
q^{\mathrm{f}}_{\pm}
are
\omega^{\pm 1}
along

\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
\omega^{\mp 1}\beta^{2}=\omega^{\mp 1}(\det J)
along
T^{*}_{q^{\mathrm{f}}_{\pm}}\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
Let
p_{\pm}
be the image of
Q^{\mathrm{f}}_{\pm}
under the quotient map
\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus(P^{\mathrm{e}}\cup P^{\mathrm{f}}\cup P^{\mathrm{v}})\to\mathbf{P}^{1}\setminus\{p^{\mathrm{e}},p^{\mathrm{f}},p^{\mathrm{v}}\}
.
Since the map (
23
) is a local biholomorphism,
p_{\pm}
are fixed points
of
f_{J}
in
E_{0}\setminus\{p_{0}^{\mathrm{e}},p_{0}^{\mathrm{f}},p_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\}
having multipliers

\omega^{\pm 1}
along
E_{0}
and
\omega^{\mp 1}(\det J)
along the fiber
T^{*}_{p_{\pm}}E_{0}
.
Since
[K^{\mathrm{f}}]\in\operatorname{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
permutes
P^{\mathrm{e}}
,
P^{\mathrm{f}}
,
P^{\mathrm{v}}
cyclically,
f_{J}
permutes

E^{\mathrm{e}}
,
E^{\mathrm{f}}
,
E^{\mathrm{v}}
cyclically.

\Box

5
Linear Models near Exceptional Components

The purpose of this section is to introduce linear model maps around the
exceptional set of a minimal resolution of a Kleinian singularity.
They are used in §
9
to detect rotation domains around
exceptional components.
Let
G
be a nontrivial finite subgroup of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
.
The orbit space
S:=\mathbb{C}^{2}/G
is realized by an algebraic surface

S=\{x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\mid u(x)=0\}\qquad\mbox{with Poincar\'{e} residue}\quad\eta_{S}:=\left.(dx/du)\right|_{S},

where
u(x)
is a certain polynomial of
x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
and
dx:=dx_{1}\wedge dx_{2}\wedge dx_{3}
.
The standard
2
-form
\eta_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}:=dw_{1}\wedge dw_{2}
on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
descends to
\eta_{S}

(up to a nonzero constant multiple) via the quotient map
\mathbb{C}^{2}\to S=\mathbb{C}^{2}/G
off the singular point
p\in S
.
Let
(X,E)\to(S,p)
be a minimal resolution of
(S,p)
with exceptional set
E
.
Then
\eta_{S}
lifts to a nonwhere vanishing holomorphic
2
-form
\eta_{X}
on
(X,E)
,
which we call the
satandard
2
-form
on it.

Let
J\in N_{\operatorname{GL}}
be a normalizer of
G
in
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
As indicated in the diagram

\begin{CD}J\curvearrowright(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)+\eta_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}f_{J}\curvearrowright(X,E)+\eta_{X}\\
@V{\mbox{quotient by $G$}}V{}V@V{}V{\mbox{minimal resolution}}V\\
(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)/G=(S,p)+\eta_{S},\end{CD}

(33)

the linear action of
J
on
(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)
descends to
(S,p)
and then lifts to
an automorphism
f_{J}
on
(X,E)
, whose structure was investigated in §§
4.1
–
4.2
.
The twist relation
J^{*}\eta_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}=(\det J)\,\eta_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}
transfers to

f_{J}^{*}\eta_{X}=(\det J)\,\eta_{X}
.
We refer to
f_{J}
as the
linear model
associated with the normalizer
J\in N_{\operatorname{GL}}
.

The other way around, we start with a nonlinear biholomorphism
f:(X,E)\to(X,E)

with twist relation
f^{*}\eta_{X}=\delta\,\eta_{X}
for a complex constant
\delta=\delta(f)
of modulus
1
.
This map descends to
(S,p)
and then lift to a
G
-equivariant nonlinear biholomorphism

F:(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)\to(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)
through the diagram

\begin{CD}f\curvearrowright(X,E)+\eta_{X}F\curvearrowright(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)+\eta_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\\
@V{\mbox{collapsing $E$}}V{}V@V{}V{\mbox{lift by $G$}}V\\
(S,p)+\eta_{S}=(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)/G.\end{CD}

(34)

Let
J:=F^{\prime}(0)\in N_{\operatorname{GL}}
be the Jacobian matrix of
F
at the origin.
Then the map
f_{J}
is referred to as the
linear model
of
f
around
E
.
We then have
\det J=\delta
,
F^{*}\eta_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}=\delta\,\eta_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}

and
f_{J}^{*}\eta_{X}=\delta\,\eta_{X}
, so
f
and
f_{J}
have the same twist number.
The map
f
permutes the
(-2)
-curves in
E
and induces an automorphism

\sigma(f)\in\operatorname{Aut}(D)
of the Dynkin diagram
D
of
E
, just as
f_{J}
induces

\sigma_{J}\in\operatorname{Aut}(D)
.

We are interested in how
f
and
f_{J}
are different from each other on
E
.
It is measured by the mappings

h:=f_{J}^{-1}\circ f:(X,E)\to(X,E),\qquad H:=J^{-1}\circ F:(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)\to(\mathbb{C}^{2},0).

(35)

which pass to each other through diagram (
34
).
It follows from
J:=F^{\prime}(0)
and formula (
8
) that

H^{\prime}(0)=I,\qquad H\circ g=g\circ H\qquad\mbox{for any}\quad g\in G.

(36)

It is possible to determine the map
h|_{E}
from a bit of information about
H
.
First we consider the case where
G
is a cyclic group of order
n+1
as
in §
3.1
, using the notation there.
Recall that
u_{i}
(resp.
v_{i}
) is an inhomogeneous coordinate on

E_{i}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
around the point
p_{i-1}
(resp.
p_{i}
) for
i=1,\dots,n
.
We write
H=(H_{1}(w),H_{2}(w))
in terms of the standard coordinate system
w=(w_{1},w_{2})
on
(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)
.
The following proposition shows that there is a subtle but not negligible
difference between
f|_{E}
and
f_{J}|_{E}
, although they are almost the same.

Proposition 5.1

Let
G
be the cyclic group of order
n+1
generated by the matrix

g=\operatorname{diag}(\zeta^{-1},\zeta)
, where
\zeta:=\exp(2\pi\mathrm{i}/(n+1))
.
For
n=1
tha map
h|_{E}
is the identity on
E=E_{1}
.
For
n\geq 2
it is described as follows:

(1)

h|_{E_{1}}
maps
E_{1}
onto itself by
u_{1}\mapsto u_{1}+b_{1}
or equivalently by

v_{1}\mapsto v_{1}/(1+b_{1}v_{1})
, where
b_{1}
is the coefficient of
w_{2}^{n}
in the Taylor series
expansion of
H_{1}(w)
at the origin
w=0
.

(i)

If
n\geq 3
, then
h|_{E_{i}}
is the identity mapping on
E_{i}
for each
i=2,\dots,n-1
.

(n)

h|_{E_{n}}
maps
E_{n}
onto itself by
v_{n}\mapsto v_{n}+b_{2}
or equivalently by

u_{n}\mapsto u_{n}/(1+b_{2}u_{n})
, where
b_{2}
is the coefficient of
w_{1}^{n}
in the Taylor series
expansion of
H_{2}(w)
at the origin
w=0
.

In particular
f
and
f_{J}
induce the same Dynkin automorphism, that is,
\sigma(f)=\sigma_{J}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathrm{A}_{n})
.

Proof
.
First we discuss the main case of
n\geq 2
.
Forced by the constraints in (
36
), the Taylor series expansions of

w_{1}^{\prime}=H_{1}(w)
and
w_{2}^{\prime}=H_{2}(w)
at
w=0
must be of the forms:

\displaystyle w_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=w_{1}\left\{\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{jk}^{(11)}\,(w_{1}w_{2})^{j}w_{1}^{k(n+1)}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{jk}^{(12)}\,(w_{1}w_{2})^{j}w_{2}^{k(n+1)}+\frac{w_{2}^{n}}{w_{1}}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}b_{j}^{(1)}\,w_{2}^{j(n+1)}\right\}

\displaystyle=w_{1}\left\{\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{jk}^{(11)}\,x^{j}y^{k}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{jk}^{(12)}\,x^{j}z^{k}+\frac{z}{x}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}b_{j}^{(1)}\,z^{j}\right\},\qquad a_{00}^{(11)}=1,\quad b_{0}^{(1)}=b_{1};

\displaystyle w_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=w_{2}\left\{\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{jk}^{(21)}\,(w_{1}w_{2})^{j}w_{1}^{k(n+1)}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{jk}^{(22)}\,(w_{1}w_{2})^{j}w_{2}^{k(n+1)}+\frac{w_{1}^{n}}{w_{2}}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}b_{j}^{(2)}\,w_{1}^{j(n+1)}\right\}

\displaystyle=w_{2}\left\{\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{jk}^{(21)}\,x^{j}y^{k}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{jk}^{(22)}\,x^{j}z^{k}+\frac{y}{x}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}b_{j}^{(2)}\,y^{j}\right\},\qquad a_{00}^{(22)}=1,\quad b_{0}^{(2)}=b_{2},

where formula (
12
) is used in the second and fourth lines.
In other words, we have

w_{1}^{\prime}=w_{1}\left\{A_{1}(x,y,z)+(z/x)\,B_{1}(z)\right\},\qquad w_{2}^{\prime}=w_{2}\left\{A_{2}(x,y,z)+(y/x)\,B_{2}(y)\right\},

(37)

where
A_{1}=A_{1}(x,y,z)
,
A_{2}=A_{2}(x,y,z)
,
B_{1}=B_{1}(z)
and
B_{2}=B_{2}(y)
are
convergent power series in the indicated variables such that

A_{1}(0,0,0)=A_{2}(0,0,0)=1
,
B_{1}(0)=b_{1}
and
B_{2}(0)=b_{2}
.
Regarding
x
,
y
,
z
as holomorphic functions on
(X,E)
, we see that

A_{1}
,
A_{2}
,
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
are holomorphic functions on
(X,E)
such that

A_{1}\equiv 1,\qquad A_{2}\equiv 1,\qquad B_{1}\equiv b_{1},\qquad B_{2}\equiv b_{2}\qquad\mbox{on}\quad E.

(38)

Let
S\to S
,
(x,y,z)\mapsto(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime})
be the map induced from
H:w\mapsto w^{\prime}
.
It follows from (
12
) and (
37
) that

\displaystyle x^{\prime}

\displaystyle=x\left\{A_{1}A_{2}+(y/x)\,A_{1}B_{2}+(z/x)\,A_{2}B_{1}+x^{n-1}\,B_{1}B_{2}\right\},

\displaystyle y^{\prime}

\displaystyle=y\left\{A_{1}+(z/x)\,B_{1}\right\}^{n+1},\qquad z^{\prime}=z\left\{A_{2}+(y/x)\,B_{2}\right\}^{n+1}.

Substituting these equations into the prime version of formula (
16
) and
using formula (
15
), we have

u_{i}^{\prime}=(x^{\prime})^{i}(z^{\prime})^{-1}=u_{i}\,C^{i}\,D^{-n-1},\qquad v_{i-1}^{\prime}=(x^{\prime})^{1-i}z^{\prime}=v_{i-1}\,C^{1-i}\,D^{n+1},\qquad 1\leq i\leq n+1,

(39)

where the functions
C=C(u_{i},v_{i-1})
and
D=D(u_{i},v_{i-1})
are defined by

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle:=A_{1}A_{2}+u_{i}^{n-i+1}v_{i-1}^{n-i}\,A_{1}B_{2}+u_{i}^{i-2}v_{i-1}^{i-1}\,A_{2}B_{1}+u_{i}^{n-1}v_{i-1}^{n-1}\,B_{1}B_{2},

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle:=A_{2}+u_{i}^{n-i+1}v_{i-1}^{n-i}B_{2}.

Putting
v_{i-1}=0
in the first formula of (
39
) and taking (
38
) into account,
we observe the following:
For
n\geq 2
and
i=1
we have
u_{1}^{\prime}=u_{1}+b_{1}
on
E_{1}
;
for
n\geq 3
and
2\leq i\leq n-1
we have
u_{i}^{\prime}=u_{i}
on
E_{i}
;
for
i=n\geq 2
we have
u_{n}^{\prime}=u_{n}/(1+b_{2}u_{n})
on
E_{n}
.
In a similar manner, putting
u_{i}=0
in the second formula of (
39
) and taking (
38
)
into account, we observe the following:
For
n\geq 2
and
i=2
we have
v_{1}^{\prime}=v_{1}/(1+b_{1}v_{1})
on
E_{1}
;
for
n\geq 3
and
3\leq i\leq n
we have
v_{i-1}^{\prime}=v_{i-1}
on
E_{i-1}
;
for
n\geq 2
and
i=n+1
we have
v_{n}^{\prime}=v_{n}+b_{2}
on
E_{n}
.

Next we consider the case
n=1
.
Formula (
12
) now reads
x=w_{1}w_{2}
,
y=w_{1}^{2}
,
z=w_{2}^{2}

and (
37
) turns into

\displaystyle w_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=w_{1}\{A_{1}(x,y,z)+(w_{2}^{3}/w_{1})\,B_{1}(w_{2}^{2})\}=w_{1}\{A_{1}(x,y,z)+(z^{2}/x)\,B_{1}(z)\},\quad

\displaystyle A_{1}(0,0,0)

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle w_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=w_{1}\{A_{2}(x,y,z)+(w_{1}^{3}/w_{2})B_{1}(w_{1}^{2})\}=w_{1}\{A_{2}(x,y,z)+(y^{2}/x)\,B_{2}(y)\},\quad

\displaystyle A_{2}(0,0,0)

\displaystyle=1.

where
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
,
B_{1}
,
B_{2}
are convergent power series in the indicated variables.
A chief difference from formula (
37
) lies in the terms
z^{2}/x
and
y^{2}/x
in front
of
B_{1}(z)
and
B_{2}(y)
; the values of
B_{1}(0)
and
B_{2}(0)
are not relevant here.
We have
u_{1}=xz^{-1}
and
v_{0}=z
from formula (
16
).
Proceeding as in the case of
n\geq 2
, we obtain
x^{\prime}=x\,C
and
z^{\prime}=z\,D^{2}
, hence

u_{1}^{\prime}=x^{\prime}(z^{\prime})^{-1}=xz^{-1}\,CD^{-2}=u_{1}\,CD^{-1}
and
v_{0}^{\prime}=z^{\prime}=zD^{2}=v_{0}D^{2}
,
where

C:=A_{1}A_{2}+u_{1}^{3}v_{0}\,A_{1}B_{2}+u_{1}^{-1}v_{0}\,A_{2}B_{1}+u_{1}^{2}v_{0}^{2}\,B_{1}B_{2},\qquad D:=A_{2}+u_{1}^{3}v_{0}\,B_{2}.

Putting
v_{0}=0
, we have
u_{1}^{\prime}=u_{1}
on
E
, that is,
h|_{E}
is the identity transformation.

\Box

Remark 5.2

When
G
is a non-cyclic finite subgroup of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
, the construction of a minimal resolution in
§
3.2
and Proposition
5.1
applied to each arm
E^{\nu}
in Figure
3.1

show that the map
h|_{E}
fixes each
(-2)
-curve in
E
pointwise except for the terminal
curves
E_{n^{\nu}}^{\nu}
,
\nu=\mathrm{e},\mathrm{f},\mathrm{v}
, on which
h|_{E}
behaves as

v_{n^{\nu}}^{\nu}\mapsto v_{n^{\nu}}^{\nu}+b^{\nu}
in terms of the inhomogeneous
coordinate
v_{n^{\nu}}^{\nu}
around the point
p_{n^{\nu}}^{\nu}\in E_{n^{\nu}}^{\nu}
,
where
b^{\nu}\in\mathbb{C}
is a suitable constant.
The formal proof of this fact is omitted, because it is not used in what follows.

We say that
f
is
equivariantly linearizable to its linear model

f_{J}
, if the map
F

in diagram (
34
) is
G
-equivariantly linearizable to
J
around
0
.
In this case
f
is biholomorphically conjugate to
f_{J}
in a neighborhood of
E
,
so
f
and
f_{J}
exhibit the same dynamics there.
By definition, the
multipliers of

f

at

E
are those of
F
at
0
,
that is, the eigenvalues of
J
.
When
f
is understood, they are also referred to as the
multipliers of

E
.
It is natural to ask when
f
is equivariantly linearizable to its linear model.
We give a partial answer to this question.

Proposition 5.3

Let
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(\beta\alpha,\beta\alpha^{-1})

be the multipliers of
E
.
If
\alpha
is a root of unity while
\beta
is an algebraic number of modulus
1
,
but not a root of unity, then
f
is equivariantly linearizable to its linear model.

Proof
.
First we show that
\alpha
is NR.
Suppose the contrary that
\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{1}^{n_{1}}\alpha_{2}^{n_{2}}=0

holds for some
(n_{1},n_{2})\in N
and
j=(3\mp 1)/2
,
where the set
N
is defined at the beginning of §
2
.
Then we have
\beta^{n_{1}+n_{2}-1}=\alpha^{n_{2}-n_{1}\pm 1}
,
hence
\beta^{k(n_{1}+n_{2}-1)}=\alpha^{k(n_{2}-n_{1}\pm 1)}=1
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
This forces
n_{1}+n_{2}-1=0
, which contradicts
(n_{1},n_{2})\in N
.
Since
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
are algebraic numbers,

\alpha
is Diophantine by Theorem
2.2
.
Theorem
2.4
then concludes that
F
is
G
-equivariantly
linearizable to
J=F^{\prime}(0)
, that is,
f
is equivariantly linearizable to its linear model
f_{J}
.

\Box

There are important cases to which Proposition
5.3
is applicable.

Theorem 5.4

Let
(X,E)
be a minimal resolution of a Kleinian singularity with exceptional set
E

and let
f:(X,E)\to(X,E)
be an automorphism with twist number
\delta
of modulus
1
.
Suppose that
\delta
is an algebraic number which is not a root of unity.
Then
f
is equvariantly linearizable to its linear model
f_{J}
, if either

(\mathrm{i})

E
is of Dynkin type
\mathrm{A}_{n}
,
n\geq 2
, and
\sigma(f)\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathrm{A}_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is nontrivial; or

(\mathrm{ii})

E
is of Dynkin type
\mathrm{D}
or
\mathrm{E}
, in which case
\sigma(f)
may be arbitrary,

Proof
.
Let
\beta\alpha^{\pm 1}
be the multipliers of
E
, that is, the eigenvalues of
J
, where
\beta=\delta^{1/2}
.
In view of Proposition
5.3
, it suffices to show that
\alpha
is a root of unity.
In case (i), by Proposition
5.1
we have
\sigma(f)=\sigma_{J}
, so a comparison
of Lemmas
4.2
and
4.3
shows that
J
must be of the form (
29
),

not
of the form (
26
).
This observation tells us that
\alpha=\mathrm{i}
.
In case
(\mathrm{ii})
we have
J=\delta^{1/2}K
with
K\in N_{\operatorname{SL}}
, where
N_{\operatorname{SL}}
is a finite
subgroup of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
by Theorem
2.9
.
So the eigenvalues
\alpha^{\pm 1}
of
K
are roots of unity and those of
J
are
\beta\alpha^{\pm 1}

with
\beta=\delta^{1/2}
.

\Box

Theorem
5.4
relies only on a
semi-local
property of the map
f

in a neighborhood of the exceptional set
E
.
In order to get similar results when
E
is of Dynkin type
\mathrm{A}
and
\sigma(f)
is trivial, however,
we need more elaborate machinery involving
global geometry
of the map
f:X\to X
.
This is one of the main issues in this article, to which a large part of
§§
6
–
9
is devoted.

6
Salem Numbers and Multipliers at Periodic Points

Let
f:X\to X
be an automorphism of a complex surface
X
.
Given a positive integer
m
, let
p\in X
be a periodic point of
f
with period

m
, that is, a fixed point of the
m
-th iterate
f^{m}
.
The eigenvalues
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
of the holomorphic tangent map

(df^{m})_{p}:T_{p}X\to T_{p}X
are referred to as the
multipliers
of
f^{m}
at
p
.
The multiplier pair
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
is said to be

multiplicatively independent
(MI for short) if any relation

\mbox{ $\alpha_{1}^{n_{1}}=\alpha_{2}^{n_{2}}$ \, with \, $(n_{1},n_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}$ \,
implies \, $n_{1}=n_{2}=0$}.

(40)

Otherwise,
\alpha
is
multiplicatively dependent
(MD for short).
Clearly, MI implies NR, or equivalently resonance implies MD.
If the power
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{k}:=(\alpha_{1}^{k},\alpha_{2}^{k})
is MD (resp. NR) for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}

then
\alpha
itself is MD (resp. NR).
Conversely, if
\alpha
is MD then
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{k}
remains MD for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
.
Even if
\alpha
is NR, however,
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{k}
may be resonant for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
.
In this sense, MD and MI are stable under taking powers, but resonance and NR are not.

Now let
f:X\to X
be a K3 surface automorphism with twist number
\delta=\delta(f)
.
Suppose that
f
satisfies condition
(\mathrm{A}1)
in §
1
, which says that

\delta
is a non-real conjugate to a Salem number
\lambda
.
Let
p\in X
be a periodic point of
f
with period
m
and let
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})

be the multiplier pair of
f^{m}
at
p
.
The tangent map
(df^{m})_{p}:T_{p}X\to T_{p}X
has determinant
\delta^{m}
, so
the multipliers satisfy
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}=\delta^{m}
.
This allows us to write

\alpha_{1}=\delta^{m/2}\alpha,\qquad\alpha_{2}=\delta^{m/2}\alpha^{-1},\qquad\alpha\in\mathbb{C}^{\times},

(41)

where
\alpha^{\pm 1}
may be swapped and the branch of
\delta^{1/2}
is chosen so that it becomes conjugate to the

positive
square root
\lambda^{1/2}
of
\lambda
.
If
\lambda^{1/2}
is again a Salem number, then the branch of
\delta^{1/2}
required is unique;
otherwise, either branch of it is alright.
The goal of this section is to establish a
criterion
for deciding whether

\alpha
is MI, or MD but NR, or resonant (Theorem
6.3
).
We shall work with the following hypothesis, or ansatz,

Hypothesis 6.1

There exists a rational function
\hat{P}(w)\in\mathbb{Q}(w)
with rational coefficients such that

\alpha+\alpha^{-1}=\hat{P}(\hat{\tau}),\qquad\hat{\tau}:=\delta^{1/2}+\delta^{-1/2},

(42)

where
\hat{P}(w)
is an
even
function for even
m
and an
odd
function for odd
m
respectively.

Imposing Hypothesis
6.1
is not unreasonable, because applying a holomorphic
Lefschetz-type fixed point formula to a K3 surface automorphism often gives rise
to such a function
\hat{P}(w)
as in (
42
);
see §§
9
–
10
for details.
Our main goal in this section is Theorem
6.3
below.
It is motivated by applications to dynamics on K3 surfaces, but the theorem itself is
logically independent of K3 surface automorphisms.

To formulate the result we introduce some terminology and notation.
A polynomial
u(z)
of even degree, say
2n
, is said to be
palindromic

if
u(z)=z^{2n}\,u(z^{-1})
.
If this is the case, then there exists a unique polynomial
U(w)
of degree
n
,
called the
trace polynomial
of
u(z)
, such that
u(z)=z^{n}\,U(z+z^{-1})
.
The most basic examples are the trace polynomials
H_{n}(w)
of
z^{2n}+1
,
which are the unique polynomials
H_{n}(w)\in\mathbb{Z}[w]
such that

H_{n}(z+z^{-1})=z^{n}+z^{-n},\qquad n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

(43)

Although of the same notation,
H_{1}(w)=w
and
H_{2}(w)=w^{2}-2
here have nothing
to do with those in §
5
.
In terms of the
n
-th Chebyshev polynomial
T_{n}(w)
of the first kind we have

H_{n}(w)=2T_{n}(w/2)
; see e.g. Rivlin
[
14
]
.
Notice that
H_{mn}(w)=H_{m}(H_{n}(w))=H_{n}(H_{m}(w))
holds for all
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

Remark 6.2

In Hypothesis
6.1
, thanks to its parity assumption, the function
\hat{P}(w)
can be expressed as

\hat{P}(w)=P(H_{2}(w))\quad\mbox{when $m$ is even};\qquad\hat{P}(w)=w^{-1}P(H_{2}(w))\quad\mbox{when $m$ is odd},

(44)

for some rational function
P(w)\in\mathbb{Q}(w)
, where
H_{2}(w)=w^{2}-2
.
If we define a function
Q(w)\in\mathbb{Q}(w)
by

Q(w):=P^{2}(w)-2\quad\mbox{when $m$ is even};\qquad Q(w):=(w+2)^{-1}P^{2}(w)-2\quad\mbox{when $m$ is odd},

(45)

then applying
H_{2}(\,\cdot\,)
to equation (
42
) gives the following equation,
free from the square root of
\delta
,

\alpha^{2}+\alpha^{-2}=Q(\tau),\qquad\tau:=H_{2}(\hat{\tau})=\delta+\delta^{-1}.

(46)

From definition (
45
) we have
Q(w)\geq-2
on the interval
w>-2
,
although
Q(w)
may have some poles there.

For each integer
n\geq 3
, the
n
-th cyclotomic polynomial
\mathrm{C}_{n}(z)
is palindromic
of even degree
\phi(n)\geq 2
, where
\phi(n)
is Euler’s totient, so we can
speak of its trace polynomial
\mathrm{CT}_{n}(w)
, that is, the
n
-th
cyclotomic
trace polynomial
.
The special case of
n=1,2
needs some care.
If we employ the nonstandard convention

\mathrm{C}_{1}(z)=(z-1)^{2},\quad\mathrm{C}_{2}(z):=(z+1)^{2},\quad\mathrm{CT}_{1}(w):=w-2,\quad\mathrm{CT}_{2}(w):=w+2,\quad\phi(1)=\phi(2)=2,

(47)

then all cases
n\in\mathbb{N}
can be treated in a unified manner.
Convention (
47
) is valid only within this section.

A
reciprocal number
is an algebraic integer
\xi\neq\pm 1

that is conjugate to its reciprocal
\xi^{-1}
over
\mathbb{Q}
.
In this case the sum
\eta:=\xi+\xi^{-1}
is called the
trace
of
\xi
.
The minimal polynomial of
\xi
is a palindromic polynomial of even degree,
and its trace polynomial is the minimal polynomial of the trace
\eta
.
Typical examples of reciprocal numbers are roots of unity (other than
\pm 1
)
and Salem numbers, whose traces are
cyclotomic traces
and
Salem traces
, respectively.
In passing from reciprocal numbers to their traces, the polynomials
H_{n}(w)
arise naturally.
Taking powers
\xi\mapsto\xi^{n}
of a reciprocal number
\xi
induces the
operations
\eta\mapsto H_{n}(\eta)
on its trace
\eta:=\xi+\xi^{-1}
.

Theorem 6.3

Let
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
be a multiplier pair as in formula (
41
),
where
\delta
is a non-real conjugate to a Salem number
\lambda
.
Suppose that
\alpha
satisfies equation (
42
) in Hypothesis
6.1
.
Let
\rho:=\lambda+\lambda^{-1}
be the trace of
\lambda
.

(\mathrm{I})

If
Q(\rho)\in[-2,\,2]
, then
\alpha
is MD exactly when there exists an integer

k\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\mathrm{CT}_{k}(Q(\rho))=0,\qquad\phi(k)\,|\,(\deg\lambda),

(48)

where
\phi(k)
is Euler’s totient and
\deg\lambda
is the degree
of
\lambda
as an algebraic number.
In this case
\alpha
is NR.

(\mathrm{I\!I})

If
Q(\rho)>2
, then
\alpha
is MD exactly when there
exist a coprime
(j,k)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
and a Salem trace
\sigma
such that

H_{j}(\sigma)=Q(\rho),\qquad\rho=H_{k}(\sigma).

(49)

In this case,
(j,k)
and
\sigma
are unique, and
\alpha

is NR if and only if
(j,k)
satisfies the conditions:

(\mathrm{N}1)

\varDelta:=km-j\geq 2
,

(\mathrm{N}2)

\varDelta
does not divide
m
,

(\mathrm{N}3)

if
\varDelta
is even,
\varDelta
divides
2m
and
(2m)/\varDelta
is odd,
then
\hat{P}(\hat{\rho})<0
,

where
\hat{\rho}:=\lambda^{1/2}+\lambda^{-1/2}
with

\lambda^{1/2}
being the positive square root of
\lambda
.

When
\varDelta=2
,
(\mathrm{N}2)
implies that
m
must be odd,
so
(\mathrm{N}3)
simply requires
\hat{P}(\hat{\rho})<0
.
In this theorem we must always keep in mind that
j
and
k
should be coprime,
even when this constraint is not mentioned explicitly.

Remark 6.4

All criteria in Theorem
6.3
are stated only in terms of the
Salem number
\lambda
, period
m
, rational function
\hat{P}(w)
and
a couple of quantities uniquely determined by them.

(\mathrm{1})

In case
(\mathrm{I})
the division relation
\phi(k)\,|\,(\deg\lambda)
in
condition (
48
) confines the integer
k
into finite possibilities.
For example, if the Salem number
\lambda
is the dynamical degree
of a K3 surface automorphism, then
\deg\lambda
is an even integer between
4
and
22
.
There are exactly
43
numbers
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\phi(k)\leq 22
,
which are given in Table
6.1
.
Hence any number
k
satisfying condition (
48
), if it exists, lies in this table.

\phi(k)

,

)

,

,

,

which satisfy
