.

(\mathrm{2})

In case
(\mathrm{I\!I})
, once
\lambda
and
\hat{P}(w)
are given explicitly, it is only a task of
finite procedures to search for those triples
(j,k;\sigma)
which satisfy condition
(
49
).
In fact, there exists a bound
(j_{0},k_{0})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
depending only on

\rho
and
Q(w)
such that the pair
(j,k)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
in condition (
49
)
must satisfy
j\leq j_{0}
and
k\,|\,k_{0}
.
Moreover, the Salem trace
\sigma
can be determined from
(\rho,k)
uniquely and concretely.
We refer to Remark
6.10
for the details of how
(j_{0},k_{0};\sigma)
is determined.
If
\alpha
is NR then
j
must also satisfy
j\leq km-2
by criterion (N1).

An easy consequence of Theorem
6.3
is the following corollary, which
already appears in
[
6
, Lemma 7.1]
.

Corollary 6.5

In the situation of Theorem
6.3
,
(\mathrm{i})
if
Q(\rho)
is not an algebraic integer,
or
(\mathrm{ii})
if
Q(\rho^{\prime})>2
for some Galois conjugate
\rho^{\prime}
to
\rho
but different from
\rho

itself, then the pair
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
is MI.

Theorem
6.3
is established at the end of §
6.3
after providing
three lemmas (Lemmas
6.12
,
6.13
and
6.14
).

6.1
Non-Resonance

In a general setting we consider when an MD pair
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
is NR.
The result of this subsection, Proposition
6.7
, is used in §
6.3

where Salem numbers come to play.
We say that a relation
\alpha_{1}^{N_{1}}=\alpha_{2}^{N_{2}}
for
\alpha
is

primitive
, if for any relation
\alpha_{1}^{n_{1}}=\alpha_{2}^{n_{2}}
there exists
an integer
k
such that
n_{1}=kN_{1}
and
n_{2}=kN_{2}
.
If at least one of
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
is a root of unity, then

\alpha
is obviously resonant.
So we assume that

\mbox{neither of $\alpha_{1}$ and $\alpha_{2}$ is a root of unity}.

(50)

Lemma 6.6

Suppose that
\alpha
is MD and satisfies condition (
50
).
Then
\alpha
admits a unique primitive relation
\alpha_{1}^{N_{1}}=\alpha_{2}^{N_{2}}

such that
N_{1}\geq 1
and
N_{2}\neq 0
.
Moreover the pair
\alpha
is NR if and only if either

N_{1}\geq 2,\quad N_{2}\geq 2;\qquad\mbox{or}\qquad N_{1}=N_{2}=1.

(51)

Proof
.
Let
\alpha_{1}^{n_{1}}=\alpha_{2}^{n_{2}}
be any nontrivial relation for
\alpha
.
Then neither of
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
is zero by condition (
50
).
We may take
n_{1}
to be positive upon replacing
(n_{1},n_{2})
by

(-n_{1},-n_{2})
when
n_{1}
is negative.
Let
\alpha_{1}^{N_{1}}=\alpha_{2}^{N_{2}}
be the nontrivial relation with the
least positive value of
N_{1}\geq 1
.
Notice that
N_{2}
is nonzero.
Given any relation
\alpha_{1}^{n_{1}}=\alpha_{2}^{n_{2}}
, Euclidean algorithm
allows us to write
n_{1}=kN_{1}+r
with
0\leq r<N_{1}
to have

\alpha_{2}^{n_{2}}=\alpha_{1}^{n_{1}}=\alpha_{1}^{kN_{1}+r}=\alpha_{1}^{r}(\alpha_{1}^{N_{1}})^{k}=\alpha_{1}^{r}(\alpha_{2}^{N_{2}})^{k}=\alpha_{1}^{r}\alpha_{2}^{kN_{2}},\quad\mbox{that is},\quad\alpha_{1}^{r}=\alpha_{2}^{n_{2}-kN_{2}}.

The minimality of
N_{1}
forces
r=0
and
\alpha_{2}^{n_{2}-kN_{2}}=1
.
The former equation
r=0
yields
n_{1}=kN_{1}
, while the latter equation
implies
n_{2}=kN_{2}
, because
\alpha_{2}
is not a root of unity.
Thus
\alpha_{1}^{N_{1}}=\alpha_{2}^{N_{2}}
is a primitive relation such that

N_{1}\geq 1
and
N_{2}\neq 0
.
The uniqueness of such a primitive relation is clear.

Next we show that
\alpha
is resonant exactly when one of the following
three conditions is satisfied:

(\mathrm{a})\quad N_{2}\leq-1;\qquad(\mathrm{b})\quad N_{1}=1,\quad N_{2}\geq 2;\qquad(\mathrm{c})\quad N_{1}\geq 2,\quad N_{2}=1.

In case (a) the pair
\alpha
is resonant, because the relation
\alpha_{1}^{N_{1}}=\alpha_{2}^{N_{2}}

yields
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{1}^{N_{1}+1}\alpha_{2}^{|N_{2}|}
with
(N_{1}+1)+|N_{2}|\geq 3
.
Suppose that
N_{2}\geq 1
and
\alpha
is resonant.
Then there exist integers
n_{1}\geq 0
and
n_{2}\geq 0
with
n_{1}+n_{2}\geq 2

such that either
(\mathrm{i})

\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{1}^{n_{1}}\alpha_{2}^{n_{2}}

or
(\mathrm{ii})

\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{1}^{n_{1}}\alpha_{2}^{n_{2}}
.
In case
(\mathrm{i})
we have
\alpha_{1}^{1-n_{1}}=\alpha_{2}^{n_{2}}
,
so there exists an integer
k
such that
1-n_{1}=kN_{1}
, i.e.
n_{1}=1-kN_{1}

and
n_{2}=kN_{2}
.
Then we have
2\leq n_{1}+n_{2}=1+k(N_{2}-N_{1})
, that is,
1\leq k(N_{2}-N_{1})
,
which implies
k\neq 0
.
Since
0\leq n_{2}=kN_{2}
and
1\leq N_{2}
, we have
1\leq k
, so

0\leq n_{1}=1-kN_{1}\leq 1-1\cdot 1=0
, thus
k=N_{1}=1
.
The inequality
1\leq k(N_{2}-N_{1})
now becomes
2\leq N_{2}
.
Hence case (i) implies case (b).
Similarly case
(\mathrm{ii})
implies case (c).
The converse implications are obvious.
The logical negation of
(\mathrm{a})\vee(\mathrm{b})\vee(\mathrm{c})

is condition (
51
), which is a necessary and sufficient
condition for
\alpha
to be NR.
\Box

Proposition 6.7

Suppose that
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
is MD and

\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}
is not a root of unity.
Then the two conditions

(\mathrm{D}1)

there exists a relation
\alpha_{1}^{n_{1}}=\alpha_{2}^{n_{2}}

such that
n_{1}n_{2}\geq 1
,

(\hat{\mathrm{D}}1)

any nontrivial relation
\alpha_{1}^{m_{1}}=\alpha_{2}^{m_{2}}

satisfies
m_{1}m_{2}\geq 1
,

are equivalent.
Moreover,
\alpha
is NR exactly when
(\mathrm{D}1)
, or
(\hat{\mathrm{D}}1)
, and
the following condition are satisfied:

(\mathrm{D}2)

\alpha_{1}\neq\alpha_{2}^{n}
and
\alpha_{2}\neq\alpha_{1}^{n}

for any integer
n\geq 2
.

Proof
.
The implication
(\hat{\mathrm{D}}1)\Rightarrow(\mathrm{D}1)
is obvious.
We show the converse
(\mathrm{D}1)\Rightarrow(\hat{\mathrm{D}}1)
.
Notice that (D1) implies condition (
50
).
Indeed, if one of
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
is a root of unity, then so is the other
by (D1) and hence
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}
is also a root of unity, contrary to the assumption.
By Lemma
6.6
the pair
\alpha
admits a primitive relation

\alpha_{1}^{N_{1}}=\alpha_{2}^{N_{2}}
with
N_{1}\geq 1
and
N_{2}\neq 0
.
Then in (D1) we have
n_{1}=kN_{1}
,
n_{2}=kN_{2}
and

1\leq n_{1}n_{2}=k^{2}N_{1}N_{2}
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, hence

N_{1}\geq 1
and
N_{2}\geq 1
.
So, for any nontrivial relation
\alpha_{1}^{m_{1}}=\alpha_{2}^{m_{2}}
we have

m_{1}=jN_{1}
,
m_{2}=jN_{2}
and
m_{1}m_{2}=j^{2}N_{1}N_{2}\geq 1
for some

j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, which verifies
(\hat{\mathrm{D}}1)
.
Now assume (D2) in addition to (D1).
By (D2) the primitive relation
\alpha_{1}^{N_{1}}=\alpha_{2}^{N_{2}}
just mentioned must satisfy condition (
51
),
so Lemma
6.6
implies that
\alpha
is NR .
Conversely, suppose that
\alpha
is NR.
Then
\alpha
satisfies condition (
50
), so Lemma
6.6
tells us
that
\alpha
admits a primitive relation
\alpha_{1}^{N_{1}}=\alpha_{2}^{N_{2}}

satisfying condition (
51
).
In particular it satisfies
N_{1}N_{2}\geq 1
and serves as a relation satisfying (D1).
Condition (D2) is also a consequence of the property
(
51
) for
(N_{1},N_{2})
.
\Box

6.2
Some Properties of Salem Numbers

As a preliminary to the next section we provide some basic properties of Salem numbers.

Lemma 6.8

For Salem numbers as defined in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec:intro}
, the following properties hold.

(\mathrm{S}1)

If
\lambda>1
is an algebraic integer all of whose
conjugates other than
\lambda
lie in the closed unit disk with at least
one conjugate on the unit circle, then
\lambda
is a Salem number.

(\mathrm{S}2)

If
\lambda
is a Salem number of degree
d
, then so is
the power
\lambda^{n}
and
\mathbb{Q}(\lambda)=\mathbb{Q}(\lambda^{n})
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(\mathrm{S}3)

If
\lambda
is a Salem number, then there exists a unique
Salem number
\lambda_{0}
, called the
\mathrm{primitive}
of
\lambda
, such that
any Salem number in
\mathbb{Q}(\lambda)
is a positive power of
\lambda_{0}
.

For these statements and their proofs we refer to
Smyth
[
16
, Lemmas 1, 2 and Proposition 3]
.
In accordance with (S3), if
\lambda_{0}
is the primitive of a Salem umber
\lambda
,
then the Salem trace
\rho_{0}:=\lambda_{0}+\lambda_{0}^{-1}
is referred to as the

primitive
of the Salem trace
\rho:=\lambda+\lambda^{-1}
.
We have
\rho=H_{k_{0}}(\rho_{0})
if
\lambda
is the
k_{0}
-th power of
\lambda_{0}
.

Lemma 6.9

Let
\rho
and
\sigma
be Salem traces and suppose that
\rho=H_{k}(\sigma)
with
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
If
\rho_{0}
is the primitive of
\rho
and
\rho=H_{k_{0}}(\rho_{0})
with
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
,
then
k_{0}
is divisible by
k
, the Salem trace
\rho_{0}
is the primitive of
\sigma
,
and
\sigma=H_{k_{0}/k}(\rho_{0})
.

Proof
.
Let
\lambda
,
\mu
and
\lambda_{0}
be the Salem numbers whose traces are

\rho
,
\sigma
and
\rho_{0}
respectively.
The relation
\rho=H_{k}(\sigma)
then means
\lambda+\lambda^{-1}=\mu^{k}+\mu^{-k}
,
which implies
\lambda=\mu^{k}
since
\lambda>1
and
\mu^{k}>1
.
Similarly we have
\lambda=\lambda_{0}^{k_{0}}
.
From
(\mathrm{S}2)
in Lemma
6.8
we have
\mathbb{Q}(\mu)=\mathbb{Q}(\mu^{k})=\mathbb{Q}(\lambda)
,
so
(\mathrm{S}3)
shows that
\lambda_{0}
is also the primitive of
\mu
, hence
\mu=\lambda_{0}^{m}

for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Thus we have
\lambda_{0}^{k_{0}}=\lambda=\mu^{k}=\lambda_{0}^{mk}
and hence
k_{0}=mk
.
Therefore
k_{0}
is divisible by
k
with quotient
k_{0}/k=m
, and we have

\sigma=\mu+\mu^{-1}=\lambda_{0}^{m}+\lambda_{0}^{-m}=H_{m}(\rho_{0})=H_{k_{0}/k}(\rho_{0})
.

\Box

Remark 6.10

This is a continuation of Remark
6.4
.(2).
The number
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
mentioned there is given as the integer
such that
\rho=H_{k_{0}}(\rho_{0})
, where
\rho_{0}
is the primitive
of the Salem trace
\rho
.
By Lemma
6.9
the integer
k\in\mathbb{N}
in condition (
49
) divides
k_{0}
and
determines the Salem trace
\sigma
as
\sigma=H_{k_{0}/k}(\rho_{0})
.
From it we have
\sigma\geq\rho_{0}
.
Note that
H_{j}(w)
is strictly increasing in
j\in\mathbb{N}
and in
w>2
.
Take
j_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
to be the least integer such that
H_{j_{0}}(\rho_{0})\geq Q(\rho)
,
which certainly exists since
H_{j}(\rho_{0})\to\infty
as
j\to\infty
strictly increasingly.
For any
j>j_{0}
we have
H_{j}(\sigma)>H_{j_{0}}(\sigma)\geq H_{j_{0}}(\rho_{0})\geq Q(\rho)
.
So the integer
j\in\mathbb{N}
in condition (
49
) must satisfy
j\leq j_{0}
and
k\,|\,k_{0}
.

Lemma 6.11

If two Salem numbers
\lambda
and
\mu
are related by

\lambda^{n}=\mu^{m}
for some
m
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then there exist a unique Salem
number
\nu
and a unique coprime pair
(j,k)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
such that

\lambda=\nu^{j}
and
\mu=\nu^{k}
.

Proof
.
From
(\mathrm{S}2)
in Lemma
6.8
we have

\mathbb{Q}(\mu)=\mathbb{Q}(\mu^{m})=\mathbb{Q}(\lambda^{n})=\mathbb{Q}(\lambda)
.
Then by
(\mathrm{S}3)
the Salem numbers
\lambda
and

\mu
have the same primitive
\lambda_{0}
and admit power representations

\lambda=\lambda_{0}^{s}
and
\mu=\lambda_{0}^{t}
for some
s
,
t\in\mathbb{N}
.
Letting
\nu:=\lambda_{1}^{g}
with
g:=\gcd\{s,t\}
, we have

\lambda=\nu^{j}
and
\mu=\nu^{k}
, where
j:=s/g
and
k:=t/g
are coprime.
The uniqueness of
\nu
,
j
and
k
is obvious from the structure of positive powers
of a Salem number.
\Box

6.3
Multiplicative Dependence and Non-Resonance

We give three lemmas, the first of which is prepared for case
(\mathrm{I})
of Theorem
6.3
,
while the second and third ones are for case
(\mathrm{I\!I})
.
They immediately enable us to establish Theorem
6.3
at the end of this subsection.

Let
\pi\in\operatorname{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
be a Galois automorphism such that
\pi(\delta)=\lambda
.
It converts equation (
46
) into

\zeta+\zeta^{-1}=Q(\rho),\qquad\zeta:=\pi(\alpha^{2}).

(52)

We note that
\alpha
is MD if and only if
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{2}:=(\alpha_{1}^{2},\alpha_{2}^{2})
is MD.
A nontrivial relation for
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{2}
is given by

\alpha_{1}^{2n_{1}}=\alpha_{2}^{2n_{2}}

(53)

where
n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
not both zero.
Under representation (
41
) this relation is equivalent to

\alpha^{2(n_{1}+n_{2})}=\delta^{m(n_{1}-n_{2})},

(
\ref{eqn:alal}^{\prime}
)

where
n_{1}+n_{2}
must be nonzero, for otherwise
n_{1}-n_{2}
is nonzero and equation
(

′

‣
6.3
) becomes
\delta^{m(n_{1}-n_{2})}=1
, which contradicts the fact
that
\delta
is not a root of unity.
We may and shall assume that

(n_{1}+n_{2})(n_{1}-n_{2})\geq 0\qquad\mbox{with}\quad n_{1}+n_{2}\neq 0,

(54)

after swapping
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
(or swapping
\alpha^{\pm 1}
) if necessary.
Since
\delta
is a non-real conjugate to the Salem number
\lambda
, equation
(

′

‣
6.3
) implies that
\alpha
is an algebraic integer of modulus
1
.
Moreover
\pi
converts (

′

‣
6.3
) into equation

\zeta^{n_{1}+n_{2}}=\lambda^{m(n_{1}-n_{2})}.

(55)

Lemma 6.12

Suppose that
Q(\rho)\in[-2,\,2]
.

(\mathrm{1})

If
\alpha
is MD, then
\mathrm{CT}_{k}(Q(\rho))=0
and
\phi(k)\,|\,(\deg\lambda)
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

(\mathrm{2})

If
\mathrm{CT}_{k}(Q(\rho))=0
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\alpha
is MD and NR.

Proof
.
Since
Q(\rho)\in[-2,\,2]
, equation (
52
) forces
|\zeta|=1
.
With convention (
47
), equation (
52
) yields

\mathrm{C}_{k}(\zeta)=\zeta^{\phi(k)/2}\,\mathrm{CT}_{k}(\zeta+\zeta^{-1})=\zeta^{\phi(k)/2}\,\mathrm{CT}_{k}(Q(\rho))\qquad\mbox{for any}\quad k\in\mathbb{N}.

(56)

(1) Suppose that
\alpha
is MD and let (
53
) be a nontrivial relation for
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{2}
.
Equation (
55
) yields
\lambda^{m(n_{1}-n_{2})}=|\zeta|^{n_{1}+n_{2}}=1
.
Since
\lambda>1
, we must have
n_{1}-n_{2}=0
, so equation (

′

‣
6.3
) becomes
\alpha^{4n_{1}}=1
.
Thus
\alpha
is a root of unity and so is
\zeta=\pi(\alpha^{2})
.
Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
be the integer such that
\zeta
is a primitive
k
-th root of unity.
Since
\mathrm{C}_{k}(\zeta)=0
, equation (
56
) yields
\mathrm{CT}_{k}(Q(\rho))=0
.
In the field extensions
\mathbb{Q}\subset\mathbb{Q}(Q(\rho))\subset\mathbb{Q}(\rho)
we have

[\mathbb{Q}(\rho):\mathbb{Q}]=\deg\rho=(\deg\lambda)/2,\qquad[\mathbb{Q}(Q(\rho)):\mathbb{Q}]=\deg(\zeta+\zeta^{-1})=\phi(k)/2.

Thus
[\mathbb{Q}(\rho):\mathbb{Q}(Q(\rho))]=(\deg\lambda)/\phi(k)
is an integer, that is,
\phi(k)
divides
\deg\lambda
.

(2) Suppose that
\mathrm{CT}_{k}(Q(\rho))=0
.
Then equation (
56
) implies
\mathrm{C}_{k}(\zeta)=0
, so
\zeta^{k}=1

and hence
\alpha^{2k}=1
by
\alpha^{2}=\pi^{-1}(\zeta)
.
Now a nontrivial relation (

′

‣
6.3
) holds with
n_{1}=n_{2}=k
, thus
\alpha
is MD.
In (
41
) the number
\alpha
is a root of unity,
while
\delta^{m/2}
is not, so an argument in the proof of Proposition
5.3
shows that
\alpha
is NR.

\Box

Lemma 6.13

Suppose that
Q(\rho)>2
.

(\mathrm{1})

Suppose that
\alpha
is MD.
Then there exist a Salem number
\mu
and a coprime pair
(j,k)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}

such that
\lambda=\mu^{k}
and for any Galois automorphism
\pi
sending

\delta\mapsto\lambda
we have
\mu^{j}=\pi(\alpha^{2})
up to swapping of
\alpha^{\pm 1}
.
Moreover equations (
49
) hold with Salem trace
\sigma:=\mu+\mu^{-1}
.

(\mathrm{2})

If equations (
49
) hold for some Salem trace
\sigma
and some
coprime pair
(j,k)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, then
\alpha
is MD.

Proof
. Since
Q(\rho)>2
, equation (
52
) implies that

\zeta
is real, positive, and
\zeta\neq 1
.

(1) Suppose that
\alpha
is MD and let (
53
) be a nontrivial relation for
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{2}
.
Using
0<\zeta\neq 1
,
1<\lambda
and (
54
) in equation
(
55
), we have
(n_{1}+n_{2})(n_{1}-n_{2})\geq 1
and
\zeta>1
.
Thus equation (
52
) determines the number
\zeta=\pi(\alpha^{2})
uniquely and independently of
\pi
.
Let
\varpi
be any Galois automorphism such that
|\varpi(\alpha^{2})|>1
.
Since
\varpi
takes (

′

‣
6.3
) into equation

(\varpi(\alpha^{2}))^{n_{1}+n_{2}}=(\varpi(\delta))^{m(n_{1}-n_{2})}
, we have
|\varpi(\delta)|>1
.
Thus
\varpi(\delta)
is conjugate to the Salem number
\lambda
and has modulus
|\varpi(\delta)|>1
.
Such a number must be the Salem number
\lambda
itself, so
\varpi(\delta)=\lambda
,
hence
\varpi
is a Galois automorphism such that
\varpi(\delta)=\lambda
.
This forces
\varpi(\alpha^{2})=\zeta
.
Therefore
\zeta
is the unique conjugate to
\alpha^{2}
with modulus
|\zeta|>1
,
while
\alpha^{2}
itself is an algebraic integer with modulus
1
.
To put it in another way,
\zeta>1
is an algebraic integer all of whose conjugates
other than
\zeta
lie in the closed unit disk, with its conjugate
\alpha^{2}
on the unit circle.
It follows from
(\mathrm{S}1)
in Lemma
6.8
that
\zeta
is a Salem number.
Lemma
6.11
can now be applied to equation (
55
) to conclude
that there exist a unique Salem number
\mu
and a unique coprime pair
(j,k)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}

such that
\pi(\alpha^{2})=\zeta=\mu^{j}
and
\lambda=\mu^{k}
.
We have
\rho:=\lambda+\lambda^{-1}=\mu^{k}+\mu^{-k}=H_{k}(\sigma)
,
the second equation in (
49
), and equation (
52
) becomes

Q(\rho)=\zeta+\zeta^{-1}=\mu^{j}+\mu^{-j}=H_{j}(\mu+\mu^{-1})=H_{j}(\sigma)
,
the first equation in (
49
).

(2) Suppose that equations (
49
) hold for a Salem trace
\sigma
and a
coprime pair
(j,k)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
.
Let
\mu
be the Salem number whose trace is
\sigma
, i.e.
\mu+\mu^{-1}=\sigma
.
From the second equation in (
49
) we have
\lambda+\lambda^{-1}=\rho=H_{k}(\sigma)=\mu^{k}+\mu^{-k}
.
Since
\lambda>1
and
\mu^{k}>1
, we have
\lambda=\mu^{k}
.
The first equation in (
49
) and equation (
52
) yield

\mu^{j}+\mu^{-j}=H_{j}(\sigma)=Q(\rho)=\zeta+\zeta^{-1}
.
So we have
\mu^{j}=\zeta=\pi(\alpha^{2})
up to possible inversion of
\alpha
.
Put
n_{1}:=km+j
and
n_{2}:=km-j
.
Then
n_{1}+n_{2}=2km\geq 1
,
n_{1}-n_{2}=2j\geq 1
and

(\pi(\alpha^{2}))^{n_{1}+n_{2}}=(\mu^{j})^{2km}=(\mu^{k})^{2jm}=\lambda^{m(n_{1}-n_{2})}=(\pi(\delta))^{m(n_{1}-n_{2})}
.
The last equation is brought back to (

′

‣
6.3
) or equivalently to (
53
)
by the inverse Galois automorphism
\pi^{-1}
.
Therefore
\alpha
is MD.

\Box

Lemma 6.14

Suppose that
Q(\rho)>2
and
\alpha
is MD.
Then
\alpha
is NR if and only if the pair
(j,k)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
in Lemma
\ref{lem:mMD}.(1)

satisfies all of the conditions
(\mathrm{N}1),(\mathrm{N}2),(\mathrm{N}3)
in Theorem
6.3
.

Proof
.
Since
\alpha
is MD, as in (
53
) or (

′

‣
6.3
) for
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{2}
, we can think of any nontrivial
relation for
\alpha
in the form

\alpha_{1}^{n_{1}}=\alpha_{2}^{n_{2}},\qquad\mbox{or equivalently}\qquad\alpha^{n_{1}+n_{2}}=\delta^{m(n_{1}-n_{2})/2}.

(57)

Here we may and shall assume (
54
).
From the way the branch of
\delta^{1/2}
is chosen, there exists a Galois automorphism

\pi
such that
\pi(\delta^{1/2})=\lambda^{1/2}
; see the sentence containing
formula (
41
).
Then
\pi(\delta)=\lambda=\mu^{k}
, so we have
\pi(\alpha^{2})=\mu^{j}

by a statement in Lemma
6.13
.(1).
Thus we have
\pi(\alpha)=\varepsilon\,\mu^{j/2}
for some sign
\varepsilon=\pm 1
, where

\mu^{1/2}
is the positive square root of
\mu>1
.
Then
\pi
converts equations (
42
) and (
57
) into

\varepsilon\cdot H_{j}(\hat{\sigma})=\hat{P}(\hat{\rho}),\qquad\varepsilon^{n_{1}+n_{2}}\,\mu^{j(n_{1}+n_{2})/2}=\mu^{mk(n_{1}-n_{2})/2},

(58)

where
\hat{\sigma}:=\mu^{1/2}+\mu^{-1/2}>2
so that
H_{j}(\hat{\sigma})>2
.
Applying
H_{2}(\,\cdot\,)
to the first equation in (
58
) leads to
the first equation in (
49
), which was already established in Lemma
6.13
.
So conditions (
58
) are equivalent to

\varepsilon\cdot\hat{P}(\hat{\rho})>0,\qquad\varepsilon^{n_{1}+n_{2}}=1,\qquad j(n_{1}+n_{2})=km(n_{1}-n_{2}).

(59)

Suppose that
\alpha
is NR.
It then follows from
(\hat{\mathrm{D}}1)
in Proposition
6.7
that
n_{1}n_{2}\geq 1
.
This together with the last equation in (
59
) yields
1\leq|n_{1}-n_{2}|<|n_{1}+n_{2}|

and
j<km
, that is,
\varDelta:=km-j\geq 1
.
Conversely, assuming that
\varDelta\geq 1
, we put
n_{1}:=km+j\geq 2
and

n_{2}:=\varDelta=km-j\geq 1
to obtain
n_{1}+n_{2}=2km
and
n_{1}-n_{2}=2j
.
Then the last two equations in (
59
) are fulfilled.
The first inequality in (
59
) is also true, if
\varepsilon=\pm 1
is the sign of

\hat{P}(\hat{\rho})\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
.
Thus we can get a relation for
\alpha
as in (
57
) such that
n_{1}n_{2}\geq 1
,
so condition
(\mathrm{D}1)
in Proposition
6.7
is satisfied.
Now that
\alpha
is MD and
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}=\delta^{m}
is not a root of unity,
Proposition
6.7
is available in the current situation.
Therefore
\alpha
is NR if and only if
\varDelta\geq 1
and
\alpha
satisfies condition

(\mathrm{D}2)
in Proposition
6.7
.
The latter condition is equivalent to say (up to swapping of
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
) that

no
pair
(n_{1},\varepsilon)
of integer
n_{1}\geq 2
and sign
\varepsilon=\pm 1
can satisfy condition (
59
) with
n_{2}=1
,
that is,

\varepsilon\cdot\hat{P}(\hat{\rho})>0,\qquad\varepsilon^{n_{1}+1}=1,\qquad j(n_{1}+1)=km(n_{1}-1).

(
\ref{eqn:jk}^{\prime}
)

The other way around, we ask when (

′

‣
6.3
) admits a solution
(n_{1},\varepsilon)
.
Hereafter we assume
\varDelta\geq 1
.

Claim 1
.
Condition (

′

‣
6.3
) is satisfied by an
odd
integer
n_{1}\geq 2
and a sign

\varepsilon=\pm 1
if and only if
\varDelta\,|\,m
.

Suppose that (

′

‣
6.3
) is satisfied by an odd integer
n_{1}\geq 2
and a sign

\varepsilon=\pm 1
.
Then the last equation in (

′

‣
6.3
) reads
j(n_{1}+1)/2=km(n_{1}-1)/2
,
where the positive integers
(n_{1}+1)/2
and
(n_{1}-1)/2
are coprime
as they differ only by
1
.
So there exists an integer
l\in\mathbb{N}
such that
j=l(n_{1}-1)/2

and
km=l(n_{1}+1)/2
.
Subtracting the former equation from the latter yields
\varDelta:=km-j=l
,
showing that
\varDelta
divides both
j
and
km
.
As
j
and
k
are coprime,
\varDelta
must divide
m
.
Conversely, suppose that
\varDelta
divides
m
and put

n_{1}=2km^{\prime}-1
with
m^{\prime}:=m/\varDelta\in\mathbb{N}
.
Clearly
n_{1}
is odd and
n_{1}\geq 1
.
More strictly we have
n_{1}\geq 2
, for otherwise
1=n_{1}=2km^{\prime}-1
would lead
to
k=m^{\prime}=1
,
m=\varDelta
and hence
j=km-\varDelta=m-\varDelta=0
,
contradicting
j\geq 1
.
We have also

\varDelta(n_{1}-1)/2=\varDelta(km^{\prime}-1)=km-\varDelta=j,\qquad\varDelta(n_{1}+1)/2=\varDelta km^{\prime}=km,

which readily lead back to the last equation in (

′

‣
6.3
).
We can determine the sign
\varepsilon=\pm 1
so that the inequality

\varepsilon\cdot P(\hat{\rho})>0
holds true.
Equation
\varepsilon^{n_{1}+1}=1
is automatically fulfilled as
n_{1}+1
is even.
This proves Claim
1
.

Claim 2
.
Condition (

′

‣
6.3
) is satisfied by an
even
integer
n_{1}\geq 2
and
a sign
\varepsilon=\pm 1
if and only if

\mbox{$\varDelta$ is even, $\varDelta$ divides $2m$,
$(2m)/\varDelta$ is odd, and $\hat{P}(\hat{\rho})>0$}.

(60)

Suppose that (

′

‣
6.3
) is satisfied by an even integer
n_{1}\geq 2

and a sign
\varepsilon=\pm 1
.
The equation
\varepsilon^{n_{1}+1}=1
forces
\varepsilon=1
, since
n_{1}+1
is odd.
Now the first inequality in (

′

‣
6.3
) reads
\hat{P}(\hat{\rho})>0
.
In the last equation in (

′

‣
6.3
) we notice that
n_{1}+1
and
n_{1}-1

are coprime, as
n_{1}\pm 1
are odd integers which differ by
2
.
So there exists an integer
l\in\mathbb{N}
such that
j=l(n_{1}-1)

and
km=l(n_{1}+1)
.
Subtracting the former equation from the latter yields
\varDelta=2l
,
which shows that
\varDelta
is even and
\varDelta/2
divides both of
j
and
km
.
As
j
and
k
are coprime,
\varDelta/2
must divide
m
, that is,

\varDelta
must divide
2m
.
We have also
(2m)/\varDelta=m/l=(n_{1}+1)/k\in\mathbb{N}
with
n_{1}+1
odd.
This shows that
(2m)/\varDelta
must be an odd integer.
Therefore we have (
60
).
Conversely, suppose that (
60
) holds.
Notice that
k
must be odd, for otherwise
j=km-\varDelta
would be even,
so
j
and
k
could not be coprime.
Take the odd integer
k^{\prime}:=(2m)/\varDelta\in\mathbb{N}
and put
n_{1}:=kk^{\prime}-1
.
Obviously,
n_{1}
is even and
n_{1}\geq 0
.
If
n_{1}=0
, then
k=k^{\prime}=1
, so
2m=\varDelta=km-j=m-j
and hence

j=-m\leq-1
, contradicting
j\geq 1
.
Thus
n_{1}
is even and
n_{1}\geq 2
.
Since
\varDelta
is even, we can write
\varDelta=2l
for some
l\in\mathbb{N}
.
The definition of
n_{1}
then leads to
km=l(n_{1}+1)
and

j=km-\varDelta=l(n_{1}+1)-2l=l(n_{1}-1)
, which give
the last equation in (

′

‣
6.3
).
The first inequality and the second equation in (

′

‣
6.3
) are
fulfilled by putting
\varepsilon=1
.
This proves Claim
2
.

Logical negation of
\varDelta\,|\,m
in Claim
1
gives condition
(\mathrm{N}2)
,
which together with
\varDelta\geq 1
yields
(\mathrm{N}1)
.
Finally, the logical negation of condition (
60
) in Claim
2
leads to condition
(\mathrm{N}3)
.
So the lemma is established.
\Box

Proof of Theorem
6.3
.
Case
(\mathrm{I})
follows from Lemma
6.12
, while case
(\mathrm{I\!I})
from
Lemmas
6.13
and
6.14
.
\Box

7
Fixed Point Formulas

Two kinds of fixed point formulas (FPFs for short) are important tools in our analysis.
They were already used in our previous articles
[
5
,
6
]
, but the main theme of this section is
a thorough study of topological and holomorphic local indices around exceptional sets,
especially around exceptional components of type
\mathrm{A}
with trivial symmetry
(§
7.2
, Theorem
7.3
), as well as components of other types
or other symmetries (§
7.3
).

7.1
Reformulations of Fixed Point Formulas

First we reformulate the FPFs used in
[
5
,
6
]
.
Let
f:X\to X
be a K3 surface automorphism satisfying conditions (A1) and (A2) in §
1
.
Let
\operatorname{Fix}^{\mathrm{i}}(f)
denote the set of all isolated fixed points of
f
, and
N(f)
the number of

(-2)
-curves in
X
fixed pointwise by
f
.
From
[
6
, Proposition 4.2]
we have a
topological
Lefschetz-type FPF,

L(f):=2+\operatorname{Tr}(f^{*}|H^{2}(X,\mathbb{C}))=\sum_{p\in\operatorname{Fix}^{\mathrm{i}}(f)}\mu(f,p)+2N(f),

(61)

where
L(f)
is the Lefschetz number of
f
and the local index
\mu(f,p)
of
f
at
p\in\operatorname{Fix}^{\mathrm{i}}(f)
is defined by

\mu(f,p):=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{C}\{z\}/\mathfrak{a}),\qquad\mbox{where}\quad\mathfrak{a}:=(z_{1}-f_{1}(z),\,z_{2}-f_{2}(z)),

with
(f_{1},f_{2})
being the local representation of
f
in terms of a local chart

z=(z_{1},z_{2})
around
p
:
z=(0,0)
.
The right-hand side of equation (
61
) is the sum of local contributions of isolated fixed points
and fixed
(-2)
-curves.
Given an
f
-invariant exceptional component
E\subset\mathcal{E}(X)
, we put

\mu(f,E):=\sum_{p\in\operatorname{Fix}^{\mathrm{i}}(f,E)}\mu(f,p)+2N(f,E),

(62)

where
\operatorname{Fix}^{\mathrm{i}}(f,E)
is the set of all
isolated
fixed points on
E
and
N(f,E)
is the number
of fixed
(-2)
-curves in
E
.
In is convenient to divide the right-hand side of formula (
61
) into
on
and
off
parts:

\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f):=\sum_{E\,:\,\mathrm{inv.}}\mu(f,E),\qquad\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f):=\sum_{p\,:\,\mathrm{off}}\mu(f,p),

(63)

where the fomer sum extends over all
f
-invariant exceptional components
E\subset\mathcal{E}(X)
,
while the latter sum extends over all fixed points
p
off
\mathcal{E}(X)
; note that they are isolated.
FPF (
61
) is then represented as

L(f)=\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)+\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f).

(64)

From
[
6
, Proposition 4.3]
we also have a
holomorphic
Lefechetz-type FPF,

\mathcal{L}(f):=1+\delta^{-1}=\sum_{p\in\operatorname{Fix}^{\mathrm{i}}(f)}\nu(f,p)+N(f)\,\dfrac{\delta+1}{(\delta-1)^{2}},

(65)

where
\mathcal{L}(f)
is the holomorphic Lefschetz number of
f
,
\delta=\delta(f)
is the twist number of
f

and the holomorphic local index
\nu(f,p)
is given by the Grothendieck residue

\nu(f,p):=\operatorname{Res}_{p}\,\dfrac{dz_{1}\wedge dz_{2}}{(z_{1}-f_{1}(z))(z_{2}-f_{2}(z))}.

The right-hand side of (
65
) is the sum of local contributions
of isolated fixed points and fixed
(-2)
-curves.

It is convenient to rewrite FPF (
65
) in terms of

\hat{\tau}:=\delta^{1/2}+\delta^{-1/2}
.
We define
\hat{\nu}(f,p):=\delta^{1/2}\,\nu(f,p)
and

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}(f,E)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{p\in\operatorname{Fix}^{\mathrm{i}}(f,E)}\hat{\nu}(f,p)+N(f,E)\,\delta^{1/2}\dfrac{\delta+1}{(\delta-1)^{2}},

(66a)

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{E\,:\,\mathrm{inv.}}\hat{\nu}(f,E),\qquad\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{off}}(f):=\sum_{p\,:\,\mathrm{off}}\hat{\nu}(f,p),

(66b)

where the summation rules in (
66b
) are the same as those in (
63
).
At a
simple
isolated fixed point
p
we have

\hat{\nu}(f,p)=\dfrac{1}{\hat{\tau}-(\alpha+\alpha^{-1})},\qquad\hat{\tau}:=\delta^{1/2}+\delta^{-1/2},

when
f
has multipliers
\delta^{1/2}\alpha^{\pm 1}
at this point.
FPF (
65
) is now rewritten as

\hat{\tau}=\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f)+\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{off}}(f).

(67)

Let
E\subset\mathcal{E}(X)
be an
f
-invariant exceptional component of Dynkin type
D
.
The map
f
then permutes the
(-2)
-curves in
E
and induces an automorphism of the Dynkin diagram
D
,

\sigma(f,E)\in\operatorname{Aut}(D).

(68)

We say that the pair
(f,E)
, or the set
E
when
f
is understood, has

trivial
,
reflectional
, or
tricyclic symmetry
, if
\sigma(f,E)
has order
1
,
2
, or
3
, respectively.
Note that
\sigma(f,E)
is written
\sigma(f)
in §
5
with
E
being understood.

Notation 7.1

For a Dynkin diagram
D
of type
\mathrm{A}
,
\mathrm{D}
or
\mathrm{E}
, when there is no fear of confusion,
we often denote an
f
-invariant exceptional component of type
D
by the same symbol
D
.
Moreover, if
D
has trivial, reflectional, or tricyclic symmetry, then we write it as

D^{\mathrm{t}}
,
D^{\mathrm{r}}
, or
D^{\mathrm{c}}
, respectively, where
D^{\mathrm{c}}
occurs only when
D=\mathrm{D}_{4}
.

Although FPFs (
64
) and (
67
) are formulated for K3 surface
automorphisms
f
satisfying conditions
(\mathrm{A}1)
and
(\mathrm{A}2)
, the quantities

\mu(f,E)
,
\hat{\nu}(f,E)
,
\sigma(f,E)
etc. are
semi-local
objects which make sense
for the map
f
restricted to a small neighborhood of
E
, and hence for a map on
a minimal resolution of a Kleinian singularity.

7.2
Exceptional Components of Type A with Trivial Symmetry

Back to the situation in §
5
, let
(X,E)
be a minimal resolution
of a Kleinian singularity with exceptional set
E
, and
f:(X,E)\to(X,E)
an
automorphism with twist number
\delta
.
We shall evaluate the numbers
\mu(f,E)
and
\hat{\nu}(f,E)
defined by formulas (
62
)
and (
66a
) respectively, especially when
E
is of type
\mathrm{A}
with trivial symmetry.

Suppose that
E
is of Dynkin type
\mathrm{A}_{n}
with trivial symmetry.
Let
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=(\alpha_{1},\,\alpha_{2})=(\delta^{1/2}\alpha,\,\delta^{1/2}\alpha^{-1})
be
the multipliers of
E
, that is, the eigenvalues of the Jacobian matrix
J:=F^{\prime}(0)\in N_{\operatorname{GL}}
,
where
F:(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)\to(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)
is the automorphism induced from
f
via diagram (
34
).
Let us consider the geometric progression

\gamma_{j}:=\alpha_{1}^{j}\alpha_{2}^{j-n-1}=\delta^{(2j-n-1)/2}\alpha^{n+1},\qquad 0\leq j\leq n+1,

(69)

with common ratio
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}=\delta
.
Suppose that
\delta
is not a root of unity.
Then the map
j\mapsto\gamma_{j}
is one-to-one, so there is no or only one index

i
such that
\gamma_{i}=1
.
Therefore we have the following trichotomy.

(C1)

\gamma_{j}\neq 1
for all indices
j=0,1,\dots,n+1
;

(C2)

\gamma_{i}=1
for a unique index
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
;

(C3)

\gamma_{0}=1
or
\gamma_{n+1}=1
.

In order to give an explicit formula for
\hat{\nu}(f,E)
, in addition to the polynomials
H_{n}(w)
in definition (
43
),
we need to use the polynomials
K_{n}(w)\in\mathbb{Z}[w]
uniquely determined by the equations

z^{n+1}-z^{-(n+1)}=(z-z^{-1})\,K_{n}(z+z^{-1}),\qquad n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

In terms of the
n
-th Chebyshev polynomial
U_{n}(w)
of the second kind we have
K_{n}(w)=U_{n}(w/2)
;
see
[
14
]
.

Remark 7.2

We note the following “even-odd” formulas which are used somewhere or elsewhere;

\displaystyle H_{2n}(w)

\displaystyle=H_{n}(H_{2}(w)),\qquad

\displaystyle H_{2n+1}(w)

\displaystyle=w\{K_{n}(H_{2}(w))-K_{n-1}(H_{2}(w))\},

\displaystyle K_{2n}(w)

\displaystyle=K_{n}(H_{2}(w))+K_{n-1}(H_{2}(w)),\qquad

\displaystyle K_{2n+1}(w)

\displaystyle=wK_{n}(H_{2}(w)).

The main result of this section is stated as follows, where we often denote
E
by

\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}}
following Notation
7.1
.

Theorem 7.3

Let
f:(X,E)\to(X,E)
be an automorphism on a minimal resolution of a Kleinian
singularity of Dynkin type
\mathrm{A}_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Suppose that the twist number
\delta
of
f
is not a root of unity and that the exceptional set

E
has trivial symmetry and multipliers

\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=(\alpha_{1},\,\alpha_{2})=(\delta^{1/2}\alpha,\,\delta^{1/2}\alpha^{-1})
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}})

\displaystyle=n+1,\qquad

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{K_{n}(\hat{\tau})}{H_{n+1}(\hat{\tau})-\{\alpha^{(n+1)}+\alpha^{-(n+1)}\}}\qquad

\displaystyle\mbox{in case $(\mathrm{C}1)$},

(70)

\displaystyle\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}})

\displaystyle=n+1,\qquad

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{K_{n}(\hat{\tau})}{(H_{2}(\hat{\tau})-2)\,K_{i-1}(\hat{\tau})\,K_{n-i}(\hat{\tau})}\qquad

\displaystyle\mbox{in case $(\mathrm{C}2)$},

(71)

where
\hat{\tau}:=\delta^{1/2}+\delta^{-1/2}
.
In case
(\mathrm{C}3)
we have
\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}})\geq n+2
.

Remark 7.4

For the multipliers
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
of
E
we consider its
(n+1)
-st
power
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{n+1}:=(\alpha_{1}^{n+1},\,\alpha_{2}^{n+1})=(\delta^{(n+1)/2}\alpha^{n+1},\,\delta^{(n+1)/2}\alpha^{-(n+1)})
.
In case
(\mathrm{C}1)
of Theorem
7.3
, if
\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}})
is known
in some way or another, say, by using FPFs (
64
) and (
67
), then the
second equation in (
70
) may be settled to have

\alpha^{n+1}+\alpha^{-(n+1)}=\hat{P}(\hat{\tau}),\qquad\hat{\tau}:=\delta^{1/2}+\delta^{-1/2},

(72)

for some rational function
\hat{P}(w)\in\mathbb{Q}(w)
as in Hypothesis
6.1
, where

m=n+1
and
\alpha
is replaced by
\alpha^{n+1}
in formula (
41
).
Then Theorem
6.3
may be used to examine whether
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{n+1}
is
MI, or MD but NR, or resonant.
This idea will be implemented in later sections, for example, in the proofs of Theorems

9.7
and
A.1
.

Remark 7.5

From dynamical point of view a remark should be in order.
From (
69
) we have
(\gamma_{n+1},\,\gamma_{0}^{-1})=(\alpha_{1}^{n+1},\,\alpha_{2}^{n+1})=\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{n+1}
.
The geometric progression (
69
) for the
m
-th iterate
f^{m}
is the
m
-th power,
\gamma_{j}^{m}
,

0\leq j\leq n+1
, of the original progression for
f
.
Thus Theorem
7.3
implies that cases
(\mathrm{C}2)
and
(\mathrm{C}3)
are stable
under iterations of
f
, while case
(\mathrm{C}1)
may switch to
(\mathrm{C}2)
or
(\mathrm{C}3)
.
However,
(\mathrm{C}1)
never switches to
(\mathrm{C}3)
, if

\mbox{neither of the multipliers $\alpha_{1}$ and $\alpha_{2}$ is a root of unity in case $(\mathrm{C}1)$}.

(73)

In particular we have
\mu(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}})=n+1
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
in case
(\mathrm{C}2)
unconditionally, while in case
(\mathrm{C}1)
under condition (
73
).
In case
(\mathrm{C}3)
we always have
\mu(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}})\geq n+2
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
In view of formula (
69
), we also remark that if
\alpha
is MI then
the iterates
f^{m}
stay in case
(\mathrm{C}1)
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof of Theorem
7.3
.
The theorem follow immediately from Lemmas
7.7
,
7.8
and
7.9
below.

\Box

To state and prove these lemmas we make some preparations.
Suppose that
E
consists of
(-2)
-curves
E_{1},\dots,E_{n}
,
the dual graph of which is diagram (
13
).
Suppose for the moment that
n\geq 2
.
By Lemma
4.2
with
\beta:=\delta^{1/2}
and Proposition
5.1
,
in terms of the inhomogeneous coordinates

u_{j}
and
v_{j}
on
E_{j}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
u_{j}v_{j}=1
, in §
3.1
,
the Möbius transformation
f|_{E_{j}}=(f_{J}\circ h)|_{E_{j}}:E_{j}\to E_{j}
,

u_{j}\mapsto u_{j}^{\prime}
and
v_{j}\mapsto v_{j}^{\prime}
is given by

\displaystyle u_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{1}(u_{1}+b_{1}),\qquad

\displaystyle v_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{1}^{-1}\,v_{1}}{1+b_{1}v_{1}},\qquad

\displaystyle j

\displaystyle=1,

(74a)

\displaystyle u_{j}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{j}\,u_{j},\qquad

\displaystyle v_{j}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{j}^{-1}\,v_{j},\qquad

\displaystyle 2

\displaystyle\leq j\leq n-1,

(74b)

\displaystyle u_{n}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{n}\,u_{n}}{1+b_{2}u_{n}},\qquad

\displaystyle v_{n}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{n}^{-1}(v_{n}+b_{2}),\qquad

\displaystyle j

\displaystyle=n.

(74c)

The points
\{p_{j}\}=E_{j}\cap E_{j+1}
,
1\leq j\leq n-1
, are the same as those
in formula (
14
) in §
3.1
, where
p_{j}
is given by
(u_{j+1},v_{j})=(0,0)
in local coordinates.
Due to the constants
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
appearing in (
74a
) and (
74c
), however,
the points
p_{0}\in E_{1}
and
p_{n}\in E_{n}
in formula (
17
) should be redefined as

\mbox{$p_{0}\in E_{1}$ \, by \, $u_{1}=\dfrac{\gamma_{1}b_{1}}{1-\gamma_{1}}$ \, if \, $\gamma_{1}\neq 1$};\qquad\mbox{$p_{n}\in E_{n}$ \, by \, $v_{n}=\dfrac{\gamma_{n}^{-1}b_{2}}{1-\gamma_{n}^{-1}}$ \, if \, $\gamma_{n}\neq 1$}.

Notice that when
\gamma_{1}\neq 1
, the new
p_{0}
is the unique fixed point of
f
on
E_{1}\setminus\{p_{1}\}
.
When
\gamma_{1}=1
, we do not define
p_{0}
, as it fails to make sense.
Similarly, when
\gamma_{n}=1
, we do not define
p_{n}
.
Formulas (
74
) tell us the following:
For each
1\leq j\leq n-1
, the multipliers of
f
at
p_{j}
are

\gamma_{j+1}=\beta^{2j+1-n}\alpha^{n+1}
along
E_{j+1}
and
\gamma_{j}^{-1}=\beta^{n+1-2j}\alpha^{-n-1}
along
E_{j}
.
If
\gamma_{1}\neq 1
, the multipliers of
f
at
p_{0}
are

\gamma_{1}=\beta^{1-n}\alpha^{n+1}
along
E_{1}
and
\gamma_{0}^{-1}=\beta^{n+1}\alpha^{-n-1}

in a normal direction to
E_{1}
.
If
\gamma_{n}\neq 1
, the multipliers of
f
at
p_{n}
are

\gamma_{n}^{-1}=\beta^{1-n}\alpha^{-n-1}
along
E_{n}
and
\gamma_{n+1}=\beta^{n+1}\alpha^{n+1}

in a normal direction to
E_{n}
.
These observations are used to calculate
\hat{\nu}(f,E)
in Lemmas
7.7
and
7.8
.

Remark 7.6

Replacing
\alpha
by
\alpha^{-1}
in the multipliers
\beta\alpha^{\pm 1}
of
E

results in the changes
\gamma_{j}\leftrightarrow\gamma_{n+1-j}^{-1}
,
b_{1}\leftrightarrow b_{2}

and
p_{j}\leftrightarrow p_{n-j}
.
Up to this symmetry, we may assume
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
in case
(\mathrm{C}2)
and

\gamma_{0}=1
in case
(\mathrm{C}3)
.

Let
n=1
.
Proposition
5.1
then implies
f|_{E_{1}}=f_{J}|_{E_{1}}
and formula (
32
) shows
that
f|_{E_{1}}
is given by formula (
74a
), where
\gamma_{1}=\alpha^{2}
.
We define
p_{1}\in E_{1}
by
v_{1}=0
, while
p_{0}\in E_{1}
by
u_{1}=\gamma_{1}b_{1}(1-\gamma_{1})^{-1}

if
\gamma_{1}\neq 1
.
When
\gamma_{1}=1
, we do not define
p_{0}
.
Then what we mentioned in the case of
n\geq 2
carries over in this case.

Lemma 7.7

In case
(\mathrm{C}1)
, the exceptional set
E
contains exactly
n+1
isolated fixed points
p_{0},\dots,p_{n}
,
all simple, and no fixed
(-2)
-curve, and formulas (
70
) hold true.

Proof
.
A careful inspection of formulas (
74
) leads to the assertion about isolated
fixed points and fixed
(-2)
-curves.
Then by definitions (
62
) and (
66a
) we obtain
\mu(f,E)=n+1
and

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}(f,E)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\dfrac{\beta}{(1-\beta^{2j+1-n}\alpha^{n+1})(1-\beta^{n+1-2j}\alpha^{-n-1})}=\dfrac{\beta(1+\beta^{2}+\cdots+\beta^{2n})}{(\beta^{n+1}-\alpha^{n+1})(\beta^{n+1}-\alpha^{-n-1})}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\beta^{n+1}-\beta^{-n-1}}{(\beta-\beta^{-1})\{\beta^{n+1}+\beta^{-n-1}-(\alpha^{n+1}+\alpha^{-n-1})\}}=\dfrac{K_{n}(\hat{\tau})}{H_{n+1}(\hat{\tau})-\{\alpha^{(n+1)}+\alpha^{-(n+1)}\}},

where
\beta:=\delta^{1/2}
and
\hat{\tau}:=\delta^{1/2}+\delta^{-1/2}=\beta+\beta^{-1}

are used.
This proves formulas (
70
).

\Box

Up to the symmetry mentioned in Remark
7.6
, case
(\mathrm{C}2)
can be
divided into two subcases:

(\mathrm{C}2\mathrm{a})

\gamma_{i}=1
for a unique index
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}\cup\{1\}
,
b_{1}=0
if
i=1
;
(\mathrm{C}2\mathrm{b})

\gamma_{1}=1
and
b_{1}\neq 0
.

Lemma 7.8

In subcase
(\mathrm{C}2\mathrm{a})
the exceptional set
E
contains just one
fixed
(-2)
-curve
E_{i}
and exactly
n-1

isolated fixed points
p_{j}
,
j\in\{0,\dots,n\}\setminus\{i-1,i\}
, all simple,
and formulas (
71
) hold true.
In subcase
(\mathrm{C}2\mathrm{b})
,
E
contains exactly
n
isolated fixed points
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}

and no fixed
(-2)
-curve, where
p_{1}
is double, while
p_{2},\dots,p_{n}
are simple, and
formulas (
71
) hold true with
i=1
.

Proof
.
In subcase (C2a), a careful inspection of formulas (
74
) leads to
the assertion about isolated fixed points and fixed curves.
Since
\gamma_{i}=1
, from definition (
69
) we have
\alpha^{n+1}=\beta^{n+1-2i}

and hence

\gamma_{j+1}=\beta^{2j+1-n}\cdot\beta^{n+1-2i}=\beta^{2(j+1-i)}=\delta^{j+1-i},\quad\gamma_{j}^{-1}=\beta^{n+1-2j}\cdot\beta^{2i-n-1}=\beta^{2(i-j)}=\delta^{i-j}.

Thus by definitions (
62
) and (
66a
) we obtain
\mu(f,E)=n+1

and the second formula in (
71
) as follows;

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}(f,E)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{i-2}\dfrac{\delta^{1/2}}{(1-\delta^{j+1-i})(1-\delta^{i-j})}+\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}\dfrac{\delta^{1/2}}{(1-\delta^{i-j})(1-\delta^{j+1-i})}+\dfrac{\delta^{1/2}(\delta+1)}{(\delta-1)^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{\delta^{3/2}(\delta^{i-1}-1)}{(\delta-1)^{2}(\delta^{i}-1)}-\dfrac{\delta^{3/2}(\delta^{n-i}-1)}{(1-\delta)^{2}(\delta^{n+1-i}-1)}+\dfrac{\delta^{1/2}(\delta+1)}{(\delta-1)^{2}}=\dfrac{\delta^{1/2}(\delta^{n+1}-1)}{(\delta-1)(\delta^{i}-1)(\delta^{n+1-i}-1)}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\delta^{(n+1)/2}-\delta^{-(n+1)/2}}{(\delta^{1/2}-\delta^{-1/2})(\delta^{i/2}-\delta^{-i/2})(\delta^{(n+1-i)/2}-\delta^{-(n+1-i)/2})}=\dfrac{K_{n}(\hat{\tau})}{(H_{2}(\hat{\tau})-2)\,K_{i-1}(\hat{\tau})\,K_{n-i}(\hat{\tau})}.

Also in subcase (C2b), a careful inspection of formulas (
74
) leads to
the assertion about isolated fixed points and fixed
(-2)
-curves.
In particular, the Möbius transformation
f|_{E_{1}}
has only one fixed point
p_{1}
on
E_{1}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
that is,
E_{1}
is a
parabolic
curve.
Recall from
[
6
, Theorem 6.3]
that if an isolated fixed point
p
lies on a parabolic curve,
then
\mu(f,p)=2
and
\nu(f,p)=(\delta+1)/(\delta-1)^{2}
.
We apply this fact to the points
p_{1}
.
Since
\gamma_{1}=1
, from definition (
69
) we have
\alpha^{n+1}=\beta^{n-1}

and hence

\gamma_{j+1}=\beta^{2j}=\delta^{j}
and
\gamma_{j}^{-1}=\beta^{2(1-j)}=\delta^{1-j}
.
Therefore, in view of definitions (
62
) and (
66a
), we obtain

\mu(f,E)=2+(n-1)=n+1
and

\hat{\nu}(f,E)=\delta^{1/2}\dfrac{\delta+1}{(\delta-1)^{2}}+\sum_{j=2}^{n}\dfrac{\delta^{1/2}}{(1-\delta^{1-j})(1-\delta^{j})}=\dfrac{K_{n}(\hat{\tau})}{(H_{2}(\hat{\tau})-2)\,K_{n-1}(\hat{\tau})},

which agrees with the second formula in (
71
) with
i=1
.
Thus Lemma
7.8
is established.

\Box

Lemma 7.9

In case
(\mathrm{C}3)
,
E
contains exactly
n+1
isolated fixed points
p_{0},\dots,p_{n}

and no fixed
(-2)
-curve, where
p_{0}
is multiple while
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
are simple,
so we have
\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}})\geq n+2
.

Proof
.
A careful inspection of formulas (
74
) shows the assertions.
In particular,
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
are simple, while
p_{0}
is multiple, as
f
has
multiplier
\gamma_{0}^{-1}=1
at
p_{0}
in a normal direction to
E_{1}
.
So we have
\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}})\geq 2+n
.
\Box

Remark 7.10

By Theorem
7.3
we always have
\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}})\geq n+1
,
where the minimum
\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}})=n+1
is attained exactly when
(\mathrm{C}3)
is not the case.
In case
(\mathrm{C}3)
the point
p_{0}
or
p_{n+1}
is an
exceptional fixed point of type

\mathrm{I\!I}
, a concept discussed
in
[
6
, §6]
, for which the holomorphic local index
\hat{\nu}(f,p)
is harder to calculate.

7.3
Other Exceptional Components

We proceed to an
f
-invariant exceptional component of type
\mathrm{A}_{n}
with reflectional symmetry.
Following Notation
7.1
, we denote it by
\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{r}}
collectively.
Moreover we denote it by
\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{r},m}
, when we consider the
m
-th iterate
f^{m}
in place of
f
.
Note that
\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{r},m}
has reflectional symmetry if
m
is odd, and trivial symmetry
if
m
is even.

Proposition 7.11

For any
f
-invariant exceptional component of type
\mathrm{A}_{n}
,
n\geq 2
,
with reflectional symmetry,

\displaystyle\mu(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{r},m})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1&\mbox{if $m$ is odd and $n$ is even},\\[2.84526pt]
2&\mbox{if $m$ is odd and $n$ is odd},\\[2.84526pt]
n+1&\mbox{if $m$ is even},\end{cases}

(75a)

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{r},m})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\dfrac{1}{H_{m}(\hat{\tau})}&\mbox{if $m$ is odd},\\[8.53581pt]
\dfrac{K_{n}(H_{m}(\hat{\tau}))}{H_{m(n+1)}(\hat{\tau})-2(-1)^{(m/2)(n+1)}}&\mbox{if $m$ is even}.\end{cases}

(75b)

Proof
.
We use Corollary
4.4
,
so we write the component
\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{r}}
also as
E
in accordance with the notation there.

First we consider the case
m=1
.
From definition (
35
) we have
f=f_{J}\circ h
, where
h|_{E}
is described
in Proposition
5.1
.
The map
f
acts on
E
essentially in the same manner as
f_{J}
.
Namely, although
f
and
f_{J}
may be slightly different on
E_{1}
and
E_{n}
, this
does not affect the current issue and Corollary
4.4
is available for
f

with
\beta=\delta^{1/2}
.
When
n=2k
is even, as in case (1) of Corollary
4.4
, the point
p_{k}

at the intersection
E_{k}\cap E_{k+1}
is the only fixed point of
f
on
E
, which
is simple and has multipliers
\pm\mathrm{i}\delta^{1/2}
(see Figure
4.2
).
Thus we have

\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{r}})=1,\qquad\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{r}})=\hat{\nu}(f,p_{k})=\frac{\delta^{1/2}}{(1-\mathrm{i}\,\delta^{1/2})(1+\mathrm{i}\,\delta^{1/2})}=\dfrac{\delta^{1/2}}{1+\delta}=\dfrac{1}{\hat{\tau}},

which verifies the case where
m=1
and
n
is even in formulas (
75a
) and (
75b
).
When
n=2k-1
is odd, as in case (2) of Corollary
4.4
, the map
f
has
exactly two fixed points
q_{\pm}
on
E
, which lie on
E_{k}
(see Figure
4.2
).
Moreover each of them is simple and has multipliers
-1
and
-\delta
.
Thus we have
\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{r}})=2
and

\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{r}})=\hat{\nu}(f,q_{+})+\hat{\nu}(f,q_{-})=\frac{\delta^{1/2}}{(1+1)(1+\delta)}+\frac{\delta^{1/2}}{(1+1)(1+\delta)}=\dfrac{\delta^{1/2}}{1+\delta}=\dfrac{1}{\hat{\tau}},

which shows the case where
m=1
and
n
is odd in (
75
).
In either case
\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{r}})
takes the same value
1/\hat{\tau}
.

Next we proceed to the case where
m
is an odd number in general.
In this case
E
is an
f^{m}
-invariant exceptional component of type
\mathrm{A}_{n}
with reflectional summetry.
So the argument in the previous paragraph can be applied to
f^{m}
in place of
f
, for which

\hat{\tau}
should be replaced by
H_{m}(\hat{\tau})
.
This proves (
75
) in this case.

Finally we consider the case where
m
is even.
In this case
E
is an
f^{m}
-invariant exceptional component of type
\mathrm{A}_{n}
with trivial symmetry,
where
\alpha=\mathrm{i}^{m}=(-1)^{m/2}
,
\beta=\delta^{m/2}
and so

\gamma_{j}=\delta^{m(2j-n-1)/2}(-1)^{m(n+1)/2}
in formula (
69
).
So, when
n
is even, we are in case (C1) of Theorem
7.3
.
Formulas (
70
) with
f
replaced by
f^{m}
yield formulas (
75a
) and (
75b
) in this case.
When
n
is odd, we are in case (C2) of Theorem
7.3
with
i=(n+1)/2
.
Note that
2\leq i\leq n-1
as
n\geq 3
.
Formulas (
71
) with
f
replaced by
f^{m}
yield

\mu(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{r},m})=n+1
and

\hat{\nu}(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{r},m})=\dfrac{K_{n}(H_{m}(\hat{\tau}))}{\{H_{2}(H_{m}(\hat{\tau}))-2\}\{K_{(n-1)/2}(H_{m}(\hat{\tau}))\}^{2}}.

Using the identity
\{H_{2}(w)-2\}\{K_{n}(w)\}^{2}=H_{2(n+1)}(w)-2
, we can observe that the last equation
is just the subcase of formula (
75b
) where
m
is even and
n
is odd.

\Box

Lemma 7.12

For any
f
-invariant exceptional component of Dynkin type
\mathrm{D}
we have

\displaystyle\mu(f,\mathrm{D}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}})

\displaystyle=n+1,\qquad

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{D}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{\tau-2}\left(\dfrac{\tau}{\hat{\tau}}-\dfrac{K_{n-4}(\hat{\tau})}{K_{n-3}(\hat{\tau})}\right),\qquad

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle\geq 4,

\displaystyle\mu(f,\mathrm{D}_{n}^{\mathrm{r}})

\displaystyle=n-1,\qquad

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{D}_{n}^{\mathrm{r}})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\left(\dfrac{1}{\hat{\tau}}+\dfrac{K_{n-3}(\hat{\tau})}{H_{n-2}(\hat{\tau})}\right),\qquad

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle\geq 4,

\displaystyle\mu(f,\mathrm{D}_{4}^{\mathrm{c}})

\displaystyle=2,\qquad

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{D}_{4}^{\mathrm{c}})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\hat{\tau}}{\tau+1}.

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle=4,

where
\tau=H_{2}(\hat{\tau})=\delta+\delta^{-1}
.
For any
f
-invariant exceptional component of Dynkin type
\mathrm{E}
we have

\displaystyle\mu(f,\mathrm{E}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}})

\displaystyle=n+1,\qquad

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{E}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{\tau-2}\left(\dfrac{\tau^{2}+\tau-1}{\hat{\tau}(\tau+1)}-\dfrac{K_{n-5}(\hat{\tau})}{K_{n-4}(\hat{\tau})}\right),\qquad

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle=6,7,8,

\displaystyle\mu(f,\mathrm{E}_{6}^{\mathrm{r}})

\displaystyle=3.\qquad

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{E}_{6}^{\mathrm{r}})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\tau+1}{\hat{\tau}\cdot\tau},\qquad

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle=6.

Proof
.
Except for the case of type
\mathrm{D}_{4}^{\mathrm{c}}
, this lemma is a reformulation of
[
6
, Lemma 5.1]

in terms of the polynomials
H_{n}(w)
and
K_{n}(w)
.
Formulas for
\mu(f,\mathrm{D}_{4}^{\mathrm{c}})
and
\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{D}_{4}^{\mathrm{c}})
are obtained by direct calculations.

\Box

Remark 7.13

Under iterations of
f
the symmetry types of an
f
-invariant exceptional component vary like

\mathrm{t}\to\mathrm{t}\to\mathrm{t}\to\cdots,\qquad\mathrm{r}\to\mathrm{t}\to\mathrm{r}\to\mathrm{t}\to\cdots,\qquad\mathrm{c}\to\mathrm{c}\to\mathrm{t}\to\mathrm{c}\to\mathrm{c}\to\mathrm{t}\to\cdots.

Formulas in Lemma
7.12
for
f^{m}
in place of
f
can be obtained by just following these patterns
and replacing
\hat{\tau}
by
H_{m}(\hat{\tau})
.
In the case of type
\mathrm{A}
with reflectional symmetry, the formulas for the iterates
f^{m}

are given in Proposition
7.11
.
The case of type
\mathrm{A}
with trivial symmetry is most intricate, but even in this case
the iteration procedure goes well with Theorem
7.3
and Remark
7.5

in a generic situation, once case
(\mathrm{C}3)
is ruled out there.

8
Method of Hypergeometric Groups

Our previous papers
[
5
,
6
]
develop a method for constructing non-projective K3
surface automorphisms of positive entropy, which we call the
method of hypergometric groups
.
Leaving full details to the original paper
[
5
]
and its amplification
[
6
]
,
we briefly survey this method.
It starts with a pair
(\varphi,\psi)
of monic polynomials of degree
22
over
\mathbb{Z}

such that
\varphi(z)
is anti-palindromic,
z^{11}\varphi(z^{-1})=-\varphi(z)
,

\psi(z)
is palindromic,
z^{11}\psi(z^{-1})=\psi(z)
, and the resultant
of
\varphi(z)
and
\psi(z)
is
\pm 1
.
Let
A
and
B
be the companion matrices of
\varphi(z)
and
\psi(z)
respectively,
so that
\varphi(z)
and
\psi(z)
are the characteristic polynomials of
A
and
B
.
Let
H=\langle A,B\rangle
be the subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{22}(\mathbb{Z})
generated by
A
and
B
.
It is a group modeled on the monodromy group of a generalized hypergeometric differential equation of order
22
.
We find that
C:=A^{-1}B\in H
is a reflection which fixes a hyperplane in
\mathbb{Q}^{22}
pointwise
and sends a nonzero vector
\mbox{\boldmath$r$}\in\mathbb{Q}^{22}
to its negative
-\mbox{\boldmath$r$}
.
We have a free
\mathbb{Z}
-module of rank
22
,

L=\langle\mbox{\boldmath$r$},A\mbox{\boldmath$r$},\dots,A^{21}\mbox{\boldmath$r$}\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}}=\langle\mbox{\boldmath$r$},B\mbox{\boldmath$r$},\dots,B^{21}\mbox{\boldmath$r$}\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}},

stable under the action of
H
.
We can make it into an
H
-invariant even unimodular lattice by providing it with
the inner product
(A^{i-1}\mbox{\boldmath$r$},\,A^{j-1}\mbox{\boldmath$r$})=\xi_{|i-j|}
, where

\xi_{0}=2
and the sequence
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is defined by the expansion

\dfrac{\psi(z)}{\varphi(z)}=1+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\xi_{i}\,z^{-i}\qquad\mbox{around}\quad z=\infty.

Turning
\varphi(z)
and
\psi(z)
upside down in the expansion gives the Gram matrix

(B^{i-1}\mbox{\boldmath$r$},\,B^{j-1}\mbox{\boldmath$r$})
for the
B
-basis.

Take
F=A
or
B
and let
\chi(z)
be the characterisctic polynomial of
F
, that is,

\chi(z):=\varphi(z)
for
F=A
and
\chi(z):=\psi(z)
for
F=B
.
A suitable choice of the pair
(\varphi,\psi)
makes the hypergeometric lattice

L
(or its negative) into an even unimodular lattice of signature
(3,19)
,
that is, a K3 lattice, equipped with the Hodge structure

L_{\mathbb{C}}=H^{1,0}\oplus H^{1,1}\oplus H^{0,2}=\ell\oplus(\ell\oplus\overline{\ell})^{\perp}\oplus\overline{\ell}\quad\mbox{of signature $(1,0)\oplus(1,19)\oplus(1,0)$},

(76)

such that
F
is a positive Hodge isometry, where
\ell
is the eigenline of
F
for the eigenvalue

\delta\in S^{1}\setminus\{\pm 1\}
and
\overline{\ell}
is the complex conjugate to
\ell
;
see
[
5
, Theorems 1.2 and 1.3]
.
The number
\delta
, which is a root of
\chi(z)
, and its trace
\tau:=\delta+\delta^{-1}
are referred to
as the
special eigenvalue
and
special trace
(ST for short) for the Hodge structure (
76
).
We remark that
\delta
is either a root of unity or a non-real conjugate to a Salem number
\lambda
.
Let us consider the Salem case.
In this case we have a factorization
\chi(z)=S(z)\cdot\chi_{1}(z)
, where
S(z)
is the minimal polynomial
of
\lambda
and
\chi_{1}(z)
is a product of cyclotomic polynomials or
1
.
The Picard lattice
\operatorname{Pic}:=L\cap H^{1,1}
is given by

\operatorname{Pic}=\{\mbox{\boldmath$v$}\in L\mid\chi_{1}(F)\mbox{\boldmath$v$}=0\}=\langle\mbox{\boldmath$s$},F\mbox{\boldmath$s$},\dots,F^{\rho-1}\mbox{\boldmath$s$}\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}},\qquad\mbox{\boldmath$s$}:=S(F)\mbox{\boldmath$r$},

with Picard number
\rho:=\deg\chi_{1}(z)=22-\deg S(z)
.
The lexicographic order on
\operatorname{Pic}
relative to the linear basis
\mbox{\boldmath$s$},\dots,F^{\rho-1}\mbox{\boldmath$s$}

gives rise to a root basis
\varDelta_{\mathrm{b}}
of the root system
\varDelta:=\{\mbox{\boldmath$u$}\in\operatorname{Pic}\mid(\mbox{\boldmath$u$},\mbox{\boldmath$u$})=-2\}
.
This together with the choice of a positive cone
\mathcal{C}^{+}\subset H^{1,1}_{\mathbb{R}}
determines a Weyl chamber

\mathcal{K}:=\{\mbox{\boldmath$v$}\in\mathcal{C}^{+}\mid\mbox{$(\mbox{\boldmath$v$},\mbox{\boldmath$u$})>0$ for any $\mbox{\boldmath$u$}\in\varDelta_{\mathrm{b}}$}\}
,
which we designate as the “Kähler cone”.
In general, the Hodge isometry
F
may not preserve
\mathcal{K}
, but there exists a unique Weyl group element

w_{F}
such that the modified Hodge isometry
\widetilde{F}:=w_{F}\circ F
preserves
\mathcal{K}
.
Let
\widetilde{\chi}(z)
be the characteristic polynomial of
\widetilde{F}
.
It has the same Salem factor
S(z)
as
\chi(z)
, decomposing into

\widetilde{\chi}(z)=S(z)\cdot\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(z),

(77)

where
\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(z)
is a product of cyclotomic polynomials or
1
,
which may differ from
\chi_{1}(z)
.
Notice that
\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(z)
is the characteristic polynomial of
\widetilde{F}|_{\operatorname{Pic}}
.
What is important here is that, once the initial pair
(\varphi,\psi)
is given concretely,
all these calculations can be carried out explicitly by computer.
In particular one can determine the Dynkin type of the root system
\varDelta
and detect how

\widetilde{F}
permutes the simple roots in
\varDelta_{\mathrm{b}}
.

Through the period mapping the modified Hodge isometry
\widetilde{F}:L\to L
lifts to a non-projective
K3 surface automorphism
f:X\to X
with entropy
h(f)=\log\lambda
, twist number
\delta(f)=\delta
,
Picard lattice
\operatorname{Pic}(X)\cong\operatorname{Pic}
, Picard number
\rho(X)=\rho
and Kähler cone

\mathcal{K}(X)\cong\mathcal{K}
; see
[
5
, Theorem 1.6]
.
The constructed automorphism
f:X\to X
satisfies conditions (A1) and (A2) in §
1
.
There exists a one-to-one correspondence between the
(-2)
-curves in
\mathcal{E}(X)
and the simple roots
in
\varDelta_{\mathrm{b}}
.
The Dynkin type of the exceptional set
\mathcal{E}(X)
is that of the root system
\varDelta
.
How the automorphism
f
permutes the
(-2)
-curves in
\mathcal{E}(X)
is faithfully
represented by the action of
\widetilde{F}
on the simple roots
\varDelta_{\mathrm{b}}
.
So the Dynkin automorphism
\sigma(f,E)
defined in (
68
) can be determined
explicitly for each
f
-invariant exceptional component
E\subset\mathcal{E}(X)
.

Among other possibilities, the article
[
6
]
employs two setups for the pair
(\varphi,\psi)
.
In
[
6
, Setup 3.2]
we put

\varphi(z)=S(z)\cdot\varphi_{1}(z)\quad\mbox{with}\quad\varphi_{1}(z)=(z-1)(z+1)\cdot C(z);\qquad\psi(z)=s(z)\cdot c(z),

(78)

where
S(z)
is a Salem polynomial and
C(z)
is a product of cyclotomic polynomials or
1
,
while
s(z)
is an unramified Salem polynomial and
c(z)
is a product of unramified
cyclotomic polynomials or
1
.
This setup works efficiently for Picard numbers
\rho=2,4,6,\dots,16
, that is, for

d:=\deg S(z)=20,18,16,\dots,6
, but not for
\rho=18
i.e.
d=4
.
To cope with the last case, we introduce
[
6
, Setup 3.4]
, where
\varphi(z)
is
the same as in (
78
), but
S(z)
is specified as
\mathrm{S}^{(4)}_{1}(z)=z^{4}-z^{3}-z^{2}-z+1
, the minimal polynomial of the smallest Salem number

\lambda^{(4)}_{1}\approx 1.7220838
of degree
4
(see Notation
8.1
),
while
\psi(z)
is an unramified palindromic polynomial of the form

\psi(z)=z^{22}+c_{1}z^{21}+\cdots+c_{10}z^{12}+c_{11}z^{11}+c_{10}z^{10}+\cdots+c_{1}z+1\in\mathbb{Z}[z],

(79)

such that
c_{j}\in\{0,\pm 1,\pm 2\}
for
j=1,\dots,9
,
the trace polynomial of
\psi(z)
has ten or eight roots on
(-2,\,2)
, and the
resultant of
\psi(z)
with
\mathrm{S}^{(4)}_{1}(z)
is
\pm 1
; there are
1019
polynomials of them.
In both setups the Hodge isometry
F
is the matrix
A
, so the polynomials
\widetilde{\chi}(z)

and
\widetilde{\chi}_{1}(z)
in (
77
) are expressed as
\widetilde{\varphi}(z)
and

\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}(z)
.
These setups give reise to a lot of K3 surface automorphisms of positive entropy,
which are listed in our database
[
7
]
.

We shall examine which of the automorphisms in
[
7
]
have rotation domains of rank
1
or of rank
2
.

Notation 8.1

Let
\lambda^{(d)}_{i}
be the
i
-th smallest Salem number of degree
d\geq 4
,
where
d
is even.
We denote by
\mathrm{S}^{(d)}_{i}(z)
the minimal polynomial of
\lambda^{(d)}_{i}
and by

\mathrm{ST}^{(d)}_{i}(w)
the trace polynomial of
\mathrm{S}^{(d)}_{i}(z)
.
The roots
\tau_{j}
of
\mathrm{ST}^{(d)}_{i}(w)
are arranged in decreasing order as

\tau_{0}>2>\tau_{1}>\tau_{2}>\dots>\tau_{d/2-1}>-2
.
To be more precise,
\tau_{j}
should be written
\tau_{i,j}^{(d)}
,
but the dependence upon
(d,i)
is omitted for simplicity of notation.
The
i
-th cyclotomic polynomial is denoted by
\mathrm{C}_{i}(z)
.
The unusual convention (
47
) for
\mathrm{C}_{1}(z)
and
\mathrm{C}_{2}(z)
is not employed here, so

\mathrm{C}_{1}(z)=z-1
,
\mathrm{C}_{2}(z)=z+1
,
\mathrm{C}_{3}(z)=z^{2}+z+1
,
\mathrm{C}_{4}(z)=z^{2}+1
, and so forth.

Remark 8.2

Recall that
\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}(z)
is the characteristic polynomial of
\widetilde{A}|_{\operatorname{Pic}}
.
Let
\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}(z)
denote the characteristic polynomial of
\widetilde{A}|_{\operatorname{Span}\varDelta_{\mathrm{b}}}
.
Notice that
\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}(z)
is divisible by
\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}(z)
, since
\operatorname{Span}\varDelta_{\mathrm{b}}\subset\operatorname{Pic}
.
If
\varDelta_{\mathrm{b}}
is partitioned into
\widetilde{A}
-invariant subsets
\varDelta_{\mathrm{b}}^{(1)},\varDelta_{\mathrm{b}}^{(2)},\dots
,
then
\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}(z)=\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}^{(1)}(z)\,\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}^{(2)}(z)\cdots
, where

\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}^{(i)}(z)
is the characteristic polynomial of
\widetilde{A}|_{\operatorname{Span}\varDelta_{\mathrm{b}}^{(i)}}
.
Moreover, if
\widetilde{A}
acts on
\varDelta_{\mathrm{b}}^{(i)}
as a permutation of cycle type
(m,n,\dots)
, then

\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}^{(i)}(z)=(z^{m}-1)(z^{n}-1)\cdots=\prod_{i|m}\mathrm{C}_{i}(z)\prod_{j|n}\mathrm{C}_{j}(z)\cdots.

In some typical cases, the factor
\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}^{(i)}(z)
is calculated in the following manner.

•

If all elements of
\varDelta_{\mathrm{b}}^{(i)}
are fixed, then
\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}^{(i)}=\mathrm{C}_{1}^{k}
where
k
is the cardinality of the set
\varDelta_{\mathrm{b}}^{(i)}
.

•

If
\varDelta_{\mathrm{b}}^{(i)}
is a root basis of type
\mathrm{A}_{n}
,
n\geq 2
, with reflectional symmetry, then

\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}^{(i)}=\mathrm{C}_{1}^{\lceil n/2\rceil}\mathrm{C}_{2}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
.

•

If
\varDelta_{\mathrm{b}}^{(i)}
is a root basis of type
\mathrm{D}_{n}
,
n\geq 4
, with reflectional symmetry, then
\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}^{(i)}=\mathrm{C}_{1}^{n-1}\mathrm{C}_{2}
.

•

If
\varDelta_{\mathrm{b}}^{(i)}
is a root basis of type
\mathrm{D}_{4}
with tricyclic symmetry, then
\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}^{(i)}=\mathrm{C}_{1}^{2}\mathrm{C}_{3}
.

•

If
\varDelta_{\mathrm{b}}^{(i)}
is a root basis of type
\mathrm{E}_{6}
with reflectional symmetry, then
\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}^{(i)}=\mathrm{C}_{1}^{4}\mathrm{C}_{2}^{2}
.

For example, if
\varDelta_{\mathrm{b}}^{(i)}
is a root basis of type
\mathrm{E}_{7}
or
\mathrm{E}_{8}
, then we have
\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}^{(i)}=\mathrm{C}_{1}^{7}
or
\mathrm{C}_{1}^{8}

as one of the top cases.

8.1
Components of Type A with Trivial Symmetry

A lot of K3 surface automorphisms of positive entropy created by the method of hypergeometric groups
are presented in our database
[
7
]
.
It contains many automorphisms
f:X\to X
having an
f
-invariant
exceptional component of type
\mathrm{A}
with trivial symmetry.
Among them, Table
8.1
focuses on those automorphisms
which have
no fixed points off
the exceptional set
\mathcal{E}(X)
.
Our previous article
[
6
]
employs two setups in applying the method of hypergeometric groups.
Table
8.1
arises from one of them, that is, from
[

, Setup 3.2]
.
For the format of Table
8.1
we refer to its legend, except for one thing
in the last column; by RH we mean “Rotation domain or Hyperbolic set”, but
we postpone the details of its meaning until §
9
(see Theorem
9.7
).

\mathrm{Hy}

As in formula (
78
) the pair
(\varphi,\psi)
is given by
\varphi(z)=(z-1)(z+1)\,S(z)\,C(z)

and
\psi(z)=s(z)\,c(z)
, where
S(z)
and
s(z)
are Salem polynomials,
while
C(z)
and
c(z)
are products of cyclotomic polynomials;

\rho
is the Picard number of
X
; ST is the special trace for the Hodge structure (
76
);
Dynkin column exhibits the Dynkin type of the root system
\varDelta
or of the exceptional set
\mathcal{E}(X)
;

\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}(z)
is the cyclotomic component of the characteristic polynomial

\widetilde{\varphi}(z)=S(z)\,\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}(z)
of
\widetilde{A}:=w_{A}\circ A
or of
f^{*}|_{H^{2}(X,\mathbb{C})}
;

L(f)
is the Lefschetz number of
f
, which is equal to
2+\operatorname{Tr}\widetilde{A}
; see §
9
for RH-column.

Table 8.1:
*

Every exceptional component appearing in Table
8.1
is
f
-invariant, because for each entry
of the table the exceptional set
\mathcal{E}(X)
contains no more than one exceptional components of the same Dynkin type.
We establish the main properties of the automorphisms in Table
8.1
in the following proposition.

Proposition 8.3

(Table
8.1
)

In No.
27
the
\mathrm{A}_{6}
-component and the
\mathrm{E}_{6}
-component have reflectional symmetry.
Any other exceptional component of type
\mathrm{A}
has trivial symmetry, where case
(\mathrm{C}3)

never occurs in Theorem
7.3
.
Every automorphism
f
in the table has no fixed point off the exceptional set
\mathcal{E}(X)
,
that is,
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=0
.

Proof
.
We use Notation
7.1
.
It is evident that any
\mathrm{A}_{1}
-component has trivial symmetry, that is,
\mathrm{A}_{1}=\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\mathrm{t}}
.
First we consider No.
27
, which has exceptional set of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}\oplus\mathrm{A}_{6}\oplus\mathrm{E}_{6}
.
By Remark
7.10
, Proposition
7.11
and Lemma
7.12
we have
\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})\geq 2
,

\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{6})\geq 1
and
\mu(f,\mathrm{E}_{6})\geq 3
, so by definition (
63
) we have

\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})+\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{6})+\mu(f,\mathrm{E}_{6})\geq 2+1+3=6
.
FPF (
64
) then implies
0\leq\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=L(f)-\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)\leq 6-6=0
.
This forces
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=0
and hence
\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})=2
,
\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{6})=1
and
\mu(f,\mathrm{E}_{6})=3
.
Remark
7.10
, Proposition
7.11
and Lemma
7.12
again show that case
(\mathrm{C}3)
in
Theorem
7.3
does not occur for
\mathrm{A}_{1}
and we have
\mathrm{A}_{6}=\mathrm{A}_{6}^{\mathrm{r}}
and
\mathrm{E}_{6}=\mathrm{E}_{6}^{\mathrm{r}}
.
Next we pick out No.
29
, which has exceptional set of type
\mathrm{A}_{11}
.
By Remark
8.2
, if
\mathrm{A}_{11}=\mathrm{A}_{11}^{\mathrm{t}}
then
\tilde{\varphi}_{2}=\mathrm{C}_{1}^{11}
,
while if
\mathrm{A}_{11}=\mathrm{A}_{11}^{\mathrm{r}}
then
\tilde{\varphi}_{2}=\mathrm{C}_{1}^{6}\mathrm{C}_{2}^{5}
.
Since
\tilde{\varphi}_{2}
divides
\tilde{\varphi}_{1}=\mathrm{C}_{1}^{11}\mathrm{C}_{2}\mathrm{C}_{4}
, we must have
\mathrm{A}_{11}=\mathrm{A}_{11}^{\mathrm{t}}
.
So, by definition (
63
) and Remark
7.10
, we have
\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{11}^{\mathrm{t}})\geq 12
.
FPF (
64
) then implies
0\leq\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=L(f)-\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)\leq 12-12=0
,
which forces
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=0
and
\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{11}^{\mathrm{t}})=12
.
By Remark
7.10
case
(\mathrm{C}3)
in Theorem
7.3
does not occur for
\mathrm{A}_{11}^{\mathrm{t}}
.
The other entries can be treated in similar or simpler manners.

\Box

The format of this table is the same as that of Table
8.1
except for the last column,
which gives the cardinality
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)
counted with multiplicities of the fixed points of
f
off the exceptional set
\mathcal{E}(X)
;

of this table is the same as No.​

of Table
8.1
.

Table 8.2:
*

Proposition 8.4

(Table
8.2
)

Every
\mathrm{A}_{n}
-component with
n\geq 2
appearing in Table
8.2
has reflectional symmetry.
The
\mathrm{D}_{11}
-component in No.​
21
and the
\mathrm{E}_{6}
-component in No.​
28
also have reflectional symmetries.
The cardinality
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)
counted with multiplicities of the fixed points off
\mathcal{E}(X)
is given in the last column of the table.

Proof
.
We continue to use Notation
7.1
.
First, we consider No.
21
, which has exceptional set of type
\mathrm{A}_{2}\oplus\mathrm{D}_{11}
.
By Remark
7.10
, Proposition
7.11
and Lemma
7.12
we have
\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{2})\geq 1
and
\mu(f,\mathrm{D}_{11})\geq 10
,
so by definition (
63
) we have
\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{2})+\mu(f,\mathrm{D}_{11})\geq 1+10=11
.
FPF (
64
) then implies
0\leq\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=L(f)-\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)\leq 11-11=0
.
This forces
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=0
and hence
\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{2})=1
and
\mu(f,\mathrm{D}_{11})=10
.
Again by Remark
7.10
, Proposition
7.11
and Lemma
7.12
we have
\mathrm{A}_{2}=\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{r}}
and
\mathrm{D}_{11}=\mathrm{D}_{11}^{\mathrm{r}}
.
Secondly, No.
28
of Table
8.2
is the same as No.
27
of Table
8.1
, which has already been discussed in Proposition
8.3
.
Thirdly, we pick out Nos.
22
–
25
, which has exceptional set of type
\mathrm{A}_{2}
.
By Remark
8.2
, if
\mathrm{A}_{2}=\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{t}}
then
\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}=\mathrm{C}_{1}^{2}
,
while if
\mathrm{A}_{2}=\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{r}}
then
\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}=\mathrm{C}_{1}\mathrm{C}_{2}
.
Since
\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}
divides
\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}=\mathrm{C}_{1}\mathrm{C}_{2}\mathrm{C}_{21}
, we must have
\mathrm{A}_{2}=\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{r}}
,
and hence
\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{r}})=1
by Proposition
7.11
.
FPF (
64
) then yields
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=L(f)-\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=4-1=3
.
The remaining entries can be treated in similar manners, except the determination of
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)
in Nos.
1
and
2
.
In these entries FPF (
64
) and formula (
75a
) imply

4=L(f)=\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)+\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)\geq\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})+\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{r}})=\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})+1
,
which means
\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})\leq 3
.
Since
\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})\geq 2
by Remark
7.10
, we have
\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})=2
or
3
, so

\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=3
or
4
, and hence
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=1
or
0
, respectively.
In fact, the case of
\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})=2
and
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=1
actually occurs, but the proof of
this fact requires much space, so it is left to Lemma
A.2
in Appendix
A
.

\Box

[

and are contained in database
[

]
.
A similar proof to that of Proposition
8.4
establishes Table
8.3
.

8.3
Components of Type D or E

Our database
[
7
]
also contains a lot of K3 surface automorphisms
f:X\to X
having an
f
-invariant
exceptional component of type
\mathrm{D}
or
\mathrm{E}
, too many to be presented here.
We restrict our attention to those which have
only one (simple) fixed point off
the exceptional set
\mathcal{E}(X)
,
i.e.
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=1
.
Tables
8.4
and
8.5
give such examples.

Table 8.4:
*

For some entries of Table
8.4
the exceptional set
\mathcal{E}(X)
contains more than
one exceptional components of the same Dynkin type; for example, two
\mathrm{A}_{1}
-components in Nos.
8
–
12

and three
\mathrm{D}_{5}
-components in No.
67
.
Such components are
f
-invariant as a whole, but they may be permuted by
f
.
It is obvious that every component of type
\mathrm{E}_{7}
or
\mathrm{E}_{8}
in Table
8.4
has trivial symmetry.
With these remarks we have the following results.

Proposition 8.5

(Table
8.4
)

In Nos.
1
–
7
the
\mathrm{D}_{6}
-component has reflectional symmetry.
In Nos.
8
–
12
the two
\mathrm{A}_{1}
-components are swapped, while the
\mathrm{D}_{4}
-component has tricyclic symmetry.
In Nos.
31
–
38
the
\mathrm{D}_{9}
-component has reflectional symmetry.
In Nos.
39
–
47
the two
\mathrm{E}_{6}
-components are swapped.
In Nos.
48
–
53
the
\mathrm{D}_{13}
-component has reflectional symmetry.
In Nos.
54
–
56
the
\mathrm{D}_{14}
-component has reflectional symmetry.
In No.
61
the
\mathrm{D}_{5}
-component has reflectioanl symmetry.
In Nos.
62
and
63
the two
\mathrm{D}_{4}
-components are swapped.
In Nos.
64
and
65
the
\mathrm{D}_{16}
-component has trivial symmetry.
In No.
66
the three
\mathrm{A}_{1}
-components are cyclically permuted, while the
\mathrm{E}_{6}
-component has reflectional symmetry.
In No.
67
the three
\mathrm{D}_{5}
-components are cyclically permuted.
Every entry of the table has exactly one fixed point, which is simple, off the exceptional set
\mathcal{E}(X)
, that is,
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=1
.

Proof
.
First let us consider Nos.
8
–
12
, which have exceptional set of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\oplus 2}\oplus\mathrm{D}_{4}
.
Suppose that the two
\mathrm{A}_{1}
-components are
f
-invariant.
By Remark
7.10
and Lemma
7.12
we have

\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})\geq 2
and
\mu(f,\mathrm{D}_{4})\geq 2
, so by definition (
63
) we have

\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})+\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})+\mu(f,\mathrm{D}_{4})\geq 2+2+2=6
.
FPF (
64
) then implies

0\leq\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=L(f)-\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)\leq 2+1-6=-3
, a contradiction.
Thus the two
\mathrm{A}_{1}
-components must be swapped and the
\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\oplus 2}
-part contributes to

\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}
by
\mathrm{C}_{1}\mathrm{C}_{2}
, where
\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}
is defined in Remark
8.2
.
If
\mathrm{D}_{4}=\mathrm{D}_{4}^{\mathrm{t}}
then
\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}=\mathrm{C}_{1}\mathrm{C}_{2}\cdot\mathrm{C}_{1}^{4}=\mathrm{C}_{1}^{5}\mathrm{C}_{2}
;
if
\mathrm{D}_{4}=\mathrm{D}_{4}^{\mathrm{r}}
then
\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}=\mathrm{C}_{1}\mathrm{C}_{2}\cdot\mathrm{C}_{1}^{3}\mathrm{C}_{2}=\mathrm{C}_{1}^{4}\mathrm{C}_{2}^{2}
;
if
\mathrm{D}_{4}=\mathrm{D}_{4}^{\mathrm{c}}
then
\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}=\mathrm{C}_{1}\mathrm{C}_{2}\cdot\mathrm{C}_{1}^{2}\mathrm{C}_{3}=\mathrm{C}_{1}^{3}\mathrm{C}_{2}\mathrm{C}_{3}
.
Since
\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}
divides
\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}=\mathrm{C}_{1}^{3}\mathrm{C}_{2}\mathrm{C}_{3}
, we must have
\mathrm{D}_{4}=\mathrm{D}_{4}^{\mathrm{c}}

and hence
\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=\mu(f,\mathrm{D}_{4}^{\mathrm{c}})=2
by Lemma
7.12
.
FPF (
64
) now gives
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=L(f)-\mu(f,\mathrm{D}_{4}^{\mathrm{c}})=2+1-2=1
.
Secondly we look at Nos.
39
–
47
, which have exceptional set of type
\mathrm{E}_{6}^{\oplus 2}
.
If the two
\mathrm{E}_{6}
-components are
f
-invariant, then
\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=\mu(f,\mathrm{E}_{6})+\mu(f,\mathrm{E}_{6})\geq 3+3=6

and hence
0\leq\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=L(f)-\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)\leq 2-1-6=-5
, a contradiction.
So the two
\mathrm{E}_{6}
-components must be swapped, hence
\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=0
and

\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=L(f)-\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=2-1-0=1
.
Thirdly we consider No.
66
, which has exceptional set of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\oplus 3}\oplus\mathrm{E}_{6}
.
If at least one of the three
\mathrm{A}_{1}
-components is
f
-invariant, then

\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)\geq\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})+\mu(f,\mathrm{E}_{6})\geq 2+3=5
and hence

0\leq\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=L(f)-\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)\leq 2+2-5=-1
, a contradiction.
Thus the three
\mathrm{A}_{1}
-components must be cyclically permuted and
\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=\mu(f,\mathrm{E}_{6})
.
If
\mathrm{E}_{6}=\mathrm{E}_{6}^{\mathrm{t}}
then
\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=7
and
0\leq\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=L(f)-\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)\leq 2+2-7=-3
,
which is absurd.
Hence
\mathrm{E}_{6}=\mathrm{E}_{6}^{\mathrm{r}}
and
\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=3
, so
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=2+2-3=1
.
The remaining entries can be treated in similar or simpler manners.

\Box

which indicates that the unique fixed point off
