Proposition 8.6

(Table
8.5
)

If the two
\mathrm{A}_{2}
-components in
\mathcal{E}(X)
are denoted by the diagram

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{2,}
\textstyle{1^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{2^{\prime},}

then the automorphism
f:X\to X
induces the cyclic permutation
(1,1^{\prime},2,2^{\prime})
and, in particular,
swaps the two
\mathrm{A}_{2}
-components.
The
\mathrm{E}_{6}
-component has reflectional symmetry and, obviously, the
\mathrm{E}_{8}
-component has trivial symmetry.
The map
f
has exactly one fixed point off
\mathcal{E}(X)
, which is simple, i.e.
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=1
.

Proof
. From Lemma
7.12
we have
\mu(f,\mathrm{E}_{6})\geq 3
and
\mu(f,\mathrm{E}_{8})=9
.
If each of the two
\mathrm{A}_{2}
-components is
f
-invariant, then

\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\oplus 2})=\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{2})+\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{2})\geq 2+2=4
by Remark
7.10
,
so
\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\oplus 2})+\mu(f,\mathrm{E}_{6})+\mu(f,\mathrm{E}_{8})\geq 4+3+9=16
,
which leads to a contradiction
0\leq\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=L(f)-\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)\leq 13-16=-3
by FPF (
64
).
Thus
f
swaps the two
\mathrm{A}_{2}
-components.
If
\mathrm{E}_{6}=\mathrm{E}_{6}^{\mathrm{t}}
, then
\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=\mu(f,\mathrm{E}_{6}^{\mathrm{t}})+\mu(f,\mathrm{E}_{8})=7+9=16

by Lemma
7.12
, and hence
0\leq\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=L(f)-\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=13-16=-3
,
again a contradiction.
Thus
\mathrm{E}_{6}=\mathrm{E}_{6}^{\mathrm{r}}
and
\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=\mu(f,\mathrm{E}_{6}^{\mathrm{r}})+\mu(f,\mathrm{E}_{8})=3+9=12
,
so
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=L(f)-\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=13-12=1
.
By Remark
8.2
the
\mathrm{E}_{6}^{\mathrm{r}}
-component and
\mathrm{E}_{8}
-component contribute to
the polynomial
\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}
by
\mathrm{C}_{1}^{4}\mathrm{C}_{2}^{2}
and
\mathrm{C}_{1}^{8}
respectively.
If
f
acts on the
\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\oplus 2}
-part by cycle type
(1,1^{\prime})(2,2^{\prime})
, then the contribution of

\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\oplus 2}
to
\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}
is
(\mathrm{C}_{1}\mathrm{C}_{2})^{2}
, so we have

\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}=(\mathrm{C}_{1}\mathrm{C}_{2})^{2}\cdot\mathrm{C}_{1}^{4}\mathrm{C}_{2}^{2}\cdot\mathrm{C}_{1}^{8}=\mathrm{C}_{1}^{14}\mathrm{C}_{2}^{4}
,
but this does not divide
\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}=\mathrm{C}_{1}^{13}\mathrm{C}_{2}^{3}\mathrm{C}_{4}
, yet another contradicition.
Hence the cycle type on the
\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\oplus 2}
-part must be
(1,1^{\prime},2,2^{\prime})
.

\Box

9
Rotation Domains

In this and next sections, putting together all what we have developed so far,
we shall construct a lot of K3 surface automorphisms of positive entropy having
rotation domains of rank
1
and/or of rank
2
.
Various particular examples from §
8
, manipulated by general theories
developed in §§
2
–
7
, are most important.

9.1
Reductions to Linear Models

Detecting rotation domains or hyperbolic sets (or points) often relies on
reduction to linear models.
We begin with the simplest linear models around a fixed point (Lemma
9.1
)
and proceed to linear models around an exceptional component (Lemma
9.2
).
A whole space that is a rotation domain is called a
rotation space
.

Lemma 9.1

Let
\mbox{\boldmath$c$}=(c_{1},c_{2})\in(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2}
be a multiplier pair such that
|c_{1}c_{2}|=1
.
For the linear map

\mathbb{C}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}^{2},\quad(z_{1},\,z_{2})\mapsto(c_{1}z_{1},\,c_{2}z_{2}),

(80)

(\mathrm{R}1)

if
|c_{1}|=|c_{2}|=1
,
c
is MD and
c_{1}c_{2}
is not a root of unity, then
\mathbb{C}^{2}
is a rotation space of rank
1
,

(\mathrm{R}2)

if
|c_{1}|=|c_{2}|=1
and
c
is MI, then
\mathbb{C}^{2}
is a rotation space of rank
2
,

(\mathrm{Hy})

if
|c_{1}|=|c_{2}|^{-1}\neq 1
, then the origin
(0,0)
is a hyperbolic fixed point and the coordinate axes are
the stable and unstable curves flowing into and emanating from the origin respectively.

Proof
.
Assertions
(\mathrm{R}1)
and
(\mathrm{R}2)
follow from the definition of a rotation domain and an
elementary result in Diophantine approximations, while
(\mathrm{Hy})
is trivial from the definition of a hyperbolic fixed point.

\Box

For a nontrivial finite subgroup
G
of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
, let
(Y,E)
denote the minimal
resolution of the quotient singularity
\mathbb{C}^{2}/G
constructed in §
3
, where
E
is the exceptional set in
Y
.
It was denoted by
(X,E)
in §§
3
–
5
, but in this section
(X,E)

is reserved for the pair of a K3 surface
X
and an exceptional component
E
on
X
.
The two sets
E\subset X
and
E\subset Y
are often identified,
so using the same symbol
E
would not be confusing.
Given a normalizer
J\in N_{\operatorname{GL}}
of
G
whose determinant has modulus
1
,
we consider its linear model map
f_{J}:(Y,E)\to(Y,E)
discussed in §§
4
–
5
.
We assume that
J
is diagonalizable when
G
is a group of order
2
, so that

J
is diagonalizable in any case by Theorem
2.9
.
We denote the eigenvalues of
J
by
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2}
.

Lemma 9.2

For the map
f_{J}:(Y,E)\to(Y,E)
mentioned above the following assertions hold.

(\mathrm{i})

When
G
is a group of Dynkin type
\mathrm{A}_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\sigma_{J}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathrm{A}_{n})
is trivial,

(\mathrm{R}1)

if
|\alpha_{1}|=|\alpha_{2}|=1
,
\alpha
is MD and
\det J
is not a root of unity, then
Y
is a rotation space of rank
1
,

(\mathrm{R}2)

if
|\alpha_{1}|=|\alpha_{2}|=1
and
\alpha
is MI, then
Y
is a rotation space of rank
2
,

(\mathrm{Hy})

if
|\alpha_{1}|=|\alpha_{2}|^{-1}\neq 1
, then
E
is a hyperbolic set to the effect that a stable curve flows into
E
and
an unstable curve emanates from
E
.

(\mathrm{ii})

Suppose that
\det J
is not a root of unity.
Then
Y
is a rotation space of rank
1
in the following cases:

(\mathrm{a})

G
is a group of Dynkin type
\mathrm{A}_{n}
,
n\geq 2
, and
\sigma_{J}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathrm{A}_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is nontrivial;

(\mathrm{b})

G
is a group of Dynkin type
\mathrm{D}
or
\mathrm{E}
, in which case
\sigma_{J}
may be arbitrary.

Proof
.
Assertion
(\mathrm{i})
.
We express the eigenvalues of
J
as
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(\beta\alpha,\,\beta\alpha^{-1})
.
Since
J
is diagonalizable, we may assume that
J
is of the form (
26
) in Lemma
4.2
,
so
f_{J}
is given by formula (
27
).
This means that on the chart
W_{i}\cong\mathbb{C}^{2}
the map
f_{J}
takes the normal form (
80
)
with
c_{1}:=\beta^{2i-n-1}\alpha^{n+1}
and
c_{2}:=\beta^{n+3-2i}\alpha^{-n-1}
.
Notice that
(c_{1},c_{2})
is MI, if and only if
(\alpha,\beta)
is MI, if and only if
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
is MI.
Notice also that
c_{1}c_{2}=\beta^{2}=\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}=\det J
.
Thus assertions
(\mathrm{R}1)
and
(\mathrm{R}2)
of Lemma
9.2
follow from those of Lemma
9.1
.
In case
(\mathrm{Hy})
, since
|\det J|=|\beta|^{2}=1
, we have
|\alpha|\neq 1
.
By Lemma
4.2
, if
|\alpha|>1
then
E_{0}
is the stable curve flowing into
E
at
p_{0}
with multiplier
(\beta/\alpha)^{n+1}
,
and
E_{n+1}
is the unstable curve emanating from
E
at
p_{n}
with multiplier
(\beta\alpha)^{n+1}
.
If
|\alpha|<1
, the stable and unstable curves alternate.
This gives assertion
(\mathrm{Hy})
.

Assertion
(\mathrm{ii})
.
To address the present issue we may replace
f_{J}
by a power of
f_{J}
.
In case (a) the matrix
J
is of the form (
29
) in Lemma
4.3
, so
J^{2}
takes the form
(
26
) with
\alpha=-1
and
\beta^{2}
in replace of
\beta
in Lemma
4.2
.
Now case
(\mathrm{R}1)
of assertion
(\mathrm{i})
can be applied to
(f_{J})^{2}=f_{J^{2}}
to have the conclusion.
In case (b) we may assume
\sigma_{J}=1
by replacing
J
with
J^{2}
or
J^{3}
according to
whether
\sigma_{J}
has order
2
or
3
.
Recall from Figure
3.1
that the exceptional set
E
consists of the core
E_{0}
and three arms

\mathrm{E}^{\nu}
,
\nu=\mathrm{e},\mathrm{f},\mathrm{v}
.
By Lemma
4.6
, case
(\mathrm{R}1)
of Lemma
9.1
applies
with
c_{1}:=(\det J)^{-i}
and
c_{2}:=(\det J)^{i+1}
in a local chart around the fixed point

p_{i}^{\nu}
,
0\leq i\leq n^{\nu}
.

\Box

We turn our attention to a K3 surface automorphism
f:X\to X
satisfying
conditions (A1) and (A2) in §
1
.
Given an
f
-invariant exceptional component
E\subset\mathcal{E}(X)
, it is interesting
to ask whether and when
E
falls into one of the cases
(\mathrm{R}1),(\mathrm{R}2),(\mathrm{Hy})

in Problem
1.1
.
Our answer is the following result.

Theorem 9.3

Let
E
be an
f
-invariant exceptional component.
If
E
is of type
\mathrm{A}
with reflectional symmetry, or if
E
is of type
\mathrm{D}
or
\mathrm{E}

with arbitrary symmetry, then
E
always falls into case
(\mathrm{R}1)
.
On the other hand, when
E
is of type
\mathrm{A}
with trivial symmetry,
all of the three cases
(\mathrm{R}1),(\mathrm{R}2),(\mathrm{Hy})
can actually occur.

Proof
.
If
E
is of type
\mathrm{A}
with reflectional symmetry, or if
E
is of type
\mathrm{D}
or
\mathrm{E}
,
then Theorem
5.4
says that the map
f
is equivariantly linearizable to
its linear model
f_{J}
.
Now the first half of the theorem follows from part
(\mathrm{ii})
of Lemma
9.2
.
To prove the second half of the theorem, we put the idea in Remark
7.4
into action.

Let
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
be the multipliers of
E
.
Using FPFs (
64
) and (
67
), we find a rational function

\hat{P}(w)\in\mathbb{Q}(w)
that satisfies equation (
72
), from which
we proceed to the function
Q(w)
in formula (
45
).
We use Theorem
6.3
to confirm that the
(n+1)
-st power

\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{n+1}=(\alpha_{1}^{n+1},\,\alpha_{2}^{n+1})
is MI, or MD but NR, hence
so is
\alpha
itself.
It follow from Theorem
2.4
that
F
is
G
-equivariantly linearizable to
J
,
where
F
is the function in diagram (
34
).
Namely,
f
is equivariantly linearizable to its linear model
f_{J}
.
We apply part
(\mathrm{i})
of Lemma
9.2
to
f_{J}
and conclude that

E
falls in case
(\mathrm{R}2)
if
\alpha
is MI and
Q(\tau)\in(-2,\,2)
;
in case
(\mathrm{Hy})
if
\alpha
is MI and
Q(\tau)>2
; and in case
(\mathrm{R}1)
if

\alpha
is MD but NR, respectively, where
\tau=\delta+\delta^{-1}
is the special trace of
f
.
This scenario works well with the K3 surface automorphisms in Table
8.1
,
realizing all the three cases
(\mathrm{R}1),(\mathrm{R}2),(\mathrm{Hy})
.
Details of these contents are presented in Theorem
9.7
and its proof.
The second half of the theorem is established in this manner.
\Box

Example 9.4

Tables
8.2
and
8.3
present K3 surface automorphisms having an exceptional component of type
\mathrm{A}

with reflectional symmetry, while Tables
8.4
and
8.5
exhibit ones having exceptional components of
type
\mathrm{D}
or
\mathrm{E}
.
By Theorem
9.3
each of these components is contained in a (periodic) rotation domain of rank
1
.

9.2
Detecting Rotation Domains

We are now in a position to detect rotation domains around an exceptional
component of type
\mathrm{A}
with trivial symmetry, by using examples from Table
8.1
.
The main result in this direction is Theorem
9.7
.
In order to deal with most entries in Table
8.1
in a unified manner, we begin
by the following lemma.

Lemma 9.5

Suppose that
\mathcal{E}(X)
consists of only one exceptional component of type
\mathrm{A}_{n}

with trivial symmetry and that
f
has no fixed point off
\mathcal{E}(X)
.
Then in case
(\mathrm{C}1)
of Theorem
7.3
we have

\alpha^{n+1}+\alpha^{-(n+1)}=\hat{P}_{n}(\hat{\tau}),\qquad\hat{P}_{n}(w):=H_{n+1}(w)-w^{-1}K_{n}(w),

(81)

where
\hat{\tau}:=\delta^{1/2}+\delta^{-1/2}
.
In case
(\mathrm{C}2)
the number
\hat{\tau}
must be a root of the algebraic equation

w\{H_{2}(w)-2\}K_{i-1}(w)K_{n-i}(w)-K_{n}(w)=0,

(82)

where
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
is the index appearing in the statement of case
(\mathrm{C}2)

in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{ss:linec}
.

Proof
.
Following Notation
7.1
we denote the unique exceptional component by
\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}}
.
Since
f
has no fixed point off
\mathcal{E}(X)
and only one exceptional component
\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}}
,
we have
\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=0
and FPF (
67
) reads
\hat{\tau}=\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}})
.
In case (C1) formula (
70
) in Theorem
7.3
allows us to rewrite the equation
\hat{\tau}=\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}})

in the form (
81
).
Similarly, in case
(\mathrm{C}2)
formula (
71
) recasts
\hat{\tau}=\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}})

into equation (
82
).
\Box

Remark
7.2
allows us to express the function
\hat{P}_{n}(w)
in (
81
) in the
form (
44
) with
m=n+1
, that is,

\hat{P}_{n}(w)=P_{n}(H_{2}(w))\quad\mbox{when $n$ is odd};\qquad\hat{P}_{n}(w)=w^{-1}P_{n}(H_{2}(w))\quad\mbox{when $n$ is even},

for some polynomial
P_{n}(w)
.
Explicitly, according to the parity of
n
, we have

P_{2j-1}(w)=H_{j}(w)-K_{j-1}(w),\qquad P_{2j}(w)=(w+1)K_{j}(w)-(w+3)K_{j-1}(w),\qquad j\in\mathbb{N}.

Similarly, the algebraic equation (
82
) for
\hat{\tau}
can be converted to an algebraic equation

R^{(n)}_{i}(w)=0
for
\tau:=H_{2}(\hat{\tau})=\delta+\delta^{-1}
.
Explicit formulas for
R^{(n)}_{i}(w)
exhibit four patterns depending on the parities of
n
and
i
,
which are somewhat too lengthy to be included here.
Instead, some examples of
R^{(n)}_{i}(w)
are given in Example
9.6
.
Due to the symmetry
R^{(n)}_{i}(w)=R^{(n)}_{n+1-i}(w)
, the index
i
may vary within
1\leq i\leq(n+1)/2
.

Example 9.6

Entries in Table
8.1
admit exceptional components of type
\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}}
for
n=1,2,6,7,11
.
For those values of
n
we illustrate the polynomials mentioned above.
Regarding the polynomials
P_{n}(w)
we find

\displaystyle P_{1}(w)

\displaystyle=w-1,\qquad P_{2}(w)=w^{2}-3,\qquad

\displaystyle P_{6}(w)

\displaystyle=w^{4}-5w^{2}-w+3,

\displaystyle P_{7}(w)

\displaystyle=w^{4}-w^{3}-4w^{2}+2w+2,\qquad

\displaystyle P_{11}(w)

\displaystyle=w^{6}-w^{5}-6w^{4}+4w^{3}+9w^{2}-3w-2.

For
n=1,2,6,7
, the polynomials
R^{(n)}_{i}=R^{(n)}_{i}(w)
,
1\leq i\leq(n+1)/2
, are calculated as

\displaystyle R^{(1)}_{1}

\displaystyle=w-3,\qquad

\displaystyle R^{(2)}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ST}^{(4)}_{6},\qquad

\displaystyle R^{(6)}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ST}^{(8)}_{47},\qquad

\displaystyle R^{(6)}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ST}^{(8)}_{21},

\displaystyle R^{(6)}_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ST}^{(8)}_{16},\qquad

\displaystyle R^{(7)}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ST}^{(8)}_{46},\qquad

\displaystyle R^{(7)}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ST}^{(8)}_{19},\qquad

\displaystyle R^{(7)}_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ST}^{(8)}_{14}.

As for
n=11
,
R^{(11)}_{1}=w^{6}-2w^{5}-6w^{4}+9w^{3}+9w^{2}-8w-2
, which is a Salem trace polynomial, and

R^{(11)}_{2}=\mathrm{ST}^{(12)}_{95},\quad R^{(11)}_{3}=\mathrm{ST}^{(6)}_{6}\cdot\mathrm{CT}_{3}\cdot\mathrm{CT}_{10},\quad R^{(11)}_{4}=\mathrm{ST}^{(4)}_{1}\cdot\mathrm{CT}_{4}\cdot\mathrm{CT}_{9},\quad R^{(11)}_{5}=\mathrm{ST}^{(8)}_{11}\cdot\mathrm{CT}_{8}.

Theorem 9.7

For any entry of Table
8.1
the exceptional component of type
\mathrm{A}

with trivial symmetry falls into one of the cases
(\mathrm{R}1),(\mathrm{R}2),(\mathrm{Hy})
.

\mathrm{RH}
-column in the table indicates which of the three occurs for
each individual entry.
It tells us that all of the three cases actually occur; No.
28
is the only entry
lying in case
(\mathrm{R}1)
, see also Remark
9.8
.
In Nos.
7
–
11
a rotation domain of rank
2
and a rotation domain of rank
1

coexist, where the former domain contains an exceptional component of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}
,
while the latter contains one of type
\mathrm{E}_{8}
.

Proof
.
For every automorphism
f
in Table
8.1
, since
\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=0
, FPF (
67
) becomes

\hat{\tau}=\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f)
.
We apply Theorem
7.3
to the current situation,
where case
(\mathrm{C}3)
in the theorem is already ruled out by Proposition
8.3
.
Case
(\mathrm{C}2)
can also be ruled out in the following manner.
First we consider Nos.
6
–
11
of Table
8.1
, which have dynamical degree
\lambda^{(12)}_{1}
and
exceptional set of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}\oplus\mathrm{E}_{8}
.
If
(\mathrm{C}2)
is the case, then it follows from formula (
71
) with
n=1
and a formula for
\mathrm{E}_{8}

in Lemma
7.12
that the FPF
\hat{\tau}=\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f)
reads

\hat{\tau}=\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})+\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{E}_{8})=\dfrac{\hat{\tau}}{\tau-2}+\dfrac{1}{\tau-2}\left(\dfrac{\tau^{2}+\tau-1}{\hat{\tau}(\tau+1)}-\dfrac{K_{3}(\hat{\tau})}{K_{4}(\hat{\tau})}\right),

which leads to an algebraic equation
R(\tau):=\tau^{5}-9\tau^{3}-9\tau^{2}+5\tau+5=0
,
where
R(w)
is a Salem polynomial of degree
5
.
Since
R(w)
is different from
\mathrm{ST}^{(12)}_{1}(w)
,
we have
R(\tau)\neq 0
and hence case
(\mathrm{C}2)
cannot occur.
Secondly we consider No.
27
, which has dynamical degree
\lambda^{(8)}_{6}
and
exceptional set of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}\oplus\mathrm{A}_{6}^{\mathrm{r}}\oplus\mathrm{E}_{6}^{\mathrm{r}}
.
If
(\mathrm{C}2)
is the case, then by some formulas in Proposition
7.11
and Lemma
7.12

the FPF
\hat{\tau}=\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f)
reads

\hat{\tau}=\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})+\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{A}_{6}^{\mathrm{r}})+\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{E}_{6}^{\mathrm{r}})=\dfrac{\hat{\tau}}{\tau-2}+\dfrac{1}{\hat{\tau}}+\dfrac{\tau+1}{\hat{\tau}\cdot\tau},

which leads to an algebraic equation
R(\tau):=\tau^{3}-3\tau^{2}-3\tau+2=0
,
where
R(w)
is a Salem polynomial of degree
3
.
Since
R(w)
is different from
\mathrm{ST}^{(8)}_{6}(w)
, we have
R(\tau)\neq 0
and hence case
(\mathrm{C}2)
cannot occur.
Thirdly, for all entries of Table
8.1
other than Nos.
6
–
11
and No.
27
,
with explicit formulas for
R^{(n)}_{i}(w)
in Example
9.6
, similar reasonings to those
mentioned above show that
(\mathrm{C}2)
is not the case at all in Theorem
7.3
.

Now assertion
(\mathrm{C}1)
of Theorem
7.3
applies to every
\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}}
-component in Table
8.1
.
Let
\delta^{1/2}\alpha^{\pm 1}
be its multipliers.
In Nos.
6
–
11
, formula (
70
) and a formula for
\mathrm{E}_{8}
in
Lemma
7.12
imply that FPF
\hat{\tau}=\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f)
becomes

\hat{\tau}=\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})+\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{E}_{8})=\dfrac{\hat{\tau}}{\tau-(\alpha^{2}+\alpha^{-2})}+\dfrac{1}{\tau-2}\left(\dfrac{\tau^{2}+\tau-1}{\hat{\tau}(\tau+1)}-\dfrac{K_{3}(\hat{\tau})}{K_{4}(\hat{\tau})}\right).

This equation is settled as
\alpha^{2}+\alpha^{-2}=\hat{P}(\hat{\tau})
,
where
\hat{P}(w)=P(H_{2}(w))
with
P(w)
given by

P(w)=\dfrac{w^{6}-7w^{4}-w^{3}+13w^{2}+3w-4}{w^{5}+w^{4}-5w^{3}-5w^{2}+4w+3}.

In No.
27
, formula (
70
) and formulas for
\mathrm{A}_{6}^{\mathrm{r}}
and
\mathrm{E}_{6}^{\mathrm{r}}
in §
7.3
imply that
FPF
\hat{\tau}=\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f)
becomes

\hat{\tau}=\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})+\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{A}_{6}^{\mathrm{r}})+\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{E}_{6}^{\mathrm{r}})=\dfrac{\hat{\tau}}{\tau-(\alpha^{2}+\alpha^{-2})}+\dfrac{1}{\hat{\tau}}+\dfrac{\tau+1}{\hat{\tau}\cdot\tau}.

This equation is settled as
\alpha^{2}+\alpha^{-2}=\hat{P}(\hat{\tau})
,
where
\hat{P}(w)=P(H_{2}(w))
with
P(w)
given by

P(w)=\dfrac{w(w^{2}-w-3)}{(w-1)(w+1)}.

For each entry other than Nos.
6
–
11
and No.
27
, formula
(
81
) is available with the associated polynomial
P(w)=P_{n}(w)
in Example
9.6
,
depending on the value
n=1,2,6,7,11
of the
\mathrm{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{t}}
-component.

; the second column exhibits

.

Table 9.1:
*

As in definition (
45
) of §
6
we set
Q(w):=P^{2}(w)-2
if
n+1
is even,
and
Q(w):=(w+2)^{-1}P^{2}(w)-2
if
n+1
is odd.
Putting necessary information from Table
8.1
into the function
Q(w)
,
we have the results in Table
9.1
.
Here AI-column concerns the question of whether
Q(\tau)
, with
\tau
being the special trace of
f
,
is an algebraic integer (AI for short) or not; the answer is yes (Y) for every entry.
The
\lambda
-column gives the dynamical degree of
f
, which is a Salem number
\lambda^{(d)}_{i}
.
Let
\tau_{0},\dots,\tau_{d/2-1}
be the roots of the Salem trace polynomial
\mathrm{ST}^{(d)}_{i}(w)
arranged as

\tau_{0}>2>\tau_{1}>\cdots>\tau_{d/2-1}>-2
.
The
\tau_{j}
-column gives yes (Y) or no (N) answer as to whether
Q(\tau_{j})\in[-2,\,2]
or not, i.e.
Q(\tau_{j})>2
.
Then the results in MID-column follow from Theorem
6.3
and Corollary
6.5
,
where MI (resp. MD) means that
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{n+1}=(\alpha_{1}^{n+1},\,\alpha_{2}^{n+1})
and hence

\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(\delta^{1/2}\alpha,\,\delta^{1/2}\alpha^{-1})
itself is MI (resp. MD).
In particular, No.
28
is the only entry that falls in the MD case, for which

Q(w)=(w+2)^{-1}(w^{2}-3)^{2}-2,\qquad\alpha^{6}+\alpha^{-6}=Q(\tau)=-1\quad\mbox{for every root $\tau$ of $\mathrm{ST}^{(8)}_{8}(w)$},

and hence
\alpha
is an
18
-th root of unity.
Now the results in RH-column of Table
8.1
follow from Theorem
2.4
, Proposition
5.3
,
Lemma
9.2
, Table
9.1
and ST-column in Table
8.1
.

An inspection of Table
8.1
shows that in Nos.
7
–
11
the
\mathrm{A}_{1}
-component is containd in
a rotation domain of rank
2
.
For these entries the
\mathrm{E}_{8}
-component is contained in a rotation domain of rank
1
by Theorem
9.3
.
Hence the coexistence assertion in the theorem follows.
\Box

Remark 9.8

The automorphism
f
in No.
28
of Table
8.1
has dynamical degree

\lambda^{(8)}_{8}=(\lambda^{(8)}_{1})^{2}
and exceptional set
E
of type
\mathrm{A}_{2}
with trivial symmetry.
We know from Theorem
9.7
that
(f,E)
lies in case
(\mathrm{R}1)
.
We show that it is
genuinely
of case
(\mathrm{R}1)
in the sense of Remark
1.3
.
Otherwise,
f
is the square of an automorphism
g
of dynamical degree
\lambda^{(8)}_{1}

having exceptional set
E
with reflectional symmetry.
We find from Table
8.1
that
f^{*}|_{H^{2}(X)}
has characteristic polynomial

\tilde{\varphi}(z)=\mathrm{S}^{(8)}_{8}(z)\cdot\mathrm{C}_{1}^{2}(z)\cdot\mathrm{C}_{42}(z)
, for which

\tilde{\varphi}(z^{2})=\mathrm{S}^{(8)}_{1}(z)\cdot\mathrm{S}^{(8)}_{1}(-z)\cdot\mathrm{C}_{1}^{2}(z)\cdot\mathrm{C}_{2}^{2}(z)\cdot\mathrm{C}_{84}(z)
.
If
f=g^{2}
then
\tilde{\varphi}(z^{2})
is divisible by the characteristic polynomial of
g^{*}|_{H^{2}(X)}
,
which takes the form
S^{(8)}_{1}(z)\cdot C(z)
, where
C(z)
is a product of cyclotomic polynomials with
\deg C(z)=14
.
Then
C(z)
must divide
\mathrm{C}_{1}^{2}(z)\cdot\mathrm{C}_{2}^{2}(z)\cdot\mathrm{C}_{84}(z)
, but this is impossible because

\mathrm{C}_{84}(z)
is an irreducible polynomial of degree
24
.

Next we illustrate the coexistence of rotation domains of ranks
1
and
2

by using Tables
8.4
and
8.5
.

Theorem 9.9

For each entry of Tables
8.4
and
8.5
, every exceptional
component of type
\mathrm{D}
or
\mathrm{E}
is contained in a rotation domain of rank
1
.
Moreover, the unique fixed point off the exceptional set
\mathcal{E}(X)
is either a center
of a rotation domain of rank
2
or a hyperbolic fixed point, as indicated by
\mathrm{R}2

or
\mathrm{Hy}
in
\mathrm{RH}
-column of the tables.
In the former case a rotation domain of rank
1
and a rotation domain of rank
2

coexist in
X
.

Proof
.
The first part of the theorem is a consequence of Example
9.4
.
For the second part we recall from Proposition
8.5
that for each entry of Table
8.4

the map
f:X\to X
has a unique fixed point
p
off
\mathcal{E}(X)
, which is simple.
If its multipliers are
\delta^{1/2}\alpha^{\pm 1}
, then

\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=\hat{\nu}(f,p)=\{\hat{\tau}-(\alpha+\alpha^{-1})\}^{-1}

and FPF (
67
) gives

\hat{\tau}=\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f)+\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f)+\dfrac{1}{\hat{\tau}-(\alpha+\alpha^{-1})}.

(83)

The Dynkin types of the
f
-invariant exceptional components (not of the entire exceptional set
\mathcal{E}(X)
)
can be read off from Proposition
8.5
and are shown in Dynkin column of Table
9.2
.
This information together with Lemma
7.12
enables us to calculate
\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f)
explicitly.
Then equation (
83
) is settled as
\alpha+\alpha^{-1}=\hat{P}(\hat{\tau})=P(\tau)/\hat{\tau}

for some rational function
P(w)\in\mathbb{Q}(w)
; cf. formulas (
42
) and (
44
) in §
6
.
Here the explicit formulas for
P(w)
are given in Table
9.2
.
We put
Q(w):=(w+2)^{-1}P^{2}(w)-2
as in definition (
45
).
With this function we can make a table similar to Table
9.1
, which together with Corollary
6.5

tells us that the multiplier pair
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
is algebraic and MI for every entry in Table
8.4
.
Thus, by Theorem
2.1
and
2.2
, in a neighborhood of
p
the map
f
is biholomorphically conjugate to
the linear model (
80
) with
(c_{1},c_{2})=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
.
Now the results in RH-column of Table
8.4
follow from
Lemma
9.1
; we have
\mathrm{R}2
if
Q(\tau_{j})\in(-2,\,2)

and
\mathrm{Hy}
if
Q(\tau_{j})>2
, when the special trace
\tau
of
f
is
\tau_{j}
.
The automorphism in Table
8.5
can be treated in a similar manner.

\Box

Table 9.2:
*

10
Periodic Cycles

Under conditions (A1) and (A2) in §
1
, the K3 surface
X
contains
only a finite number of exceptional components.
Moreover, any automorphism of a Dynkin diagram of type
\mathrm{A}
,
\mathrm{D}
or
\mathrm{E}

has order
1
,
2
or
3
(not respectively).
So there exists the smallest number
m_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any exceptional
component
E\subset\mathcal{E}(X)
the map
f^{m_{0}}
preserves
E
and induces
the trivial Dynkin automorphism
\sigma(f^{m_{0}},E)=1
.
Once we know how
f
acts on
\mathcal{E}(X)
, it is relatively easy to detect the way in which
all iterates
f^{m}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, act on
\mathcal{E}(X)
.
The pattern of these actions is periodic in
m
with period
m_{0}
.
In particular, in a generic situation, it is not hard to see how the numbers
\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f^{m})

and
\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f^{m})
vary as
m
moves (Remark
7.13
),
where
\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)
and
\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f)
are defined in (
63
) and (
66b
).

A
periodic cycle
of
primitive
period
m
is an
f
-orbit
C=\{p,f(p),\dots,f^{m-1}(p)\}

consisting of distinct
m
points such that
f^{m}(p)=p
.
Since
\mathcal{E}(X)
is
f
-invariant, we have either
C\subset\mathcal{E}(X)
or
C\subset X\setminus\mathcal{E}(X)
,
that is to say,
C
is either
on
or
off

\mathcal{E}(X)
.
By definition, the
multiplicity
of
C
is the local index
\mu(f^{m},p)
at a representative point
p\in C

and the
multipliers
of
C
are those of
f^{m}
at the point
p
.
They are well defined, because they do not depend on the choice of
p
.
We say that
C
is
simple
if its multiplicity is
1
, or equivalently if neither of its multipliers is
1
.
We say that
C
is non-resonant (NR) if its multipliers are NR.

Given any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we consider the following two situations and introduce a condition
(\mathrm{PC})_{k}

as follows.

(k)_{0}

There is
no
periodic cycle of primitive period
k
off
\mathcal{E}(X)
.

(k)_{1}

There is
exactly one
periodic cycle
C_{k}
of primitive period
k
off
\mathcal{E}(X)
.
In addition
C_{k}
is
simple
.

Condition

(\mathrm{PC})_{k}

Either
(k)_{0}
or
(k)_{1}
should occur and in the latter case
C_{k}
should be NR.

Let
\{\bar{\mu}(k)\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be the unique sequence of integers defined recursively by

\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f^{k})=\sum_{j\,|\,k}\bar{\mu}(j),\qquad\mbox{or directly by}\qquad\bar{\mu}(k)=\sum_{j\,|\,k}\operatorname{M\mbox{\"{o}}b}(k/j)\cdot\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f^{j}),

(84)

where the sums extend over all (positive) divisors
j
of
k
and
\operatorname{M\mbox{\"{o}}b}
is the Möbius
function in elementary arithmetic.
We say that a divisor
j
of
k
is
proper
if
j<k
, in which case
we write
j\,|\!|\,k
.
In particular
1
has no proper divisor.
The multipliers of
C_{k}
can and shall be expressed as
\delta^{k/2}\alpha_{k}^{\pm 1}

for some number
\alpha_{k}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

Lemma 10.1

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
and suppose that conditions
(\mathrm{PC})_{k}
hold for all proper divisors
k
of
m
.
Then,

(\mathrm{1})

for each proper divisor
k
of
m
we have either
\bar{\mu}(k)=0
or
\bar{\mu}(k)=k
,

(\mathrm{2})

for
\varepsilon=0,1
, the case
(m)_{\varepsilon}
occurs if and only if
\bar{\mu}(m)=\varepsilon m
.

If either case
(m)_{0}
or
(m)_{1}
occurs, then FPF (
67
) for the map
f^{m}
takes the form

H_{m}(\hat{\tau})=\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f^{m})+\sum_{k\,|\!|\,m}\dfrac{\bar{\mu}(k)}{H_{m}(\hat{\tau})-H_{m/k}(\alpha_{k}+\alpha_{k}^{-1})}+\dfrac{\bar{\mu}(m)}{H_{m}(\hat{\tau})-(\alpha_{m}+\alpha_{m}^{-1})}.

(85)

Proof
.
For each
j\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathcal{P}_{j}
be the set of all
f
-periodic points of primitive period
j
off
\mathcal{E}(X)
.
For each
(k,j)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
with
j\,|\,k
, let
\bar{\mu}(k,j)
denote the sum of local indices

\mu(f^{k},p)
over all points
p\in\mathcal{P}_{j}
.
Then we have

\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f^{k})=\sum_{j\,|\!|\,k}\bar{\mu}(k,j)+\bar{\mu}(k,k)\qquad\mbox{for every}\quad k\in\mathbb{N}.

(86)

Claim 1
. For any
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon=0,1
, the case
(j)_{\varepsilon}
occurs if and only if
\bar{\mu}(j,j)=\varepsilon j
.

Notice that
\bar{\mu}(j,j)
is
j
times the cardinality, counted with multiplicity, of
all periodic cycles of primitive period
j
off
\mathcal{E}(X)
.
Claim 1 is clear from this characterization of the number
\bar{\mu}(j,j)
and the definition of
(j)_{\varepsilon}
.

Claim 2
. For every divisor
k
of
m
we have
\bar{\mu}(k)=\bar{\mu}(k,k)
.

Let
k\,|\,m
and
j\,|\!|\,k
so that
j\,|\!|\,m
.
From
(\mathrm{PC})_{j}
and Claim 1 we have either
\bar{\mu}(j,j)=0
or
\bar{\mu}(j,j)=j
.
In the former case
\mathcal{P}_{j}
is empty, so
\bar{\mu}(k,j)=0=\bar{\mu}(j,j)
.
In the latter case
\mathcal{P}_{j}=C_{j}
and, as
C_{j}
is NR by assumption
(\mathrm{PC})_{j}
, neither of the
multipliers of
C_{j}
is a root of unity.
Thus
\mu(f^{k},p)=\mu((f^{j})^{k/j},p)=1
for all
p\in C_{j}
, and so
\bar{\mu}(k,j)=j=\bar{\mu}(j,j)
.
In either case we have
\bar{\mu}(k,j)=\bar{\mu}(j,j)
for every
j\,|\!|\,k
.
Thus equation (
86
) becomes

\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f^{k})=\sum_{j\,|\!|\,k}\bar{\mu}(j,j)+\bar{\mu}(k,k)=\sum_{j\,|\,k}\bar{\mu}(j,j)\qquad\mbox{for every $k\,|\,m$}.

This together with definition (
84
) shows that the two sequences
\{\bar{\mu}(k)\}_{k|m}
and

\{\bar{\mu}(k,k)\}_{k|m}
satisfy the same recurrence relation, so they must be the same sequence.
This proves Claim 2.

Assertions (1) and (2) readily follow from Claims
1
and
2
.
Suppose that
(m)_{\varepsilon}
is the case.
Then FPF (
67
) for the map
f^{m}
reads
H_{m}(\hat{\tau})=\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f^{m})+\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{off}}(f^{m})
,
where
\hat{\mu}_{\mathrm{off}}(f^{m})
is given by the middle and last terms in the right-hand side of (
85
),
which are the contributions of the periodic cycles
C_{k}
for
k\,|\,m
with
\bar{\mu}(k)=k
.
Here the denominators
H_{m}(\hat{\tau})-H_{m/k}(\alpha_{k}+\alpha_{k}^{-1})
are nonzero,
since
C_{k}
is NR for
k\,|\!|\,m
and simple for
k=m
.

\Box

Induction 10.2

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Suppose that for all proper divisors
k
of
m
the conditions
(\mathrm{PC})_{k}
are satisfied
and the numbers
\alpha_{k}+\alpha_{k}^{-1}
with
\bar{\mu}(k)=k
are known.
With this hypothesis, we try to deduce condition
(\mathrm{PC})_{m}
and find the value
of
\alpha_{m}+\alpha_{m}^{-1}
in case
\bar{\mu}(m)=m
.
In view of Lemma
10.1
the first step is to confirm that

\mbox{$\bar{\mu}(k)=0$ or $\bar{\mu}(k)=k$ for every divisor $k$ of $m$}.

(87)

•

If
\bar{\mu}(m)=0
, we are done as Lemma
10.1
.(2) shows that we are in case
(m)_{0}
.
Confirmation of equation (
85
) supports the correctness of the preceding
calculations of
\alpha_{k}+\alpha_{k}^{-1}
for proper divisors
k
of
m
.

•

If
\bar{\mu}(m)=m
, we are in case
(m)_{1}
and led to
the next step of solving equation (
85
) in the form

\alpha_{m}+\alpha_{m}^{-1}=\hat{P}_{m}(\hat{\tau}),

where
\hat{P}_{m}(w)=P_{m}(H_{2}(w))
for even
m
and
\hat{P}_{m}(w)=w^{-1}P_{m}(H_{2}(w))
for odd
m

with some function
P_{m}(w)
as in formulas (
42
) and (
44
) in Hypothesis
6.1

and Remark
6.2
.
Theorem
6.3
is then used to examine whether the multipliers

\delta^{m/2}\alpha_{m}^{\pm 1}
of the periodic cycle
C_{m}
are NR.
If so, our trial is successful.

There is a recipe to carry out the test (
87
) in the context of
the method of hypergeometric groups.

Recipe 10.3

Let
f:X\to X
be a K3 surface automorphism arising from the modified
Hodge isometry
\widetilde{A}
for a hypergeometric group

H=\langle A,B\rangle
as described in §
8
.
Then our recipe proceeds as follows.

(\mathrm{1})

The Lefschetz numbers
L(f^{m})=2+\operatorname{Tr}((f^{m})^{*}|{H^{2}(X)})=2+\operatorname{Tr}(\widetilde{A}^{m})

for
m\in\mathbb{N}
are given by

L(f^{m})=2+\mbox{the coefficient of $z^{m}$ in the Maclaurin expansion of
$-z\dfrac{d}{dz}\log\widetilde{\varphi}(z)$},

(88)

where
\widetilde{\varphi}(z)=S(z)\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}(z)
is the characteristic
polynomial of
\widetilde{A}
; see
[
6
, formula (41)]
.

(\mathrm{2})

Calculate the numbers
\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f^{m})
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, following the recipe
in Remark
7.13
, where
\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)
is defined by formula (
63
) in §
7
.
When an exceptional component of type
\mathrm{A}
with trivial symmetry is involved,
this step is rather intricate but manageable, once case
(\mathrm{C}3)
is ruled out in Theorem
7.3
.

(\mathrm{3})

FPF (
64
) for the
m
-th iterate
f^{m}
yields

\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f^{m})=L(f^{m})-\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f^{m})
.

(\mathrm{4})

Use formula (
84
) to produce the sequence
\{\bar{\mu}(k)\}_{k|m}
from

\{\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f^{k})\}_{k|m}
and check the condition (
87
).

Example 10.4

To illustrate how Recipre
10.3
works, we apply it to the automorphisms
f
in
Nos.
11
–
15
of Table
8.2
, which have exceptional set of type
\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{r}}
.
Step (1) of Recipe
10.3
with polynomial

\widetilde{\varphi}=\mathrm{S}^{(8)}_{1}\cdot\mathrm{C}_{1}\mathrm{C}_{2}\mathrm{C}_{28}

yields the second row of Table
10.1
.
In step (2) we have
\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f^{m})=2+(-1)^{m}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
which follow from formula (
75a
) in Proposition
7.11
.
Step (3) amounts to subtracting the third row from the second one in Table
10.1
.
Step (4) is just to pass from the fourth row to the fifth one via formula (
84
).
The last row shows that the test (
87
) is successful for all values of
m
up to

14
except for
m=13
.
Thus it is worth trying Induction
10.2
for those values of
m
.
It turns out that this inductive procedure is successful, leading to Theorem
10.6
below.

Theorem 10.6

Each automorphism
f:X\to X
in Nos.
11
–
15
of Table
8.2
has a
unique periodic cycle
C_{m}
of primitive period
m=1,2,3,5,7,9,10,11,12,14
off
\mathcal{E}(X)
,
which is NR.
Each points of
C_{m}
is a center of a (periodic) rotation domain of rank
2

(\mathrm{R}2)
or of rank
1

(\mathrm{R}1)
,
or a hyperbolic periodic point
(\mathrm{Hy})
, as indicated in Table
10.2
.
Moreover we know from Example
9.4
that
\mathcal{E}(X)
is contained in a rotation domain of rank
1
.

Proof
.
Since
f
has exceptional set of type
\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{r}}
, we have
\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f^{m})=\hat{\nu}(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{r},m})
in the notation of §
7.3
, where

\hat{\nu}(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{r},m})=\dfrac{1}{H_{m}(\hat{\tau})}\quad\mbox{if $m$ is odd};\qquad\hat{\nu}(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{r},m})=\dfrac{K_{2}(H_{m}(\hat{\tau}))}{H_{3m}(\hat{\tau})-2(-1)^{m/2}}\quad\mbox{if $m$ is even},

(89)

by formula (
75b
) with
n=2
in Proposition
7.11
.
Solving equation (
85
), with (
89
) in mind, for the unknown
\alpha_{m}+\alpha_{m}^{-1}

recursively along the divisors of
m
, we find that the functions
P_{m}(w)

in Induction
10.2
are given by

\displaystyle P_{1}(w)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{w(w+2)}{w+1},\hskip 85.35826ptP_{2}(w)=\dfrac{w^{4}-w^{3}-3w^{2}+w+1}{w(w^{2}-w-1)},

\displaystyle P_{3}(w)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{(w-1)(w+1)^{2}\,\mathrm{ST}^{(8)}_{1}(w)}{w(w^{2}-w-1)(w^{2}+w-1)}\quad\mbox{with}\quad\mathrm{ST}^{(8)}_{1}(w)=w^{4}-4w^{2}-w+1,

\displaystyle P_{5}(w)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{(w^{2}-w-1)(w^{9}+4w^{8}-18w^{6}-20w^{5}+5w^{4}+6w^{3}-12w^{2}-8w-1)}{w(w+1)(w^{3}-3w-1)(w^{3}+w^{2}-2w-1)},

and so on.
Formulas for
P_{m}(w)
,
m=7,9,10,11,12,14
, are too messy to be given here, so left to Appendix
B
.

Define the function
Q_{m}(w)
from
P_{m}(w)
as in definition (
45
) in Remark
6.2
and apply Corollary
6.5
to it.
For each
m=1,2,5,7,9,10,11,12,14
, we have
Q_{m}(\tau_{j})>2
for at least one index
j=1,2,3
,
so the multipliers
\delta^{m/2}\alpha_{m}^{\pm 1}
of
C_{m}
are MI, in particular NR, where the case

Q_{m}(\tau_{j})>2
leads to
\mathrm{Hy}
, while the case
Q_{m}(\tau_{j})\in(-2,\,2)
leads to
\mathrm{R}2
in Table
10.2
.
Things are different for
m=3
, because
P_{3}(\tau_{j})=0
and
Q_{3}(\tau_{j})=-2
for
j=0,1,2,3
, and hence

\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{3}^{-1}=0
, that is,
\alpha_{3}=\pm\mathrm{i}
.
We are in case (I) of Theorem
6.3
with multipliers
\pm\mathrm{i}\delta^{3/2}
being MD but NR.
Thus each point of
C_{3}
is a center of a periodic rotation domain of rank
1
.

\Box

with

.

Example 10.7

The method of hypergeometric groups à la
[
6
, Setup 3.2]
produces two automorphisms
f:X\to X

in Table
10.3
, which have dynamical degree
\lambda^{(8)}_{17}
and empty exceptional set.
Since
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=L(f)-\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=1-0=1
, the map
f
has a unique fixed point
p\in X
,
which is simple, and the function
Q(w)
in (
45
) is
Q(w)=(w^{2}-3)/(w+2)
.
We observe that
Q(\tau_{j})=2\cos(2\pi/5)
for
j=0,3
, and
Q(\tau_{j})=2\cos(6\pi/5)
for
j=1,2
,
where
\tau_{0}>\tau_{1}>\tau_{2}>\tau_{3}
are the roots of
\mathrm{ST}^{(8)}_{17}(w)=w^{4}+w^{3}-5w^{2}-7w-1
.
Thus we are in case (I) of Theorem
6.3
and the multipliers of
f
at
p
are algebraic, MD but NR.
In conclusion
f
admits a rotation domain of rank
1
(with center at
p
), while
X
contains no exceptional set.
These automorphisms were found by a computer search due to Y. Takada, after a disscussion
with the author about the possibility of such examples.

Appendix A
Study of Further Examples

For a further illustration of Induction
10.2
as well as for a supplement to Proposition
8.4
,
we study in detail the automorphisms
f:X\to X
in Nos.
1
and
2
of Table
8.2
,
which have dynamical degree
\lambda^{(18)}_{6}
and exceptional set of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}\oplus\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{r}}
.
They are interesting because they contain an
\mathrm{A}_{1}
-component (necessarily having trivial
symmetry), which is intricate to handle under iterations of
f
in the sense of Remarks
7.5
and
7.13
.

Theorem A.1

In both of Nos.
1
and
2
in Table
8.2
, the
\mathrm{A}_{1}
-component
is contained in a rotation domain of rank
2
, while the
\mathrm{A}_{2}
-component is
contained in a rotation domain of rank
1
.
For each
m=1,7,8,9,10,11
, there is exactly one periodic cycle
C_{m}

of primitive period
m
off
\mathcal{E}(X)
.
In No.
1
the periodic cycles
C_{1}
and
C_{10}
serve as centers of rotation domains of rank
2
,
while for each
m=7,8,9,11
,
C_{m}
is a hyperbolic periodic cycle.
In No.
2
the unique point in
C_{1}
is a center of a rotation domain of rank
2
,
while for each
m=7,8,9,10,11
,
C_{m}
is a hyperbolic periodic cycle.

The proof of Theorem
A.1
is completed after Lemma
A.4
is proved.

Lemma A.2

We have
\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})=2
, hence for the
\mathrm{A}_{1}
-component the case
(\mathrm{C}3)

does not occur in Theorem
7.3
.

Proof
.
It was shown in the proof of Proposition
8.4
that either

\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})=2
or
\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})=3
held.
We rule out the latter possibility.
Suppose the contrary that
\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})=3
is the case, so that the
\mathrm{A}_{1}
-component
falls into case (C3) of Theorem
7.3
with
n=1
.
Then this component contains two fixed points
p
and
q
with local indices
\mu(f,p)=2
and
\mu(f,q)=1
.
The multipliers of
f
at
p
are
\delta
along
\mathrm{A}_{1}
and
1
in a normal direction to
\mathrm{A}_{1}
, while
those at
q
are
\delta^{-1}
along
\mathrm{A}_{1}
and
\delta^{2}
in a normal direction to
\mathrm{A}_{1}
.
Notice that
p
is an exceptional fixed point of type
\mathrm{I\!I}
in the terminology of
[
6
, §6]
.
From
[
6
, Theorem 6.8]
we have for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mu(f^{m},p)=2,\qquad\nu(f^{m},p)=\dfrac{m-1+(m+1)\delta^{m}+\theta(1+\delta+\cdots+\delta^{m-1})}{m(1-\delta^{m})^{2}}

for some constant
\theta\in\mathbb{C}
independent of
m
.
A little manipulation of
\nu(f^{m},p)
yields

\hat{\nu}(f^{m},p):=\delta^{m/2}\nu(f^{m},p)=\dfrac{mH_{m}(\hat{\tau})+\hat{\theta}K_{m-1}(\hat{\tau})}{m\{H_{2m}(\hat{\tau})-2\}},\qquad\hat{\theta}:=\delta^{1/2}+\delta^{-1/2}(\theta-1).

Since
f^{m}
has multipliers
\delta^{(m/2)\pm(3m/2)}
at
q
, we have
\mu(f^{m},q)=1
and

\hat{\nu}(f^{m},q)=\dfrac{1}{H_{m}(\hat{\tau})-H_{3m}(\hat{\tau})}=-\frac{1}{H_{m}(\hat{\tau})\,\{H_{2m}(\hat{\tau})-2\}},

where
H_{3}(w)-w=w\{H_{2}(w)-2\}
is used.
Therefore
\mu(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{1})=\mu(f^{m},p)+\mu(f^{m},q)=2+1=3

and

\hat{\nu}(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{1})=\hat{\nu}(f^{m},p)+\hat{\nu}(f^{m},q)=\dfrac{1}{H_{2m}(\hat{\tau})-2}\left\{H_{m}(\hat{\tau})-\dfrac{1}{H_{m}(\hat{\tau})}+\dfrac{\hat{\theta}}{m}K_{m-1}(\hat{\tau})\right\},\quad m\in\mathbb{N}.

By formula (
75a
) in Proposition
7.11
we have
\mu(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{r},m})=2+(-1)^{m}

and so
\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f^{m})=\mu(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{1})+\mu(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{r},m})=3+\{2+(-1)^{m}\}=5+(-1)^{m}

for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Moreover,
\hat{\nu}(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{r},m})
is given by formulas (
89
).
All these formulas enable us to calculate
\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f^{m})=\hat{\nu}(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{1})+\hat{\nu}(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{r},m})
explicitly.
For
m=1
we have
L(f)=4=\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)
, hence
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=L(f)-\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f)=0
and
so
\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=0
.
FPF (
67
) now reads
\hat{\tau}=\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f)
.
Solving it for the unknown
\hat{\theta}
gives
\hat{\theta}=(\tau+1)(\tau-3)/\hat{\tau}
.
For
m=2
we have
L(f^{2})=6
from formula (
88
) with

\widetilde{\varphi}=\mathrm{S}^{(18)}_{6}\mathrm{C}_{1}^{2}\mathrm{C}_{2}^{2}
.
Since
\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f^{2})=5+(-1)^{2}=6
, we have
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f^{2})=L(f^{2})-\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f^{2})=6-6=0

and so
\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{off}}(f^{2})=0
.
FPF (
67
) applied to
f^{2}
then becomes
H_{2}(\hat{\tau})=\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f^{2})
.
Substituting
\hat{\theta}=(\tau+1)(\tau-3)/\hat{\tau}
into this equation, we obtain an
algebraic equation
2\tau^{4}+\tau^{3}-7\tau^{2}-\tau+1=0
.
But this is absurd, as
\tau
is a root of
\mathrm{ST}^{(16)}_{6}(w)
.
Hence
\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})=3
is impossible and we must have
\mu(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})=2
.

\Box

Lemma A.3

For the
\mathrm{A}_{1}
-component the case
(\mathrm{C}2)
also does not occur in Theorem
7.3
.

Proof
.
Formula (
88
) with
\widetilde{\varphi}=\mathrm{S}^{(18)}_{6}\mathrm{C}_{1}^{2}\mathrm{C}_{2}^{2}
evaluates

L(f^{m})
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, as in the second row of Table
A.1
.
Contrary to the assertion, suppose that
(\mathrm{C}2)
is the case.
Then by Remark
7.5
we have
\mu(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{1})=2
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Thus
\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f^{m})=\mu(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{1})+\mu(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{r},m})=2+\{2+(-1)^{m}\}=4+(-1)^{m}

as in the third row of Table
A.1
,
so the numbers
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f^{m})
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, are given as in the fourth row.
In particular, for each
m=1,\dots,6
, the unique
f^{m}
-fixed point
p_{1}
off
\mathcal{E}(X)
is simple.
Let
\delta^{1/2}\beta^{\pm 1}
be the multipliers of
f
at
p_{1}
and put
B:=\beta+\beta^{-1}
.
Then for
m=1,\dots,6
, we have
\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{off}}(f^{m})=\{H_{m}(\hat{\tau})-H_{m}(B)\}^{-1}

with denominator being nonzero.
Since
\hat{\nu}(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{1})=H_{m}(\hat{\tau})\,\{H_{2m}(\hat{\tau})-2\}^{-1}
by
formula (
71
) in Theorem
7.3
, FPF (
67
) becomes

H_{m}(\hat{\tau})=\dfrac{H_{m}(\hat{\tau})}{H_{2m}(\hat{\tau})-2}+\hat{\nu}(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{r},m})+\dfrac{1}{H_{m}(\hat{\tau})-H_{m}(B)},\qquad m=1,\dots,6,

where
\hat{\nu}(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{r},m})
is given by formula (
89
).
For
m=1
, solving this equation for the unknown
B
, we have

B=\hat{\tau}(\tau^{2}-3\tau-2)/(\tau^{2}-2\tau-4)
.
Substituting this into the equation for
m=2
, we find that
\tau
satisfies the algebraic equation

\tau^{5}-2\tau^{4}-8\tau^{3}+8\tau^{2}+11\tau-1=0
.
This contradicts the fact that
\tau
is a root of
\mathrm{ST}^{(18)}_{6}(w)
.
Therefore for the
\mathrm{A}_{1}
-component case (C2) does not occur in Theorem
7.3
.

\Box

It follows from Lemmas
A.2
and
A.3
that the
\mathrm{A}_{1}
-component falls
in case (C1) of Theorem
7.3
.
Let
\delta^{1/2}\alpha^{\pm 1}
be the multipliers of the
\mathrm{A}_{1}
-component.
Formula (
70
) with
n=1
yields

\hat{\nu}(f,\mathrm{A}_{1})=\hat{\tau}\,\{H_{2}(\hat{\tau})-(\alpha^{2}+\alpha^{-2})\}^{-1}
.
Since
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=1
, the map
f
has a unique fixed point
p_{1}
off
\mathcal{E}(X)
,
which is simple and whose multipliers can be expressed as
\delta^{1/2}\beta_{1}^{\pm 1}

for some
\beta_{1}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.
We shall evaluate
A:=\alpha^{2}+\alpha^{-2}
and
B_{1}:=\beta_{1}+\beta_{1}^{-1}
in terms of
\tau
.

Lemma A.4

In the situation and notation mentioned above we have

A:=\alpha^{2}+\alpha^{-2}=-(\tau^{2}-3)(\tau^{2}-\tau-1),\qquad B_{1}:=\beta_{1}+\beta_{1}^{-1}=\dfrac{\hat{\tau}(\tau-1)(\tau^{4}-4\tau^{2}+2)}{\tau^{5}-5\tau^{3}+6\tau+1}.

(90)

Moreover, the pairs
\delta^{1/2}\alpha^{\pm 1}
and
\delta^{1/2}\beta_{1}^{\pm 1}
are MI,
all data in Table
A.1
is correct,
\mu(f^{m},p_{1})=1
and

\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f^{m})=\dfrac{H_{m}(\hat{\tau})}{H_{2m}(\hat{\tau})-H_{m}(A)}+\hat{\nu}(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{r},m}),\qquad\hat{\nu}(f^{m},p_{1})=\dfrac{1}{H_{m}(\hat{\tau})-H_{m}(B_{1})},

(91)

for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\hat{\nu}(f^{m},\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{r},m})
is explicitly given
by formula (
89
).

Proof
.
Although Table
A.1
is not yet established,
we already know that
L(f^{m})
-row and the column of
m=1
are correct in Table
A.1
.
Since
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f)=1
, we have
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f^{2})\geq 1
.
Remark
7.10
and formula (
75a
) in Proposition
7.11
imply

\mu(f^{2},\mathrm{A}_{1})\geq 2
and
\mu(f^{2},\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{r},2})=3
, thus FPF (
64
) yields

6=L(f^{2})=\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f^{2})+\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f^{2})=\mu(f^{2},\mathrm{A}_{1})+\mu(f^{2},\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{r},2})+\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f^{2})\geq 2+3+1=6,

which forces
\mu(f^{2},\mathrm{A}_{1})=2
,
\mu_{\mathrm{on}}(f^{2})=5
and
\mu_{\mathrm{off}}(f^{2})=1
.
This implies that the column of
m=2
in Table
A.1
is correct.
A similar reasoning shows that the columns of
m=3,\dots,6
are also correct.
In particular, for
m=1,\dots,6
, we find that
p_{1}
is the only
f^{m}
-fixed point off
\mathcal{E}(X)
,
which is simple, and
\hat{\nu}(f^{m},p_{1})
is given by the second formula in (
91
).
Moreover, as in the proof of Lemmas
A.2
and
A.3
, for each
m=1,\dots,6
, we can show
that the
\mathrm{A}_{1}
-component falls in case (C1) of Theorem
7.3
relative to the map
f^{m}
.
Hence by formula (
70
) in Theorem
7.3
,
\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f^{m})
is
given by the first formula in (
91
).
FPF (
67
) applied to
f^{m}
now reads

H_{m}(\hat{\tau})=\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f^{m})+\hat{\nu}(f^{m},p_{1}),\qquad m=1,\dots,6.

(92)

Solving equation (
92
) with
m=1
for the unknown
B_{1}
, we have

B_{1}=\hat{\tau}\dfrac{(\tau+1)(\tau-2)-A\tau}{\tau^{2}-2-A(\tau+1)}.

(93)

Substituting formula (
93
) into equations (
92
) with
m=2,3
, we have
two algebraic equations for
A
,

\{A+(\tau^{2}-3)(\tau^{2}-\tau-1)\}\,R_{m}(\tau;A)=0,\qquad m=2,3,

where
R_{2}(\tau;A)
and
R_{3}(\tau;A)
are certain polynomials in
A
of degrees
1
and
3
,
respectively, with coefficients in the field
\mathbb{Q}(\tau)
.
We observe that these polynomials have no roots in common,
so we have
A=-(\tau^{2}-3)(\tau^{2}-\tau-1)
, the first formula in (
90
).
Substituting this into (
93
) yields the second formula in (
90
).

Let
\tau_{0}>\tau_{1}>\cdots>\tau_{8}
be the roots of
\mathrm{ST}^{(18)}_{6}(w)
.
Substituting
\tau=\tau_{j}
into formulas (
90
) gives the table

\tau_{j}

(94)

where the signs Y and N for

and

and the same rule applies to

.
By Corollary
6.5
the multipliers
\delta^{1/2}\alpha^{\pm 1}
of the
\mathrm{A}_{1}
-component
and those at
p_{1}
, i.e.
\delta^{1/2}\beta_{1}^{\pm 1}
are MI.
By Remark

the

-component stays in case (C1) of Theorem

relative to

for all

.
In particular we have

.

Proof of Theorem

A.1
.
From Table
8.2
the special trace
\tau
of
f
is either
\tau_{8}
or
\tau_{6}
,
for which
A
and
B_{1}
have Y in table (
94
).
So Lemmas
9.2
and
9.1
combined with Theorems
2.4
and
2.1

imply that the
\mathrm{A}_{1}
-component is contained in a rotation domain of rank
2
and the point
p_{1}
is a center of
a rotation domain of rank
2
.
On the other hand, the
\mathrm{A}_{2}
-component is contained in a rotation domain of rank
1

by Theorem
9.3
.

It is clear from Table
A.1
that for each
m=7,8,9,10,11
, the map
f

has a unique periodic cycle
C_{m}
of prmitive period
m
off
\mathcal{E}(X)
, which is simple.
Let
\delta^{m/2}\beta_{m}^{\pm 1}
be the multipliers of
C_{m}
.
Since
C_{1}=\{p_{1}\}
is the only periodic cycle off
\mathcal{E}(X)

whose primitive period is a proper divisor of
m
, FPF (
67
) takes the form

H_{m}(\hat{\tau})=\hat{\nu}_{\mathrm{on}}(f^{m})+\hat{\nu}(f^{m},p_{1})+\dfrac{m}{H_{m}(\hat{\tau})-(\beta_{m}+\beta_{m}^{-1})},\qquad m=7,8,9,10,11,

where the first and second terms in the right-hand side are given by formulas (
91
).
Solving this equation, we have an expression
\beta_{m}+\beta_{m}^{-1}=\hat{P}_{m}(\hat{\tau})
as in formula
(
42
) with (
44
), to which we can associate a rational function
Q_{m}(w)

as in definition (
45
).
Explicit formula for
Q_{m}(w)
is too long to be included here, but for each
m=7,8,9,10,11
,
it tells us that
Q_{m}(\tau_{6})>2
, so the multipliers
\delta^{m/2}\beta_{m}^{\pm 1}
are MI
by Corollary
6.5
.
For the automorphism
f
of No.
2
, which has special trace
\tau_{6}
, the periodic cycle
C_{m}
is hyperbolic
for every
m=7,8,9,10,11
.
On the other hand, we have
Q_{10}(\tau_{8})\in(-2,\,2)
and
Q_{m}(\tau_{8})>2
for
m=7,8,9,11
.
Thus for the automorphism
f
of No.
1
, which has special trace
\tau_{8}
, we have the result
stated in the theorem.

\Box
